MADE IN THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA

PREFACE 

THIS book is intended for boys ana girls in the upperelementary and junior high-school grades. It contains an account of the outstanding movements and developments in the history of the United States from the days of Christopher Columbus to those of President Franklin

D. Roosevelt. 

The volume is based on the belief that it is better educative procedure to follow, one by one, the main lines of progress that have marked the history of our country than to attempt to carry them all along at the same time. 

Instead of presenting a mass of miscellaneous and unrelated facts merely in the order of their happening, we have sought, therefore, to bring out in a unified way the great movements in our history, their causes, beginnings, and growth, and to make known the achievements and character of the great men and women who have made the United States what it is today.

Accordingly the story of the significant movements in American history is told with a proper recognition of the time order of events within such movements, but without causing a break in the narrative by introducing topics and facts which, although contemporary and important, are without logical connection with the subject under discussion. Thus each line of progress is presented by itself. When the story of each is fully told, the story of the whole is more effective and more easily understood. 

If the movements are clearly related or are interdependent, the relation is explained in the text; cross references also enable the pupils to grasp readily the historical connections.

The plan of selecting and organizing historical material explained above makes it unnecessary to burden the

PREFACE 

teacher and the pupil with the mention of everything that has happened in the four hundred years of our history. 

For teaching purposes at least many matters are best omitted. It is important rather to include only the details that are of moment both in themselves and as a means of clarifying the significant lines of development in our history as a nation. 

The Table of Contents shows what we conceive to be the great movements in American history from the discovery of the New World to the problems of the present day. 

These movements, when properly apprehended, reveal the history of the United States in its various aspectssocial, industrial, educational, political, governmental, inventive, including the story of the receding frontier, the pioneer conquest of the continent, and our advancing democracy. The story is one to inspire patriotism and lead to better citizenship.

We have sought to present the history of our country in a clear, readable style. No pains have been spared to bring every sentence and paragraph within the easy understanding of children in the upper-elementary and junior high-school grades. With this end in view the text was critically read and the vocabulary and sentence structure thoroughly revised by Miss Nelle E. Moore, Supervisor of Elementary Education, Wilmette Public 

Schools, Wilmette, Illinois. The procedure included (1) securing a list of probable difficulties through the reader's judgment and a check on the Thorndike 10,000 word list, (2) trying out the expressions likely to cause difficulty with upper-grade pupils of a public school, (3) recasting sentences to remove difficulties in vocabulary and sentence structure, (4) inserting explanations of historical terms which were necessary to the text, (5) including vocabulary self-checking exercises for pupils.

PREFACE 

ume. 

The Appendix should prove a helpful part of the vol- Here are found the texts of the Declaration of Independence and of the Constitution of the United States. These are cited frequently in the book and a study of them can be made with profit. The Appendix can also be used for reference and significant statistics. 

Here the pupil will find a chronological list of the principal facts and dates in our history; the list and terms of the Presidents, the political parties to which they belonged, and the States from which they came; the population of the States by the last census, the order in which the Original Thirteen ratified the Constitution, and the date when each subsequent State was admitted to the Union; the growth of our population by decades since 

1790, and the number of members of Congress. 

Careful attention has been given to the pedagogical features of the volume. Previews, giving the heart of the movement to be studied, will be found at the beginning of each of the ten Parts of the book. Class 

Activities, containing questions, problems, projects, and exercises of a wide variety, provide suggestive material to enable the teacher to meet the varying needs of the pupils. A Class Library is given on the pages following the Preface. The citations to these books, which are made at the end of every chapter in the volume, will be found helpful in stimulating wide reading. The illustrations, maps, and other graphic aids form an integral part of the volume; they have been selected to illuminate and interpret the text, not to adorn it. 

Special mention should be made of the exercises and tests that are provided under the title "Looking Backward" at the end of each of the ten Parts into which the volume falls. The activities there presented are intended to round out or summarize the movement that the pupils have just completed (1) by providing suitable review

PREFACE 

material, (2) by revealing the respects in which study may have been inadequate, and (3) by clarifying matters that may not have been understood. The systematic use of this material-a distinctive feature of the volumewill prove a valuable aid in guiding instruction. 

In bringing this preface to a close a word concerning the viewpoint of the authors is in order. We have faith in America. We would inculcate that faith in the rising generation. We believe there is reason for pride in 

America's past and we have bright hopes for her future.

To bring about that greater and better future requires that the children in the schools be taught how their liberties were obtained; how the Union of the States was formed; how our Constitution came to be; how the nation has grown, the changes it has undergone, the perils that have threatened it, and the courage and wisdom that have sustained it. These are the central themes of our story. We believe that when the children of America come to know their country's history they will become better citizens, more devoted to the principles of government and life for which their fathers struggled. They will then be more determined to strive for good will among nations, for obedience to law, and for the maintenance of the freedom and order which have been bequeathed to them.

: A REVIEW CHAPTER 

Our Ancestors in Europe. The history of the United 

States begins with the Old World. Our ancestors all came from Europe, except the Indians who were found here and the Negroes who were brought here as slaves from Africa. Long before America was known to the world, travelers from Europe had found their way to 

Asia. The Crusades had made the Near East known. to Europeans and had opened up a profitable trade in those regions. But after the Turks captured Constantinople (1453) and endangered the commerce with the East, European merchants were eager to find a new route to the Indies, as they called the faraway lands where spices, precious stones, and fine fabrics could be obtained.

Discoveries and Explorations. With this purpose in view, Vasco da Gama, a Portuguese, sailed around the Cape of Good Hope and found a new way to India. Six years later (1492) Christopher Columbus, an Italian, aided by the King and Queen of Spain, sailed to the West in order to find the East. He believed that the earth was round, not flat, and that if he sailed west far enough he would come to India or China. He did not reach the goal he sought, but instead discovered America, one of the most important events in world history. 

Columbus thought the earth was much smaller than it really is, and he died believing that he had reached the outlying islands of Asia. 337

Following Columbus came many bold sailors and explorers. John Cabot, sailing under English colors, touched the mainland of North America at Labrador (1497). Balboa, a Spaniard, crossed the Isthmus of 

Panama and discovered the great Pacific Ocean (1513).

Magellan, a Portuguese, sailed through the stormy straits that bear his name, and then struck out over the broad Pacific. In spite of difficulties he refused to turn back, and he and his men suffered great hardships. He was killed in the Philippines, but one of his ships, still going west, sailed on to Spain and completed the first voyage around the world (1519-1522).

Meanwhile the Spaniards had begun to explore the interior of North America. The soldier, Ponce de Leon, seeking the fountain of perpetual youth," discovered 

Florida (1513). Several years later another Spaniard,

De Soto, landed in Florida, marched overland, discovered the Mississippi, and was buried in its waters (1541). 

A short time before, the Frenchman, Cartier, discovered the mouth of the St. Lawrence (1534) and laid the foundations for the later French settlements in Canada.

The sixteenth century (1500-1600) was chiefly a time of discovery and exploration, although before its close the 

Spanish settled St. Augustine, Florida (1565), the first permanent European settlement in the United States. 

Planting Colonies. The more important European settlements in America began about forty years later. 

Sir Humphrey Gilbert and his half-brother, Sir Walter Raleigh, tried to plant colonies in the New World in the latter part of the sixteenth century (1579–1585), but Gilbert was lost at sea and the colonists Raleigh brought to Virginia disappeared and were lost to history. 

The English planted their first permanent colony in America at Jamestown, Virginia, in 1607. The colony

was saved from disaster and failure by the courage and leadership of the famous Captain John Smith whose life, it is said, was saved on one occasion by the Indian maiden Pocahontas after her father, the Chief Powhatan, had condemned Smith to death.

In 1620 the Pilgrims, religious exiles from England, after a brief residence in Holland, landed from the Mayflower at Plymouth and began the settlement of 

Massachusetts. Seeking religious freedom,

This band of exiles moored their bark On the wild New England shore. 

Ten years later a Puritan migration, a thousand strong, came over from England, founded Boston, and set up the Massachusetts Bay Colony. Many years later the colony at Plymouth united with the stronger 

Massachusetts colony. 

The early colonists in bleak New England, as in the warmer climate of Virginia, had to contend against the Indians and against famine and disease. Many of the first Virginia colonists were gold hunters and adventurers, who found it hard to settle down to make their living from the soil. Early in the history of Virginia (1619) slaves, bought from Dutch traders, were introduced in the colony. Here was a source of subsequent sorrow. 

In 1609 Henry Hudson, an Englishman sailing under the Dutch flag, discovered the river that bears his name. 

Five years later the Dutch settled Manhattan Island, in New York, which remained a Dutch colony until 1664 when it was seized by the English. 

Meanwhile the French had also begun to establish settlements in America. The famous Champlain sailed up the St. Lawrence in 1603, and a few years later explored the coast of New England. In 1608 he founded

Quebec, one of the oldest towns in America and the first permanent French colony in Canada. The next year 

Champlain discovered the lake which bears his name. 

As years passed by other great Frenchmen followed 

Champlain. French priests, Marquette and Hennepin, and a French trader named Joliet, passed through the Great Lakes and down the Wisconsin River, discovering the Upper Mississippi (1673). A few years later 

Hennepin and several companions went up the river as far as the Falls of St. Anthony (Minneapolis). About the same time La Salle, an able French commander, went to the mouth of the Mississippi, and there took possession of the vast Mississippi Valley" in the name of the King of France. As a result of the foregoing explorations, it seemed that the French might control both the Great Lakes and the valleys of the St. Lawrence and the Mississippi, and thus establish in America a vast power to be known as New France.

The Thirteen Original Colonies. While the French were establishing their claims in the interior, the English were planting colonies along the Atlantic coast. In 1634 

Lord Baltimore established the Catholic colony of 

Maryland. The next year Thomas Hooker led a band of colonists from Massachusetts and settled Connecticut. 

Roger Williams, being banished from Massachusetts for his opinions on religion and politics, settled Rhode 

Island, at Providence, in 1636. William Penn established the Quaker colony of Pennsylvania at Philadelphia in 1681. Georgia, the last of the thirteen to be settled, was founded by General Oglethorpe at Savannah in 1733, as a haven for released prisoners and for people who were poor and unfortunate. The other colonies, New 

Hampshire, New Jersey, Delaware, and the Carolinas, were either outgrowths of neighboring colonies or were settled in accordance with authority granted by the King.

Shortly after they were established, the colonies became partly self-governing, although they recognized the authority of the King and Parliament. They grew in numbers, at first slowly and then more rapidly. Their trade and industry also increased. For a time four of the New England colonies united in the New England Confederation (1643-1684) for their mutual protection against the Indians and the Dutch. 

The colonists lived a simple life. Most of the people lived in the country, although a few small cities developed, the most important being Philadelphia, Boston, New York, Baltimore, and Charleston. The population gradually moved westward. Their governments were modeled after that of England. Occasionally they had difficulties with the home government, but they were loyal to the mother country, and when England was at war with Spain or France, as happened five times within sixty years (1689-1748), the colonies did their share of the fighting. They felt the need of protection against both France and Spain.

The Struggle for America. Finally a great war occurred that settled the possession of America. As the 

English colonists on the seaboard expanded toward the West, they came into conflict with the French who were establishing forts and trading posts in the interior. The friction that resulted helped to bring about the French and Indian War (1754-1763), as the struggle was called in America. The conflict was really a world war, battles being fought in India, America, and Europe as well as on the seas. So far as America was concerned the struggle was to determine whether America was to become French or English. George Washington, a young

Virginian of twenty-two, first came into public notice. during this war. He fought on the side of the English, distinguishing himself at Braddock's defeat (1755).

Benjamin Franklin, one of the notable men of the time, urged the colonies to unite, pointing out that otherwise they might all be conquered by France and Spain. 

An attempt at union (the Albany Plan, 1754) failed, however, although the colonies stood together in support of Great Britain during the war. On the Plains of 

Abraham, near Quebec, was fought the decisive battle of the war (1759). This engagement was one of the important battles in the history of the world. Here Wolfe triumphed over Montcalm and the French had to retire from the continent of America.

In the treaty of peace ending the war France gave up to England all of Canada except two small islands off the coast of Newfoundland, and all of Louisiana east of the Mississippi (the eastern half of the Mississippi valley) except the island of New Orleans, which, together with that part of Louisiana west of the Mississippi, went to Spain. Spain ceded Florida to England, but England had to cede it back in 1783. Florida did not become a part of the United States until 1819. 

The Quarrel with the Mother Country. The English colonies in America were now relieved from fear of the French. The Spanish on the south were also not threatening. Expansion of the population toward the west became more rapid. The trade and manufactures of the colonists increased, and as their wealth grew the English Government began to show a keener interest in their activities. Heretofore the English Government had paid little attention to the colonies, allowing them to manage their own affairs largely as they pleased. 

Now that the colonies were growing richer and becoming more important, the home Government began to think of them as a source of revenue to help bear the expenses of the empire as well as to serve as a market for English goods.

Accordingly the English Government, then led by Lord Grenville, began to enforce the Navigation Acts, as the laws regulating commerce were called, and, more important still, proposed to tax America. The English ministry intended to use the money secured from the American taxes to meet government expenses in America and to help support British troops in the colonies. The 

British believed the troops were necessary to protect the colonies from the Indians and to safeguard America against a renewal of French attacks. The Americans, however, did not want the troops, declaring that they could take care of themselves. They wanted to build up their manufactures and have freer trade, and they especially objected to being taxed by a legislature (Parliament) in which they were not represented. They thought they should be taxed only by themselves, that is, by their own colonial assemblies. "No taxation without representation," was their cry.

The foregoing matters brought on a bitter quarrel between the colonies and the mother country, a quarrel which eventually ended in war and independence. When 

Parliament passed the Stamp Act (1765), Americans resisted the collection of the tax, sometimes in violent ways. The Stamp Act Congress, as it is called, composed of delegates from nine colonies met in New York (1765) and set forth the American grievances. Many 

Americans refused to buy English goods. 

Surprised by such opposition the British Government repealed the Stamp Act, but the next year levied taxes on several kinds of goods brought into America. It also interfered with the town meetings in New England, took away some of the chartered rights of the colonists, and sought to restrict the westward expansion of the people. 

More troops were sent to America to "make war on the colonies," as the Americans said.

To defend their rights the colonists took up arms against the British and in 1775 battles were fought in Massachusetts at Lexington, Concord, and Bunker Hill. 

Shortly afterwards the English hired German Hessians to fight the Americans. Early in 1776 Tom Paine wrote his pamphlet Common Sense, declaring that it was foolish. for the Americans to depend on England any longer. 

Finally the Continental Congress deemed it wise and necessary to adopt the Declaration of Independence (July 4, 1776), declaring that "these united colonies are and of right ought to be free and independent states."

The War for Independence. General George Wash ington led the American forces. For seven years the 

Americans resisted the attempts of the British to conquer them. The Continental army fought at Princeton and Trenton, at Monmouth and Brandywine, at Saratoga and Bennington, in the North; and at Savannah, Cowpens, King's Mountain, and Guilford Court House, in the South. They endured severe sufferings at Valley 

Forge. General George Rogers Clark led a Virginian expedition to the Northwest and after undergoing great hardships captured the region from the Indians and the British.

In 1777 the Americans forced General Burgoyne to surrender a British army at Saratoga, and the French were then willing to join in an alliance with America and make war on Great Britain. Thus powerful French aid was obtained for the American cause. Finally the 

British General Cornwallis surrendered his forces to Washington at Yorktown, and the war was over (October, 1781). Great Britain signed the final treaty of peace in 1783, recognizing American independence. 

The Articles of Confederation. The Americans fought the war without any regular form of union.

They were

held together by their fears, their common dangers, and their common interests. They followed the guidance of the Continental Congress, which represented all the colonies, but they had no regular constitution binding them into a union.

They realized, however, the importance of a union. 

The signers of the Declaration of Independence knew, as Franklin said at the time, that "they must all hang together or they would all hang separately." 

Accordingly, at the same time that the Continental Congress appointed a committee to draft a Declaration of Independence the Congress also appointed another committee to draw up a plan of union. 

The committee, after more than a year of effort, reported the Articles of Confederation, a plan that required the consent of every State before it could go into operation. New Jersey objected to the plan because it did not give Congress the power to regulate commerce and collect revenue. Maryland objected to the arrangement because the western lands were not given over to the control of Congress by the States that claimed the lands. Finally promises were made that the lands would be ceded to the United States and that the other defects would be remedied. As a result all the States ratified the Articles of Confederation, but not until the war was nearly over (1781).

The union which was formed by the Articles of Confederation was not a strong one. It provided for only a league of States, and did not establish a real central government. Power over taxation, commerce, and the raising of troops was left with the States. 

Congress was to consist of only one house whose members were to be elected by the States and who were to vote by States. There was no President to execute the laws and no Supreme Court to interpret them. Congress was

more like a diplomatic body than a legislature; it might pass laws, or resolutions, but it could not enforce them. 

It could not raise an army, nor collect money by taxes, nor regulate commerce among the States nor with foreign countries. Under the arrangement the States began to fall apart and to quarrel with one another. 66 66 The Constitution of 1787. Thoughtful men soon saw that a more perfect union" was necessary, in order to establish justice and provide for the common defense." Washington, Madison, Hamilton, and other leaders, induced Congress to call the Convention of 1787, which met in Philadelphia in May, and by September had drawn up our present Constitution. In 1788 this document was ratified and went into operation the following year.

The Constitution remedied the defects of the Articles of Confederation and established a real government which could enforce its own laws through its own courts. 

The citizens of the States were now citizens of the United States. From this fact a real nation was to grow. 

The Ordinance of 1787. In the same year that the new 

Constitution was formed, the Congress of the Old Confederation passed the Ordinance of 1787, a famous act which provided for free soil, free men, free schools, and free religion in the Old Northwest, which George Rogers Clark had conquered for the United States during the 

Revolutionary War. 

The New Government: Washington as President. The new Government under the Constitution was an experiment. Its success was uncertain. Washington, the first 

President, guided public affairs with wisdom and patriotism. He made Jefferson Secretary of State and 

Hamilton Secretary of the Treasury. When a war broke out between Great Britain and France, Washington declared that America should not enter the struggle.

Hamilton by his financial measures induced Congress to pay at full face value the debts of the Old Confederation which had been incurred in the Revolution, to assume the debts that the States had contracted during the war, to establish a United States Bank to aid the Government in its finances, and to levy taxes on certain alcoholic liquors. When the tax was resisted in the "Whisky Rebellion" in Pennsylvania (1794), the

Government showed itself able to enforce its laws and to collect the tax. Jefferson reluctantly helped Hamilton to carry out his financial plans in return for the location of the national capital on the banks of the Potomac. 

It was agreed that Washington, the "Federal City,' was to become the capital of the nation in 1800, and that Philadelphia was to serve as the seat of government until that year. 99 66 99 During Washington's administration political parties arose, the "Hamiltonian Federalists" and the "Jeffersonian Republicans.'

The Federalists sup ported Hamilton's financial measures; they desired a liberal construction of the Constitution, under “ implied powers," so as to give large powers to the National Government. The Republicans believed in "strict construction of the Constitution so as to limit the authority of the National Government and leave as many powers as possible to the States. Washington leaned toward the Federalists and approved Hamilton's measures. 99 Having served eight years, Washington refused a third term. In his Farewell Address, upon leaving office, he advised his countrymen to observe good faith toward all nations, to avoid wars and large standing armies, and to restrain sectional strife and an excess of party spirit. 

John Adams, a Federalist, succeeded Washington as President. His party passed the unwise Alien and

Sedition acts, which led Jefferson and Madison (Republicans) to write the Kentucky and Virginia Resolutions, denying such powers to Congress and declaring that when Congress passed acts that were unconstitutional the States might declare them null and void. People had not yet come to accept the idea that the Supreme Court was the body to exercise that power. Trouble with 

France occurred during Adams' administration, but Adams was wise enough and firm enough to preserve the peace.

Election of 1800: Jefferson Becomes President. In 

1800 Jefferson was elected President. He was the founder of the present Democratic Party, though his party was then called Republican. A tie-vote occurred in the Electoral College between Jefferson and Burr, and the House of Representatives after a long struggle elected Jefferson. Following this election the Twelfth 

Amendment was adopted, changing the method of voting in the Electoral College by requiring that each Elector designate whom he wished to choose for President and for Vice President. Before this amendment was made. the Electors had voted for two men without indicating the office each man was to occupy, the candidate receiving the larger number to become President. Consequently, when strict party voting developed in the Electoral College a tie would have taken place probably at every election had the amendment not been made. 

At his inauguration Jefferson set forth the great democratic principles by which he wished the country to be governed:

Equal and exact justice to all men. 

Peace, commerce, and honest friendship with all nations.

The support of the State governments as the surest bulwarks of republicanism, but also the preservation of the Union in its full vigor, for the sake of peace at home and safety abroad.

Free elections, with absolute submission to the decisions of the majority.

Economy in public expenditures, honest payment of public debts, and the supremacy of the civil over the military authority. 

These principles soon came to to be accepted by 

Americans of all parties.

The Annexation of Louisiana. Jefferson's purchase of 

Louisiana from Napoleon (1803) was one of the most important events in American history. The purchase gave the settlers in the West what they wanted — a port of deposit at New Orleans and access to the sea through the mouth of the Mississippi. The annexation also opened up the Far West to American expansion. 

Jefferson sent Lewis and Clark to explore the vast region that had been secured from France. 

Neutral Rights. During this period (1801–1812) 

France and Great Britain were almost continuously at war (the Napoleonic wars). Napoleon controlled the continent of Europe and sought to reduce Great Britain to his will. Both powers imposed upon American commerce, denying our ships the free use of the sea. 

Jefferson and Madison, who followed Jefferson as President, did all they could to preserve the peace. England did the greater harm to our commerce because she had more ships than France; she also impressed large numbers of our seamen into her navy. After long hesitation we finally declared war against Great Britain. 

The War of 1812. The War of 1812 has been called the "second war for independence." Were we to enjoy the rights and privileges of an independent nation, or should our seaman, ships, and commerce be controlled by some other power? On the sea our small navy gained fame and for a time held its own, but on the land we made little headway. We were not able to conquer 

Canada. The British armies invaded our soil, captured

Washington, and burned the Capitol. A peace treaty was signed at Ghent, in Belgium (December 24, 1814), which left matters much as they had been before the war, except that Napoleon had been defeated and that occasion no longer existed for Great Britain to impose restrictions upon our commerce.

Two weeks after the treaty of peace was signed, General Andrew Jackson, leading a small American army, gained a decisive victory over the British at New 

Orleans. That battle made Jackson a national hero and gave Americans the satisfaction of feeling that they were able to close the war" in the arms of victory." If the electric telegraph and the Atlantic cable had been in existence, the battle of New Orleans would not have been fought. 66 The Era of Good Feeling. Following the war came an era of good feeling in party politics. The 

Federalist Party, which had opposed the war, disappeared after the election of 1816. In that year Monroe was elected President, and in 1820 was re-elected almost unanimously, only one vote in the Electoral College being cast against him. National feeling developed in the country and Americans turned their attention in large measure from foreign affairs to domestic affairs. 

Internal improvements began for better roads and more canals. The Erie Canal was built, due chiefly to energy of Governor De Witt Clinton of New York 

(1817-1825), and the region around the Great Lakes was opened to settlement. Steamboats, which had been begun by Fitch and Fulton years before, were increased upon our rivers.

Manufactures had developed rapidly during the troubles with England and France, since English imports were cut off. To prevent the new factories from being closed by cheap imports, the first protective tariff was

enacted (1816). A Second United States Bank was established (1816) to help regulate the currency. 

Florida was purchased (1819), and to prevent European interference with the South American republics that were struggling for their independence, the Monroe Doctrine was announced (1823). On account of the severe financial panic of 1819 many people lost their jobs and their homes, and thousands of pioneer home hunters moved to the West.

Personal Politics: Election of 1824. Since the political parties had merged into one, a period of “ personal politics" arose. Personal leaders were supported by their followers for President - Jackson, Clay, John 

Quincy Adams, Crawford, Webster, Clinton, Calhoun.

In 1824 no candidate received a majority in the Electoral College, and accordingly the election fell to the House of 

Representatives. Here only the three highest candidates could be voted for, Jackson, Adams, and Crawford. 

Largely through Clay's influence Adams was elected. 

When a few months later Adams appointed Clay Secretary of State, Jackson's friends charged that" a corrupt bargain "had been made between Adams and Clay. The accusation was not true, but Jackson believed it and arrayed his supporters for the contest of 1828, when he defeated Adams for re-election. 

Democracy Triumphant. Democracy was strong on the frontier West. There everybody was looked upon as on the same level. The election of Jackson was hailed as a triumph for Western democracy. New parties arose, the "Jacksonian Democrats " (later the Democrats) led by Jackson and the "National Republicans (later the Whigs) led by Clay. Jackson introduced the "spoils system" into politics by making sweeping removals from office on party grounds on the principle that "to the victors belong the spoils." 99

Nullification. New tariff acts were passed, in 1824, 1828, and 1832. That of 1828 was called the "Tariff of 

Abominations." South Carolina, led by Calhoun, opposed the law as unfair to the South and claimed the right to declare it null and void and prevent the tariff taxes from being collected in that State. During the controversy a notable debate took place in the Senate between Webster and Hayne (1830), in which Webster upheld the national character of the Union and the Constitution. Jackson opposed Calhoun, vowing that "the American Union must be preserved." The controversy was settled by compromise, but the Union and the national spirit had been greatly strengthened. 

Congress passed an act to re-charter the Second United States Bank, but Jackson opposed the measure and defeated it by his veto. Jackson was the center of many political battles, but his principles and policies were everywhere triumphant. He was re-elected (over 

Clay) in 1832, and four years later dictated the choice of his successor, Van Buren.

In Van Buren's administration hard times developed, following a great financial panic (1837), and when

Van Buren came up for re-election in 1840, he was defeated in the great Whig campaign for " Tippecanoe and Tyler too." By this time the antislavery question had begun to agitate the country.

PART SIX THE STRUGGLE OVER SLAVERY

THE STRUGGLE OVER SLAVERY 

As the nation developed the seeds of a controversy grew which soon threatened its very life. 

In colonial days slaves had been held by Northerners and Southerners alike. Slave labor did not pay in the 

North, however, and the system soon died out. But in the South slaves were used in the cotton fields, and the planters came to believe that the system was essential for their welfare. This difference between the 

North and the South caused little trouble at first, for there was no thought of doing away with slavery in the States in which it existed. But after new territories were acquired, the advocates of slavery expected that the institution would be allowed there, while the anti-slavery people were determined to keep it out. 

To settle the controversy, a line was drawn through the Louisiana territory, the region to the north of the line to be free and that to the south to be open to slavery. Thirty years later another plan was tried, and the people in the territories were allowed to choose for themselves. But the question would not stay settled; in one aspect or another it came up again and again. as At last the Republican Party was formed with the purpose of keeping slavery out of the territories altogether. In 1860 this party elected Abraham Lincoln 

President. The South was greatly alarmed.

Believing that their most cherished rights were in danger, eleven States withdrew from the Union. The 

North, however, declared that no State could leave the Union; and a great civil war ensued. 

The superior resources of the North in man power and in money, factories, and railroads finally brought victory to the Federal Government, for the South was defeated. Since that time the United States has been united in fact as well as in name—“an indestructible union, composed of indestructible States."

DETROIT PUBLISHING CO. 

STATUE OF ABRAHAM LINCOLN IN LINCOLN PARK, CHICAGO

PART SIX THE STRUGGLE OVER SLAVERY CHAPTER XXI  

A Divided Country. Slavery existed in very early ages and among all peoples. We have seen how it was introduced into Virginia in the early days of the colony (see page 71). Gradually slavery spread into all the colonies, North as well as South. Few people, if any, thought it wrong

When people needed slaves for their work, they bought them if they could afford to do so. There was no apparent difference of opinion between the Puritan of New England and the Cavalier of Virginia on the right to hold slaves. However, by 1850 the right or wrong of slavery was the most hotly contested question between the North and the South. The people of New England had become convinced that slavery was wrong, while the people of the South (or most of them) held with equal honesty that it was right.

Why did this change of opinion come about? Largely because slavery did not pay in the North and because it paid in the South. Slaves could be used in field labor, but not so well in factory labor. Besides, "the long cold winters of New England produced such a long idle season, requiring so much food, clothing, and shelter, that slavery in the North cost more than it yielded and died a natural death in colonial days. 991 'Smith, J. Russell, North America, page 6. 355

Here we have a good illustration of the way men's interests govern their opinions. John Adams pointed this out in the Continental Congress when the members were discussing whether slaves should be counted in determining the number of representatives each State should have. He said, "Reason, equity, justice never had weight enough to govern in the councils of men; it is interest that does it." Henry Ward Beecher, while 

ELI WHITNEY'S COTTON GIN CONTRASTED WITH A MODERN MACHINE (see page 357) 

The modern cotton gin plucks the fiber from the seed by means of a combing process. The principle of the gin has never changed, although Whitney's has been greatly improved upon. speaking against slavery, once said: "When Uncle 

Tom sold for only $300, slavery was a positive evil to be gotten rid of as soon as possible. When

Tom had risen in value to $800, slavery was a necessary evil'; we would like to be rid of it, but it was impossible '; but, when Uncle Tom came to sell for $1500, slavery had become divine institution.""

It was a question of 'a the pocketbook. It was not so with all men, but with the common run of men. Many Southerners opposed slavery and set their slaves free at a sacrifice of their worldly interests. The early anti-slavery movement had its supporters in the South as well as in New England. In a short-sighted sense, it may be thought profitable for men to make other men work for them for their "board and keep," but in the long run and in a broader economic sense slavery did not pay anywhere. It was a wasteful system of labor and, of course, harmful to both the slave and his master. The South has prospered much more

without it. Yet the matter of slavery was determined largely by the supposed interest of the white man. 

When our independence was declared, there were slaves in all parts of the country. But here and there people in all sections spoke against slavery. John Wesley did so in Georgia; the Quakers and the

Scotch-Irish Presbyteri ans did so in Virginia and the Carolinas. By the time the Constitution was adopted, slavery was disappearing in the 

North. It was, for the most part, looked upon as an evil in both sections, and most people expected that it would gradually die out in the 

South as well as in the 

North. The slave system did not pay well in 

Maryland and Virginia, and Washington, Jefferson, Patrick Henry, George

Mason, and other Virginians spoke against slavery and expected to see it disappear. 

MODERN COTTON GIN 

In spite of this opposition, the slave interests were still strong in the South in 1787. By 1800 two clearly marked sections could be seen-a slave section and a free section. 

Would the time ever come when the country would be all free?

The Cotton Gin. The invention of the cotton gin alone seems to have prevented the early disappearance of slavery. This was an event of great importance. The fiber of the cotton clings to the seed. The purpose of the

gin is to make easier the separation of the two. Removing the fiber from the seed by hand was a slow process. 

Not more than four or five pounds of cotton could be cleaned by one man in a day. This added so greatly to the cost of cotton that only a small amount was used. 

Eli Whitney had gone to Georgia to teach school. He was a guest in the home of Mrs. Nathaniel Greene, the widow of the famous General Greene of the Revolutionary War. Whitney saw the difficulties under which cotton was prepared for use. He at once set himself to invent a machine by which the fiber could be cleaned more quickly. The result was the cotton gin, with which one man could clean a thousand pounds in a day and do the work better than when the task was done by hand. 

The first cotton gin was operated by hand, by means of a hand crank, but later horse power, then water power, and finally steam power were applied, and now cotton is ginned in the South with great rapidity. 

The principle of Whitney's machine was very simple. 

By turning a crank a series of saw-like teeth drew the fiber of the cotton through holes which were too small for the seed to pass through. In this way the seeds were pulled off and the cotton cleaned. (See page 356.)

One result of Whitney's invention was to make slavery more profitable. The raising of cotton was immensely increased and the way was opened for cotton to become the one great crop of the South. When John Jay made his famous treaty with England (1794) he did not seem to know that cotton was an article of export from the United States, so small was the crop at that time. But with the help of the cotton gin, America became a great cotton-producing country. Slave owners moved to the 

Southwest to raise cotton; the demand for slaves became greater; slaves rose in value; and gradually the industries of the South came to depend on slavery.

Slavery and the "Equilibrium." At the close of Washington's administration there were eight slave States and eight free States. The boundary line separating 

Pennsylvania (a free State) and Maryland (a slave 

State) was known as " Mason and Dixon's Line," receiving its name from the two English surveyors who had fixed the line in 1767. Historically, Mason and Dixon's

Line came to be known as the line that separated the North from the South, or the slave States from the free States.

North of Mason and Dixon's Line population increased more rapidly than south of it. The result was that the free section had more representatives in Congress and more votes in the Electoral College than did the slave section. In the Senate the States have equal power, each 

State having two votes. If the "equilibrium," or balance, were to be preserved, it could be done only by keeping the slave States equal in number to the free States. This balance of power in the Senate, the slave 

States believed, would prevent the passage of laws that might be harmful to slavery, and this idea or policy came to have great weight. 

Slavery and Missouri. Slavery existed in Louisiana when the United States bought that territory in 1803, and afterwards slaveholders from the Southern States settled in the new territory with their slaves. The State of Louisiana was admitted to the Union in 1812 with no dispute over slavery, but it was soon seen that if nothing were done, other slave States would spring up in that vast region.

Such was the case in 1819 when Missouri applied for admission as a slave State. People from the South had settled there with their slaves, and they now wished Arkansas to be set off as a new territory and Missouri to be made into a slave State. It was clear that if

Missouri and Arkansas were left alone, as Louisiana had been, they would choose to be slave States.

Northern men proposed that Missouri, before being admitted, should provide for gradually freeing her slaves, and in time become a free State. The Northern men declared that no more slave States should be made from the Louisiana purchase. They said that slavery was a blight to a new land, worse than poisonous weeds, or disease, and that such an evil ought to be restricted. 

The South stood up for the right of Missouri to come into the Union as she pleased. Southern men said that it was not against the evils of slavery that the North was contending, but for an unfair share of power in the National Government. They said it would be unconstitutional to require Missouri to abolish slavery; that the original States were allowed to decide for themselves whether they would have slavery or not, which was true, and that if Missouri were not allowed to decide for herself, she would be wronged, as she would not have equal rights with the other States. The Senate voted to admit 

Missouri as a slave State, but the House refused. This made a deadlock, that is, neither side could get its way and the question went over until the next meeting of Congress.

When Congress met again, in December, 1819, Maine applied for admission as a free State.¹ The House readily consented to admit Maine, but the Senate attached the Missouri bill (admitting Missouri as a slave State) as a "rider" to the Maine bill in the hope of forcing the friends of Maine to admit Missouri. Missouri was to ride into the Union on the back of Maine, or the two States were to stay out together. 

¹Ohio (free) was admitted in 1803, Louisiana (slave) in 1812, Indiana (free) in 1816, Mississippi (slave) in 1817, Illinois (free) in 1818, Alabama (slave) in 1819. Therefore the slave and free States had been keeping an even pace.

The Missouri Compromise. Both sides stood out firmly and the two houses were again in a deadlock. A compromise was necessary. A compromise is a plan for the settlement of a question by which both sides give up some of the points for which they are contending. A conference committee was appointed, consisting of members from each house which, after heated argument, agreed to the following compromise proposed by Senator 

Thomas of Illinois: 

1. That Maine should be admitted as a free State. 

2. That Missouri should be admitted as a slave State. 

3. That in all the rest of the Louisiana territory north of 36° 30' (which is the southern boundary of Missouri) slavery shall be and is hereby forever prohibited.’ 

This agreement was approved by both houses of Congress in 1820. Such was the famous Missouri Compromise between the North and the South. 66 The Importance of the Missouri Struggle. This was the first hard contest between the States over slavery. 

Bitterness had arisen; States had threatened to leave the Union. The struggle showed that in such a contest the South would stand by slavery, and that opposition to slavery might be expected from the North. A geographical line was now recognized dividing the North from the South. For the first time under the Constitution, Congress had exercised the power to prohibit slavery in the territories.

CHAPTER XXII  Lundy and the Beginnings of Abolitionism. The excite ment over the Missouri struggle soon died away and for ten years little attention was given to the subject of slavery. When Jackson became President in 1829, therefore, slavery was stronger than ever. It seemed that the anti-slavery spirit of Washington's time was dying out. In the South a new generation had arisen, composed of men who were eager to develop rich cotton lands and who were not much moved by the rights of men as Jefferson and Patrick Henry had been in the days of the Revolution. Slavery had come to be looked upon as a fixed system in Southern life, and as a result many anti-slavery men and women in the South moved to the 

Northwest. 

Following 1820 only a few voices here and there were raised against the slave system. Benjamin Lundy, a

New Jersey Quaker, traveled through the country on horseback and on foot, trying to make people see the wrong in slavery. He founded a little journal with a big name, The Genius of Universal Emancipation, which he distributed on his travels. For a time he settled in 

Baltimore, continuing to publish his paper. Here among his exchanges he noticed the Journal of the Times, published in Bennington, Vermont. Interested by the antislavery sentiments in the paper, Lundy went to Boston 365

and from Boston to Bennington, a distance of 125 miles, which he traveled on foot, his knapsack on his back, amid the snows of winter. There he met the young editor, William Lloyd Garrison. "Two bright stars in the moral firmament " had come together.

By his zeal and devotion Lundy persuaded Garrison to go to Baltimore to help on the Genius. Soon after 

WILLIAM LLOYD GARRISON 

1805-1879 coming to Baltimore Garrison was jailed for an antislavery article which he published. After his release he went to Boston and there, on 

January 1, 1831, he established the Liberator. From that time on there was no more peace on the subject of slavery.

Garrison and Abolitionism. 

Garrison soon became the most noted of the extreme anti-slavery men known as abolitionists. He was a bold agitator who would not yield. In his Liberator he announced: "I will be as harsh as truth, as uncompromising as justice. I am in earnest, I will not equivocate, I will not retreat a single inch, and I will be heard."

Garrison demanded the immediate emancipation, or freeing, of the slaves, without payment to their owners and without taking the slaves from the country. He cut loose from and denounced the American Colonization Society, which had been organized in 1816 to remove the free blacks from America. To help this Society the

National Government in 1822 established the colony of Liberia on the west coast of Africa, and by 1830 about 

1100 emancipated Negroes had been sent there, where most of them died from malaria. Garrison believed that

the slaveholders who belonged to this Society merely wished to get rid of the free blacks and not to disturb the slave system.

In 1833 Garrison, Whittier, and others formed the American Anti-Slavery Society at Philadelphia. Their declaration of principles is an important document in the history of the United States, because it marks the beginning of a movement that aroused the country on slavery and finally broke up political parties and divided the Union. The abolitionists admitted that under the 

Constitution each State had the right to retain and regulate slavery within its borders, but they demanded that Congress stop the interstate slave trade, put an end to slavery in the territories and the District of Columbia, and that no more slave States be admitted to the Union. 

The Anti-Slavery Society proposed to send agents to speak against slavery and to organize local anti-slavery societies in every city and village in the land. By 1840 over 200 local societies had been formed with nearly 200,000 members. These positive anti-slavery men and women denounced slaveholding in unsparing terms as man-stealing and robbery, as a sin and a crime and a disgrace. 

Effect of Abolitionism on the South. The South was soon aroused by these attacks. The slaveholders were indignant at what they regarded as insults. They felt no guilt in holding slaves. Their slaves, they claimed, were treated kindly and were taken care of far better than they could care for themselves, and much better than many Northern factory hands. They claimed that the Negroes were happier as slaves than they would be if free, with no one to care for them, and that the slaves were well fed and well clothed. 

The Southerners felt that the Southern people were responsible for Southern slavery; they wanted no

meddling, and they declared that Northern abolitionists should mind their own business, give attention to their own sins, and leave the South alone. Since the South had over a billion dollars invested in slave property, it was not in human nature for the slave owners meekly to submit and give up this property. Southerners believed that to abolish slavery would be ruinous to the South, especially to the cotton belt where cotton raising was profitable, and where the planters claimed cotton could not be produced successfully without slave labor. They felt, too, that the abolitionists were attacking not only their property but their characters, since the slaveholders were constantly spoken of as criminals and cruel oppressors. People are especially sensitive when their characters are attacked.

Slave Insurrection. The Southerners said that if the slaves were set free a horrible race war would result, and that the only effect of the abolition agitation would be to arouse the slaves to slaughter their masters and to destroy the homes of the whites. The very year Garrison's Liberator first appeared, the Nat Turner Insurrection took place in Virginia, in which sixty whites were killed and thousands of dollars worth of property destroyed before the uprising of the slaves was put down. Although there was no connection between the 

Liberator and this insurrection, Southern people believed that such Negro uprisings would result if the abolition agitators were not stopped. The "black terror "always hung like a nightmare over the Southern communities.

John Randolph said that a mother in the South never heard the fire bell in the night without clasping her children to her arms in fear of a slave insurrection. the result of such fear and of the agitation by the abolitionists, the lot of the slaves became harder. They were not allowed to learn to read, or to leave the plantation, As

or to go out at night, or to meet together in groups. 

Runaways were harshly treated. 

Stirred up by fear and anger, the South demanded that the abolitionists be put down and that their societies be broken up by law. The legislature of Georgia offered a reward of $5000 to any one who would kidnap Garrison and bring him to that State for trial. The mayor of 

Boston was called upon to suppress the Liberator. He

PART OF THE FIRST ISSUE OF The Liberator reported that he had "ferreted out the paper, " which he had not heard of before, " and that Garrison's office was an obscure hole, his only visible help a Negro boy." 

Later, James Russell Lowell made this the text of his poem, "To W. L. Garrison," beginning:

In a small chamber, friendless and unseen, Toiled o'er his types one poor, unlearned young man: 

The place was dark, unfurnitured and mean, Yet there the freedom of a race began. 

The Effect of Abolitionism on the North. The aboli tionists were almost as unpopular in the North as in the 

South. Mobs tried to suppress them. Garrison and his

followers were everywhere abused and denounced. Their meetings were broken up, their speakers stoned, and for a time it seemed that free speech and a free press were to be denied to them.

Prudence Crandall in Connecticut had her school broken up, her school house stoned and set on fire, because she had admitted a few Negro girls to the school. James 

G. Birney, an abolitionist writer in Cincinnati, had his printing press destroyed and thrown into the Ohio River; 

Rev. Elijah P. Lovejoy, another abolitionist leader in the West, was killed at Alton, Illinois, while defending his press against a pro-slavery mob (1837). At a meeting in Boston Garrison was seized and dragged through the streets amid a howling mob. The police had the greatest difficulty in saving his life, putting him into jail for safety.

Growth of Abolitionism. In spite of such attacks the abolitionists stood their ground, and the persecutions they suffered made friends for their cause. Many antislavery men who were not abolitionists joined them in defending free assembly, free speech, and a free press. 

One of these supporters, William Ellery Channing, a noted minister who had written a powerful essay against slavery, said the abolitionists had won everlasting honor for the courage with which they had met the mob outrages that were heaped upon them. 

Another defender was Wendell Phillips, a rich young lawyer in Boston, who saw Garrison dragged through the streets by the mob. The sight led Phillips to devote his life to the cause of the slave. He became the most noted of anti-slavery orators, and a powerful champion of the abolition cause. One of the most dramatic scenes in the anti-slavery struggles took place in Faneuil Hall, Boston, in 1837, when Phillips made his famous speech on the death of Lovejoy. "In the annals of American

speech there had been no such scene since Patrick 

Henry's electrical warning to George III. Three such scenes are illustrious in our history, Patrick Henry at Williamsburg, Wendell Phillips in Faneuil Hall, and

Abraham Lincoln at Gettys burg- these three and there is no fourth." 

WENDELL PHILLIPS 

1811-1884 The Abolitionists and the 

Government. The slavery question could not be kept out of Government activities.

The abolitionists sent by mail to all parts of the country, their papers, pamphlets, and illustrated almanacs describing the abuses of slavery.

Southern people objected vigorously to this use of the mails and demanded that the abolitionists be prevented from sending their documents through the post office. 

They declared that such documents were "incendiary," that is, that they would stir up the slaves to insurrection. or induce them to run away. The abolitionists insisted upon their right to use the mails as part of the freedom. of the press guaranteed in the Constitution. No law was passed against them in this matter, but many postmasters refused to deliver abolitionist literature, and much discussion was aroused. 

The Right of Petition-Gag Rule. Another cause of excitement was the petitions which the abolitionists sent to Congress asking that body to abolish slavery in the District of Columbia, where it had continued to exist after Maryland and Virginia had given the District to the United States for a capital. The anti-slavery men felt that the whole nation was responsible for slavery at the

national capital and they wished it abolished. Hot discussions arose in Congress and at one time all the Congressmen from the South threatened to leave in a body, angrily declaring that they would not permit slavery to be discussed and slaveholders denounced on the floor of Congress. They insisted that they would not stay in a Union or continue to support a Government in which they and their property interests were attacked in such an outrageous way. The party leaders were anxious, therefore, not to have the question of slavery discussed at all in Congress, as such discussion would tend to divide their parties and to destroy the Union. 66 To prevent the bringing up of the slavery question in 

Congress a gag rule" was adopted to shut off the abolition petitions. This rule provided that all petitions, or papers, relating to slavery should be laid aside without notice. This action raised a new issue. The right to petition the Government is guaranteed in the Constitution and down to this time (1834-1837) Congress had always treated petitions with respect. Should this right now be denied? The attempt to destroy it and the effort to " gag "the anti-slavery men brought them a new and fiery champion, John Quincy Adams, who henceforth was a sturdy enemy of slavery and an able ally of the abolitionists.

After retiring from the presidency, Adams had been elected in 1831 to the House of Representatives. He was not an abolitionist, and he did not favor the abolition of slavery in the District of Columbia. But he did not wish the slavery interests to control the Government and he was ready to fight for free petition and free debate. 

He denounced the "gag rule" and said that the people had a right to the proper reception of their petitions on any subject. Adams kept up the fight for eight years, until the "gag policy" was abandoned in 1844. This

struggle and the effort to prevent the discussion of slavery tended to increase the anti-slavery ranks and to arouse the abolitionists to fight harder than ever for their cause.

The Anti-Slavery Movement and Politics. By 1840 

the abolitionists had split into two factions- the "Garrisonians" and the "Liberty Party Men." Garrison and his followers were the more radical faction. They refused to vote, or to go into politics, or to try to elect any one to public office. They were satisfied to denounce wrongs and to stir people up. Garrison was even ready to break up the Union. "No Union with slaveholders," he declared, and he denounced the Constitution as covenant with death and an agreement with hell." He believed that it was a pro-slavery document. He represented an extremely small wing of anti-slavery men. 66 The "Liberty Party Men," on the other hand, believed in forming a political party, going into politics, and trying to get control of the Government. They believed that the Constitution was "a liberty document." They denounced certain pro-slavery clauses in it, such as the fugitive-slave clause, but they believed that if its general purpose and spirit were carried out slavery could be limited and in time destroyed. In 1840, therefore, they organized the Liberty Party and nominated James 

G. Birney for President. In the presidential election that year this party polled only 7000 votes, but it deserves to be remembered as the beginning of a political movement against slavery. Four years later the 

Liberty Party again nominated Birney for the presidency, and cast 62,000 votes for him.

The next four years were to witness the disappearance of the party, but were to see its successor, the Free Soil Party, muster several hundred thousand Americans to its support.

CHAPTER XXIII  

If slavery had been confined or restricted to the Southern States, it would probably have died a natural death or the Southern people would have abolished it. Only a handful of people in the North really desired, or intended, to interfere with slavery in the slave States. 

The great majority were willing to let it alone. The abolitionists could not make much headway by trying to stir people up over slavery in States where slavery did not exist. It was chiefly when territorial expansion occurred or when new territories were to be organized that the slavery question became a sore subject of discussion between the North and the South. This appears clearly in the annexation of Texas and the events that followed.

Early Settlements in Texas. Texas was first claimed by the United States in 1803 as a part of the Louisiana Purchase. By the Florida treaty of 1819, however, we gave up our claim and Texas became a part of Mexico. 

Many Americans thought Texas ought not to have been surrendered and the United States on several occasions tried to buy the territory back, but Mexico, which had become independent of Spain, refused to sell it. 

Meanwhile an American named Moses Austin obtained from Mexico a grant of land for an American colony in Texas. Soon afterward Austin died, but his son, 376

Stephen F. Austin, the founder of modern Texas, led a colony of several hundred American families into the region. Other Americans came from year to year, as land in Texas was fertile and cheap and could be bought at a very low price. In 1832 Sam Houston (hus'tun) came. He was a friend of Andrew Jackson and had been 

Governor of Tennessee and a member of Congress. 

Houston became noted as a Texan leader. 

The Texan Revolution. These American Texans did not like the Mexican Government. The ruler, Santa

Anna, was a dictator, and in 1835 changed the constitution of Mexico, reducing Texas to a province and depriving her people of local self-government. Mexico had abolished slavery (1829) and now sought to prevent other 

American slaveholders from coming into Texas. The

Texans felt that their lives and property were not protected. They differed from the Mexicans in both race and religion, and they wanted a republican local government of their own, such as they had been accustomed to in the United States. In 1835 they rose in revolt against 

Mexico and declared their independence. 

Under Houston, Bowie (bō'i), and Crockett, the Texans fought bravely for freedom. Their struggle was marked by heroic courage and devoted patriotism. At Goliad (gō'li ǎd'), 370 Texans, who had been compelled to surrender to a large force of Mexicans, were marched out and shot down like cattle on the plea that they were 

American "filibusters," that is, organized outlaws invading a foreign country. A few months later (1836), a band of 183 Texans were besieged at the Alamo (ä'lä mō) in San Antonio by an army of 1000 Mexicans. Colonel 

Travis, the Texan commander, said that he would "never surrender nor retreat." The Mexicans attacked the building from three sides and a desperate hand-to-hand struggle followed in which all but a half dozen of the

Texans died fighting. The survivors were all massacred, not a man being left alive to tell the tale. 

The Texans never forgot Goliad and the Alamo. Their brothers who died in those battles were regarded as 35 Santa Fe 30° 100°

TEXAN INDEPENDENCE AND THE MEXICAN WAR 

martyrs to liberty, and the Texans resolved never again to submit to Mexican rule.

Several weeks later the Texans defeated the Mexicans and put them to flight in the decisive battle of San 

Jacinto (sån ja sin'tō). The Texans charged in a

terrible onset, shouting their war cry, "Remember the 

Alamo." The Mexicans lost 1600 men, twice the number of the whole Texan army, while only two Texans were killed and twenty-three wounded. Soon afterward

Texan independence was recognized by Great Britain, France, and the United States, and for nine years (18361845) Texas was the independent" Lone Star Republic."

The Movement for Annexation. The Texans and many 

Southern leaders wished Texas to be annexed to the United States. Texas was a large and fertile country in which slavery was allowed by the new Texan government. Many Southerners believed that the region was necessary for the extension of slavery; they looked upon the Texans as brother Americans; and they maintained that the annexation of Texas would be only the re-annexation of territory that ought never to have been given up. They also felt that more slave States were needed in order to preserve the "balance of power " in the Senate. Owing to its enormous size Texas could be subdivided and more slave States obtained. The region was so vast that six States the size of Ohio, or seven the size of Indiana, or thirty as large as Massachusetts, could be made out of it. The act proposing the annexation of Texas provided that the State might be subdivided into four more States.

Opposition to Annexation. The reasons that caused slavery leaders to urge the annexation of Texas caused anti-slavery men to oppose annexation. They declared that at all hazards slavery extension must be prevented. They had little sympathy with the Texan struggle for independence. To admit Texas, they maintained, would be like seizing the territory of a weaker nation. Such an act would surely cause war with Mexico and would only strengthen the control of the slaveholders over the National Government. Some of them even said

that if Texas were annexed the Union ought to be broken up.

Annexation and the Election of 1844. The question of annexation became the leading issue in the presidential election of 1844. It was expected that Van Buren would again be the Democratic candidate, but he had opposed annexation and the Southern leaders would not have him. Instead, they nominated James K. Polk of Tennessee. He is called the first "dark horse" in American politics, because he had not been talked of for the presidency before he was nominated. Polk came out openly for annexation.

The Whigs nominated Henry Clay, their great leader and the advocate of a protective tariff. They thought he would surely defeat the obscure and commonplace Polk. But Clay's attitude on annexation was not clear. 

Some Southern Whigs feared that he would oppose annexation and some anti-slavery men in the North were afraid that he would favor it; as a result he lost both kinds of voters and Polk was elected. The campaign song of the Democrats helped to bring them victory: 

Hurrah for Polk and annexation, Down with Clay and high taxation.

The Liberty Party again nominated Birney, who received nearly ten times as many votes as he had received in 1840. Since many anti-slavery Whigs voted for Birney because of their distrust of Clay, the thirdparty abolitionists were blamed for Clay's defeat. 

Oregon and "Fifty-Four-Forty or Fight." In the election of 1844 the Democrats were aided in the election of Polk by their bold claim to all of the Oregon country. 

Sixty-two years before (1792) the Columbia River had been discovered by Captain Gray of Boston. In 1805 the Northwest had been explored by Lewis and Clark,

and a few years later a trading post had been established by John Jacob Astor at Astoria, near the mouth of the Columbia. During succeeding years American missionaries came into Oregon to work with the Indians, and in 1843 Dr. Marcus Whitman led a band of missionaries and settlers into what is now the State of Washington.

These events gave the United States a good claim to the Oregon country, a claim that was disputed, however, by the British who had also made early discoveries and had established trading posts in the region. 

In the campaign of 1844 the Democrats declared that they were for the "re-annexation of Texas and the re-occupation of Oregon." They said that they favored more territory for the North as well as for the South, and this was popular. "All of Oregon or none," "Fifty-four-forty or fight!" they shouted.

The Oregon country was west of the Rockies and extended from the northern boundary of California to the southern boundary of Alaska, or from 42° to 54° 40'. 

In 1818 Great Britain and America agreed to occupy the region together, but the time had now come when the 

PRESIDENT JOHN TYLER 

John Tyler was born in Virginia in 1790 and was graduated from 

William and Mary College seventeen years later. He was the first Vice President to succeed to the presidency. He served as a member of the House and also of the Senate. Like Jefferson, he was an excellent violinist. But unlike Jefferson, he was an excellent public speaker. He later served as Chancellor of William and Mary College and spent the last seventeen years of his life in the retirement of his country home on the James River. He died there in 1862. ownership had to be settled.

There was talk of war to decide the dispute, but neither nation wished an armed conflict and neither would give up all its claim to the other. 

Therefore a compromise was agreed to and the territory was divided by a treaty made the year after Polk became 

President. By the treaty the forty-ninth parallel was made the boundary between the 

United States and the British possessions. Thus by a compromise we gave up part of our claim in the Northwest and avoided war with Great Britain.

Texas Annexed and Slavery 

Extended. John Tyler, as 

Vice President, succeeded to the presidency after the death of President Harrison in 

April, 1841. Tyler was a warm friend of annexation, and after Polk's election in 1844, which President Tyler interpreted as a command from the people to annex Texas, he urged Congress to act in the matter. A joint resolution passed Congress in the last days of Tyler's

administration (March 1, 1845), providing for the admission of Texas. The Texan Congress gave its consent and Texas became a State in December, 1845-the last slave State that ever came into the Union.

Causes of the Mexican War. The annexation of Texas led directly to war with Mexico, as the anti-slavery men had declared it would. Mexico had not given up her claim to Texas and, when Texas was admitted to the 

Union, Mexico ordered her minister to leave Washington; that is, she refused to have any further dealings with the United States.

Texas claimed the territory east of the Rio Grande (rē ō grän'då) from the mouth of that river to its source. 

Mexico said that the land between the Nueces (nwa'sĕs)

and the Rio Grande was not a part of Texas. President Polk held that when we annexed Texas we annexed all that Texas claimed, and he ordered General 

Taylor with 4000 men to the Rio Grande "to protect 

THE BATTLE OF BUENA VISTA, 1847 

The artist's conception of the battlefield when General Taylor won his decisive victory over the Mexicans. the Texan frontier." The Mexicans looked on this act as an invasion of their territory and attacked some of Taylor's men in the disputed district. When Taylor's dispatch announcing the attack reached Washington, Polk sent a war message to Congress (May 12, 1846) in which he said that Mexico had invaded our territory and had "shed American blood on American soil."

Congress adopted Polk's view and, without taking time to inquire into the truth or justice of the claim, declared that "war exists by the act of the Republic of Mexico." 

Polk was blamed for taking a step that was sure to result in war, instead of leaving the dispute about the territory open to settlement through conference.

The question might have been settled by arbitration and the war could have been avoided. But there were other provoking matters. Mexico refused to pay for 

American property seized in raids or destroyed by revolutions in Mexico. On the other hand Mexico resented the aid Americans had given to the Texan revolutionists. Besides, President

Polk was very eager to acquire California, which was then a part of Mexico. The expansion fever had set in among Americans and it was claimed that our "manifest destiny" should lead us to the Pacific coast. All these things led to war with Mexico. 

GENERAL WINFIELD SCOTT 

Hero of Lundy's Lane in the 

War of 1812, victor at Cerro 

Gordo in the Mexican War, a 

Virginian and a Union man' in the Civil War. 

The War and Its Results. 

The Mexican War was soon ended. It lasted less than two years, from May, 1846, to February, 1848. General 

Taylor beat the Mexicans at Palo Alto (pa'lō ǎl'tō) and

Resaca de la Palma (rå sä’'kå då lä päl'må) on May 8 and 9, 1846. He then crossed the Rio Grande and stormed the walls of Monterey (mon'tê ra') on September 24, 1846. At this time he was ordered to send 10,000 of his men to General Scott, who was planning to invade Mexico from another direction. With only 5000 men remaining, Taylor was attacked by Santa Anna, the Mexican commander, with 20,000 men. Santa Anna told Taylor he must surrender or be cut to pieces; but Taylor chose his ground and against great odds totally defeated the Mexicans in the decisive battle of Buena Vista (bü'nȧ vis'tȧ), February 23, 1847. As a result of his victory

northeastern Mexico was conquered and the "Hero of 

Buena Vista," or "Old Rough and Ready," as Taylor was affectionately called by his men, began to be thought of as a good candidate for the 

GENERAL JOHN C. FREMONT presidency. 

General Scott captured Vera Cruz (va'rȧ kroos') 

early in 1847 and at once began a march of two hundred miles to the Mexican capital. 

He stormed the mountain pass of Cerro Gordo (ser'rō gôr'do), and after several rapid victories led his triumphant army into the City of Mexico, September 14, 1847. Meanwhile General 

Kearny (kär'ni), marching from Missouri by the old 

Santa Fé trail, had conquered 

New Mexico; and General 

John C. Frémont with the help of several ships and a few hundred American settlers had conquered California. All the vast regions westward from Texas to the

Pacific Ocean, which had been Spanish-Mexican territory, were now in the possession of the victorious armies of the United States. 

General Frémont was born in 

Georgia in 1813. His father was a Frenchman and his mother a

Virginian. He spent a large part of his early life as an explorer and was familiarly known as the pathfinder." He fought with distinction in the Mexican and 66 

Civil Wars. He was a United 

States Senator from California, and a candidate for the presidency on the Republican ticket in 1856. He died in New York in 1890.

Mexico was conquered. Her territory was overrun,. her armies helpless, her government broken up, her capital in the hands of the enemy, and she had to submit. 

A few Americans favored annexing all of Mexico to the United States, but President Polk said that he had been

By the treaty of peace (1848) Mexico was compelled to give to the United States all territory north of the Rio Grande and the Gila (he'là) Rivers, including New 

Mexico, California, Nevada, Utah, Arizona, and parts of

Colorado and Wyoming-about 800,000 square miles in all. The United States agreed to pay $15,000,000 to 

Mexico and to pay $3,500,000 more of claims that our citizens had against the Mexican Government. 

The war cost about $100,000,000 and 13,000 lives. As the result of the conflict the United States became a power along the Pacific, and a new westward movement began, which we shall describe in a later chapter. We must now notice the problems that immediately followed the war.
