Aguinaldo (ä'ge näl'dō), fought with the Americans, but when they heard that the United States did not intend to grant them inde- pendence, they rose in insurrection. 

Guerrilla warfare continued for two or three years, but after General Funston captured 

Aguinaldo, the uprising was put down and peace and order were restored. 

Civil Government for the Philippines. 

These islands, of which there are more than seven thousand, were discovered by Magellan in 1521. Their

O UNDERWOOD UNDERWOOD LIFE IN THE PHILIPPINES 

present population Life in the Philippines is tropical in character. 

Note the abundant vegetation and the temporary character of the houses. is nearly 12,000,000.

Following the Spanish War, military rule was established for a time in the islands, but after peace was restored, preparations were made to give the Philippines a civil government. 

Hundreds of teachers were sent over from America to open public schools and to teach the people how to read and write. Better roads were built, better farming methods were introduced, and as fast as possible civil government was established. Although the Filipinos have not been made citizens of the United States, they

have most of the rights and privileges of such citizenship without its obligations.

A commission under the leadership of William H. 

Taft (later Secretary of War, President of the United States, and Chief Justice of the United States) set up a form of government in the Islands (1902). In 1907, as provided by Congress, the Filipinos were permitted to elect a legislature with limited powers, and in 1916 Congress abolished the commission under which the islands had been governed since 1902 and substituted a legislature chosen by the inhabitants-a Senate of 24 members and a House of Representatives of 91 members, all elected by popular vote, except two Senators appointed by the Governor-General. The Governor- 

General, appointed by the President, remains at the head of the government of the Islands. 66 In the act of 1916 Congress declared America's intention to grant independence to the Philippines as soon as stable self-government " could be established, but fixed no date. In 1926 President Coolidge sent a deputy to investigate the conditions in the Islands, but he reported against granting independence at that time.

America and World Politics. The Spanish War brought the United States definitely into world politics. 

For eighty years Americans had been concerned chiefly with internal affairs—the tariff, internal improvements, slavery, civil war, reconstruction, promotion of business. 

Now we had distant colonies, and had become the controlling power in the Caribbean Sea. Many Americans saw at once that the Panama Canal would have to be built.

Hawaii Annexed. During the Spanish War, we had annexed Hawaii, 2000 miles from the California coast, and had thereby gained a valuable naval base in the

Pacific. We had been interested in the islands for many years. Hawaii was the "sugar bowl" of the Pacific coast, and friendly relations had long been maintained. 

In 1893 a revolt occurred in Hawaii, the Queen was overthrown, and a provisional government was set up which negotiated a treaty of annexation with the United States. 

In the last week of his administration Harrison submitted this treaty to the Senate, but when Cleveland became President he withdrew the treaty and sent an agent to Hawaii to investigate. This agent of the government ordered the American flag to be lowered from the Government buildings in Honolulu and the American Marines to leave the city. An effort was made to restore the 

Queen, but the provisional government retained its power and in 1898 Congress annexed the islands by a joint resolution agreed to by Hawaii.

American ships crossing the Pacific had long found Hawaii a good stopping place. American missionaries had been the first foreigners to educate and help the people. American business and American money had done much to improve the islands, and many Americans had made their homes there. The people of the islands were friendly. In the Spanish War they were on our side, supplying our fleet with provisions and coal. Annexation therefore came about with mutual consent. seen that possession of the islands might be in the future a great advantage to the United States. In 1900 Hawaii was made a territory and American citizenship was conferred on the Hawaiians. 

It was 

China and the "Open Door." Through the annexation of the Philippines, the United States was brought into contact with the rising power of Japan and into closer relations with eastern Asia, a storm center of world politics. American trade interests in China were increased, and the United States put forward the demand for the

66 66 open door" in China, meaning that the trading ports of China should be open to all nations on equal terms. 

At this time European nations were obtaining concessions," or special rights to ports and territory in China and were seeking monopolies in trade. Gradually the whole coast of China had come under the control of foreign countries. Each country closed the port it controlled to the rest of the world. America desired no "concessions" and wished China to be free from control by foreign powers, asking only the "open door." John

Hay, the American Secretary of State, announced this friendly policy toward China and for a time put a stop to the seizure of power there by foreign countries. 

The Boxer Rebellion in China. The foregoing events made the United States a world power, and we soon had occasion to take part in distant affairs. In 1900 the 

Chinese Boxers, a secret society opposed to all foreign interference in Chinese affairs, rose in rebellion against the Chinese Government and began to murder foreigners, or to drive them out. The Boxers killed the 

German ambassador and many other foreigners in Peking. In this crisis the United States sent soldiers who joined with the troops of other powers (Japan, Germany, England, Russia, France) and helped to put down the insurrection in order to protect their citizens in China.

After this affair the Chinese were forced to pay large sums for damages. Every country demanded payment, far exceeding the amount of the actual damages. When it was found that the damages awarded to the United States were excessive, we kept only what we were fairly entitled to and returned the balance to China with the understanding that the money be used as an endowment for the education of Chinese students in the United States. This increased Chinese friendship toward

America and enabled hundreds of capable young Chinese to study in American universities, to learn something of the American people and republican government. 

In the course of years all this was doubtless an important influence in bringing about the revolution in China, which was led by young Chinese who had been educated in America. This uprising resulted in the overthrow of the 

Emperor and the establishment of a republican form of government in China.

Imperialism. Following the Spanish War, as we have seen, islands over the sea came into the possession of the United States. Some of these islands were 6000 miles away across the broad Pacific. We had acquired new territories many times before, but in every instance the treaties had made provision that the inhabitants, trans-. ferred with the new lands, become citizens of the United States, and that in time the new territories be admitted to the Union as "" equal republican States." 66 But no such provision was included in the treaty that ended the war with Spain. The newly acquired islands were to be "possessions " or " dependencies" and not a part of the United States, and their inhabitants were to be subjects, not citizens, of America. The treaty by which the islands were acquired promised no rights to their people; they were to be under the absolute control of Congress. The Constitution, according to a decision of the Supreme Court, did not apply to the islands; and their people could not appeal to its protection and guarantees. An act of Congress would be necessary to extend the Constitution over them and meanwhile Congress could govern them as it pleased.

This was called imperialism; that is, holding and governing subjects without constitutional rights. Many 

Americans protested against this policy. They declared that the Constitution made no provision for governing

dependencies; that the principles of the Declaration of 

Independence were opposed to the government of people without their consent. Senator George F. Hoar of

Massachusetts, one of the leading Republicans, asked in the United States Senate: "Can you lawfully buy with money, or get by brute force of arms, the right to hold in subjugation an unwilling people, and to impose upon them such a constitution as you, and not they, think best for them?"

Election of 1900. The question of imperialism became the main issue in the campaign of 1900. Bryan, who opposed imperialism and the holding of the islands, was again nominated by the Democrats. The Republicans renominated President McKinley. They endorsed what had been done in acquiring the islands and promised just government in the Philippines in the interest of the inhabitants. 

Gold in the Klondike. The gold standard was now definitely adopted by Congress (1900). Gold had been discovered in the Klondike (in Alaska) and more money had come into circulation. Good times were returning to the country. Farm products were rising in price. Cattle were selling at $4.00 per 100 pounds on the hoof on 

Western farms. Business was prospering, and the voters refused to get excited about imperialism and the fate of the dark-skinned Filipinos 6000 miles away. Americans believed that Congress would govern the islands fairly, advance their interests, and give the inhabitants such rights as we asked for ourselves. Labor was more fully employed and workingmen were urged to vote for "four years more of the full dinner pail.

Such conditions brought about the re-election of President McKinley by a decisive vote, and Theodore Roosevelt, then Governor of New York, was chosen Vice 

President.

CHAPTER XXXV  

A State of War in Europe. At the opening of the twentieth century, Europe was prepared for war. The nations were armed to the teeth. There were national hatreds, fears, and jealousies. There were racial dislikes, commercial rivalries, conflicting ambitions. France wanted the return of Alsace-Lorraine which Germany had taken from her in 1871. Germany wanted her place in the sun," that is, she wanted colonies outside of Europe, and a navy as big as England's to defend them. Secret treaties and rival alliances were formed. 

Provinces of small nations such as Serbia were seized by stronger powers, and the large nations of Europe. seemed divided into hostile camps. Only a spark was needed to cause a deadly explosion. 66 Origin of the World War. The spark was lighted in 

Serbia. On June 28, 1914, Archduke Francis Ferdinand, heir to the throne of Austria-Hungary, was assassinated while on a visit to Bosnia, a southern province of the empire, which had been taken from Serbia. The young man who killed the Archduke was a member of a Serbian secret society which had as one of its aims the separation of Bosnia from Austria-Hungary and its restoration to Serbia.

It was generally supposed that Austria would make certain demands on Serbia because a Serb had murdered 614

the Archduke, but four weeks passed before anything was done. Then, on July 23, Austria sent a harsh note to Serbia, charging the Serbian Government with responsibility for the murder of Francis Ferdinand and his wife, and making most humiliating demands upon the country. Serbia was given forty-eight hours in which to comply with the demands.

In its reply Serbia agreed to all the requirements except one, that Austrian officers should sit in Serbian courts to see that justice was done. This demand meant the surrender of Serbian independence and Serbia refused; she said, however, that if her reply was not satisfactory she was willing to submit the whole matter to the Hague Tribunal or to a conference of European powers.

Austria held the reply to be unsatisfactory. She declared that she would submit the question to no tribunal but her own and at once proceeded to invade Serbia to reduce that country to subjection. Germany, an ally of 

Austria, stood by, consenting to this act of war. 

When Austria invaded Serbia, Russia began to mobilize her troops, or gather her soldiers together, to protect the little Slav state. The Russians and the Serbians are both members of the Slav race and Russia had always been looked upon as the "big brother" and protector of the smaller Slavic states in southeastern Europe. Germany immediately sent a message to Russia demanding that Russia stop military preparations within twelve hours. At the same time Germany asked

France whether she would remain neutral in case of war between Russia and Germany. She gave France eighteen hours in which to reply. Russia ignored the 

German demand and France replied that she would act as her interests demanded. Germany at once attacked both countries.

The Neutrality of Belgium: Great Britain Enters the 

War. The German plan was to invade France, crush the French army, capture Paris, and render France helpless before Russia could bring her vast armies into the field. Since strong fortifications guarded the boundary between France and Germany, the German commanders decided to invade France through Belgium. That was the easiest way. But the neutrality of Belgium had been guaranteed years before by a treaty signed by the great powers of Europe, including Germany. Germany asked 

Belgium to allow German troops to cross Belgian territory on their way to France; if Belgium consented, Germany said that her army would safeguard Belgian territory; if Belgium refused, Germany would treat her as an enemy. Belgium replied that her neutrality had been guaranteed by a solemn treaty signed by the great powers of Europe and that she would resist any invasion of her territory.

When England learned that German armies were marching into Belgium she demanded assurances from 

Germany that Belgian neutrality would be respected. 

The German chancellor replied that military necessity required the German armies to cross Belgium, and he used to the British ambassador in Berlin a sentence that is still remembered—that Britain ought not to enter the war for the sake of a scrap of paper.' The contempt thus expressed for a solemn treaty helped to bring public opinion in Great Britain behind her Government when she declared war on Germany (August 4, 1914). 66 99 A large part of Europe was now ablaze with war and other nations were to enter the conflict. The struggle became indeed a "World War." Before the conflict ended the following countries were at war with Germany: 

Belgium, Brazil, China, Costa Rica, Cuba, France, Great

Britain, Greece, Guatemala, Haiti, Honduras, Italy,

Japan, Liberia, Montenegro, Nicaragua, Panama, Por tugal, Roumania, Russia, Serbia, Siam, and the United States. Czechoslovakia and Poland joined these Allies in so far as they were able to do so, and Bolivia, Peru, Uruguay, and Ecuador broke off diplomatic relations.

American Neutrality. When the war began, the American people had no thought of taking part in the conflict. They expected, as a matter of course, that America would keep out. Our policy since the time of Washington had been to avoid European entanglements. 

Accordingly President Wilson issued a proclamation of neutrality, warning his countrymen against the danger of taking sides and asking them to be " neutral in thought and deed," with a "spirit of impartiality, fairness, and friendliness to all concerned." 66 The Blockade. The war, however, soon involved 

American property and American lives. Hardly had the conflict started than Great Britain's navy, in company with other allied fleets, placed a blockade around Germany and Austria, to prevent those countries from getting supplies from the outside world. German ships were swept off the ocean, and the German navy, about two-thirds as strong as the British, remained in hiding during practically the entire war. Only once did her battle fleet venture out of the Baltic, when it was soon turned back by a detachment of the British navy in the battle of Jutland (1916). In this battle Britain's margin in weight of naval metal was twice as great as Germany's.

In carrying out her naval warfare Great Britain claimed the right to force all neutral ships bound for Holland, Norway, Sweden, or Denmark to stop for inspection to let English naval officers see that such ships were not carrying contraband goods, such as munitions

and materials used for war. Britain was determined that such goods should not reach Germany either directly or through neutral ports. This policy recalled the causes of the War of 1812 and gave the United States much concern because it interfered with our rights as a neutral nation.

But we soon were even more concerned by greater wrongs committed by Germany. Great Britain had declared that all food was contraband if shipped to Germany, or if it might reach Germany, since food for her soldiers was as necessary for continuing the war as were munitions. 

To Germany the British decision seemed like dooming an entire nation to destruction through starvation. 

In retaliation, Germany declared the waters about the British Isles a "war zone" and declared that all enemy merchant vessels would be sunk regardless of the lives of passengers and crew. Passengers from neutral countries were warned to stay off British passenger ships, and neutral vessels were told to stay out of the 66 war zone. 99 66 The Submarine Warfare. The recently invented submarines, or "U boats," were the only sea weapons

Germany could use to carry out her intentions against Great Britain. The British navy could block the German ports as far as the surface of the sea was concerned, but the submarines could steal out under the water and sink British merchant ships and sometimes torpedo a war vessel. Germany now proposed to use the submarine to starve out England, to destroy British shipping, to sink her merchantmen and the ships of neutrals that were carrying food and goods to England. If Germany could sink enough ships and cut off supplies to the British Isles, Great Britain would have to sue for peace.

The German plan was a desperate one, and had never before been used in sea warfare. But war itself is a monstrous cruelty which no nation can humanize or refine. Previously it had been the rule in international law that warships should not destroy unresisting mer- 60

This the submarine cannot do. It is impossible for the officers of a submarine to visit a merchantman at sea and examine her papers and cargo. It is also impossible for them to make a prize of her, and if they sink her, the crew and all on board are usually left to the mercy of the sea. Yet, regardless of international law and the lives of innocent passengers, the German submarines proceeded to carry out the plan. 020

THE SUBMARINE WAR ZONE 

On February 1, 1917, Germany began her unrestricted submarine warfare in the waters around the British Isles and off the coast of France. 

On May 7, 1915, a German submarine sank without warning the great British passenger ship, the Lusitania, off the coast of Ireland. About 1200 people were drowned, many of them women and children. Of those drowned 114 were American citizens.

American Protest. The sinking of the Lusitania aroused much feeling against Germany in the United

States. Many Americans demanded that all relations with Germany should be broken off at once. On the other hand, many German sympathizers criticized President Wilson for being too lenient toward England when that country interfered with our commerce. President 

Wilson was patient with both sides, desiring above all else to keep the country out of war. Toward Germany he now asserted that submarines could not be used against merchantmen without a " violation of the sacred principles of justice and humanity." He protested against such warfare as illegal and inhuman, and demanded reparation and the prevention of such occurrences. Finally, after many notes had been exchanged, he succeeded in exacting a promise from the German Government that ships would not be sunk without due regard to the safety of the passengers and crew. 66 But the promise was not kept. A year after the Lusitania was sent to the bottom, a German submarine sank the passenger steamer Sussex without warning and with great loss of life. Two Americans were among the injured.

The President at once served notice upon Germany that he would break off diplomatic relations unless 

Germany abandoned its unlawful submarine warfare.

Germany promised to do so, in case the United States should induce Great Britain to give up certain features of her blockade. The American reply was to the effect that Great Britain's acts had nothing to do with 

Germany's violations of American rights.

Developments were anxiously awaited. They came on 

January 31, 1917, when the German ambassador, Count von Bernstorff, notified President Wilson that on the following day "unrestricted submarine warfare would be begun on a large area around the British Isles." 

Three days later President Wilson severed diplomatic

relations with Germany, dismissed Count von Bernstorff, and recalled Ambassador Gerard from Berlin. It was chiefly this submarine warfare that brought the United 

States into the war.

American Public Opinion. America had other com plaints and grievances against Germany. The great majority of Americans had come to believe that Germany was responsible for the war; that she had started the conflict for purposes of national ambition; that she cared nothing for international law or the rights of humanity; that she was fighting for the control of Europe, and for world power. 

To add fuel to the flame, the "Zimmerman note" was published shortly after diplomatic relations were broken. off. Dr. Zimmerman, of the German Government, in a note to the German minister to Mexico, proposed that in case the United States declared war against Germany an alliance should be made between Germany and Mexico and, if possible, Japan's aid should also be obtained. 

An invasion of the United States should then be made and the Mexican cessions (Arizona and New Mexico) should be restored to Mexico.

This scheme, together with other events, convinced the United States that Germany was plotting against this country and would show no regard for American rights. For some time German agents and spies had encouraged labor troubles and strikes in American factories. Munition factories had been blown up, bombs placed in ships sailing to England and France, American ships sunk, and American lives destroyed. Explosions, riots, and other discords in America were traced to German sources at a time when Germany and the United States were supposed to be on friendly terms. 

America Enters the War. American patience was at an end. A special session of Congress was convened at

which President Wilson read a message in which he recommended that Congress recognize a state of war as existing between Germany and the United States, because the course of Germany was nothing less than war. She had assailed our flag, disturbed our peace and order, OULD WOLD & UNDERIDEn ARRIVAL OF FIRST AMERICAN TROOPS IN FRANCE 

sunk our ships, and drowned our citizens without thought of help or mercy. She had so disregarded our rights that friendly relations could no longer be maintained. 

Four days later, April 6, 1917, Congress adopted the recommendation of the President, and American troops were soon on their way to France. 

The first great task was to prepare the nation for war. 

Congress quickly passed a Selective Service or "Draft "

Act, requiring all men between the ages of twenty-one and thirty-one, inclusive, to register for military service. 

Approximately 10,000,000 men registered under the Act on June 5, 1917. A later act lowered the age limit to

eighteen and raised the upper limit to forty-five. On 

September 12, 1918, about 13,000,000 men registered under this act. Here was the raw material for the largest army ever raised by any nation. 

The navy was already on a war footing. A considerable part of it was sent at once to European waters to help the Allied navies in their fight against the submarines. 

But it takes time to train and equip an army. To this end, huge camps, some of them capable of holding 

50,000 to to 75,000 men, were built in 

A UNITED STATES SUBMARINE 

The submarine is an American invention. many parts of the United States. Officers' Training 

Camps also prepared thousands of officers to drill and lead the troops.

The great factories of the country abandoned their usual business and took up the making of munitions and other supplies of war-guns, ammunition, uniforms, shoes, foodstuffs, airplanes.

Gigantic shipyards were established along both oceans and after some delay and blundering ships were turned out with astonishing speed. While the submarine was doing its deadly work, there was a crying need for shipping. The swift destroyers of the Allied and American navies, however, did worthy service in sinking the submarine or frightening it away from its former fields of activity.

The Cost of the War. All this of course was a very expensive business. Experts have estimated that our

part in the war, brief as it was, cost the United States $41,000,000,000, not including loans to our Allies. This enormous sum would mean an outlay of $400 for every

WEAPONS FOR LIBERTY A LIBERTY LOAN POSTER 

Artists were active in preparing appealing posters for the various Liberty Loan Drives." This is one of the most effective and was extensively 66 used. man, woman, and child in the United States. 

To meet these expenditures Congress acted promptly. It passed taxing measures of magnitude and made provision for borrowing vast sums of money. Liberty Loan 

Bonds were put upon the market and the people were asked to subscribe for $14,000,000,000 worth before the close of the war in November, 1918. The nation responded loyally by subscribing for $18,000,000,000 worth of bonds.

Government Operation of Railroads. In order to act with efficiency, the

Government took over the management of the railroads of the country. Practically all of them were placed under the Secretary of the 

Treasury, William G. McAdoo. Passenger service was reduced and simplified, and in freight transportation preference was given to war supplies. It was arranged that the roads were to be given back to their owners within twenty-one months after the declaration of peace. 

The Government raised wages and rates, but was operating the railroads at a loss when the war closed.

Food and Fuel. Armies cannot fight well unless they are well fed. The war withdrew millions of men from the industries of peace, and the food supply the world over was greatly diminished. The United States was called upon to help feed many of the European nations. 

The Government asked the farmers to increase the food supply and the people in cities and towns to plant small gardens and not to waste food. Potato patches were planted on waste strips of land along railway lines and in other ways there was a loyal response. In this emergency the United States established the Food 

Administration, with Herbert C. Hoover at its head.

Mr. Hoover had already done an able piece of work in directing the distribution of food among the starving millions of Europe. The Food Administration lessened profiteering" by fixing the prices of certain commodities and by specifying the conditions under which they could be purchased. Special regulations were made for wheat, flour, sugar, and meat. As a result of these efforts, the United States was able to send large cargoes of food to the nations of Europe. 66 Saving fuel was also necessary. As coal was needed for war, the people had to use less of it. The President appointed Dr. Harry A. Garfield, President of Williams 

College, to be United States Fuel Administrator, with general control of the entire situation. There were also 

State and local fuel administrators. These officers fixed the prices of coal, directed its distribution, and determined the amount which might be sold to any one person. "Heatless" days, "lightless nights, and "gasless

Sundays were inaugurated in an effort to conserve the supply of coal and gasoline. 99 99 Loans to the Allies. Besides sending food and coal, we also loaned to the Allies nearly $10,000,000,000, largely in the form of credits to be expended in this

country for the purchase of supplies. Thus our men, money, and food were all important items in bringing the conflict to a successful conclusion. 

Voluntary Organizations. The American army was better cared for than the armies of Europe. Hardship and suffering occurred here and there, due to indi- 

ARWOOD UNDERWOOD RED CROSS WORKERS 

66 Red Cross nurses were looked upon as angels of mercy." In the World War, Red Cross workers preparing supplies for the nurses were to be found in every city and hamlet and at every crossroads in the United States. vidual blundering and the wholesale manner in which things were necessarily done, but on the whole the men were well cared for. In this work voluntary or semiofficial organiza- tions had an important part. The 

Red Cross looked after wounded soldiers and the suffering civil popula- tion as well. The Junior Red Cross, composed of school children, also did a noble work in purchasing and preparing supplies.

The Young Men's Christian Association, the Knights of Columbus, the Young Men's Hebrew Association, and the Salvation Army ministered to the needs of the men while on and off duty. The Young Women's Christian 

Association opened hostess houses at the various camps in the United States where the men could find rest and recreation and meet their relatives and friends. The 

American Library Association put good books into the soldier's hands, and the War Camp Community Service furnished him entertainment while outside the camp.

The people of the United States by voluntary gifts raised hundreds of millions of dollars for these different agencies. 

American Troops in Europe. Early in the morning of 

June 14, 1917, 15,000 American soldiers sailed from New

York for France. On the following Fourth of July a battalion of American infantry marched through the city of Paris and a 

INTERNATIONAL FILM SERVICE 

wreath was laid upon the tomb of 

Lafayette. Except for General Per shing and his staff, who had gone to 

France a short time before, these were the first 

American fighting men to set foot on the soil of Europe. 

From this time on American troops went to France in a steady stream and in spite of the German submarine. None were lost at sea. By the beginning of 1918, 250,000 American soldiers were in Europe and many times that number were in the camps making ready to go. In the fall of 1918 more than 2,000,000 American "doughboys" were in Europe, and additional troops were crossing the Atlantic at the rate of 300,000 a month. 

The "convoy system" of escorting ships by destroyers and warships worked well and the Allied destroyers, with their depth bombs, guarded the transports against the German submarines. The safe transport of more than 2,000,000 troops across the sea, with their munitions, was a remarkable achievement; it was made possible 

A CAMOUFLAGED TRANSPORT 

Ships sailing between the United States and

Europe were painted in fantastic designs to confuse the enemy gunners. When in motion the ship presents a very difficult target.

only by the protection given by the American and British navies. 99 Women and the War. The women and girls of England, France, and the United States helped also to win the war. They kept the home fires burning while the men fought the battles. The Women's Land 

Army" of England did practically every kind of labor that a man could do, thus releasing thousands of men for the army. Clad in uniforms, with trousers taking the place of skirts, they worked in the fields and in the factories, in addition to nursing in the hospitals and driving ambulances and automobiles.

As the Selective Service Draft Act made inroads upon the labor forces of the United States, American women took the places of the men in offices, banks, elevators, street cars, and factories. In addition, tens of thousands of them went to Washington to become stenographers and typists in the Government departments. If the war had continued, the Women's Land Army of England would have been duplicated on a much larger scale in the United States.

Espionage Act and Pacifists. Before America entered the war, any citizen had the right to oppose such action. 

But after the Government had declared war, was a citizen justified in doing anything that would obstruct the Government in prosecuting the war? To meet such a situation Congress passed the Espionage Act shortly after our entrance into the struggle, imposing penalties for denouncing the war, for refusing military service, for encouraging disloyalty, for obstructing the recruiting service, and for other conduct tending to defeat the United States by interfering with its military and naval forces.

There were a few pacifists, conscientious people, who were opposed to war under all circumstances. The

99 

Socialists denounced the conflict as a 66 capitalist war, and their leader, Eugene V. Debs, was imprisoned for speaking against it. Nearly 2000 "conscientious objectors "were imprisoned for opposing the war or refusing military service. These were not ordinary criminals, but were known as "political prisoners" because they opposed the policy of war. Provision was made in the 

Espionage Act for conscientious objectors belonging to any religious sect whose creed or principles forbade its members to participate in war. They were allowed to render service in non-military ways, and the Quakers distinguished themselves in noble war service by nursing the sick and wounded, feeding the hungry, and bringing relief to the suffering. 66 

Efforts Toward Peace: Wilson's Fourteen Points. In an address to Congress on January 8, 1918, President

Wilson stated the attitude of the United States in the war and set forth what he would like to see accomplished. 

In this address he announced his famous "Fourteen 

Points" as the war aims of the United States: (1) "Open diplomacy ". no more secret treaties to be made. (2) Freedom of the seas, in war as well as in peace. 

Peaceful trade not to be interfered with by warring powers. (3) Striking down of trade barriers between nations. (4) Reduction of armaments. (5) Fair adjustment of the claims of the rival nations to possession of disputed colonies. (6) Territory occupied by the Germans during the war (in Russia, Belgium, France, and the Balkan states) to be given up to their own peoples, each nation to be free to develop "institutions of its own choosing." (7) Restoration of Belgium with the payment of war damages. (8) Righting the wrong done in Alsace-Lorraine when those provinces were taken from France by Germany in 1871.

(9) Readjusting the boundaries of Italy on national lines to bring Italian people under Italian rule. (10) Restoration of Serbia, which had been conquered and possessed by Austria.

GENERAL JOHN J. PERSHING 

General Pershing was born in 

Missouri in 1860 and was graduated from West Point Military

Academy in 1886. He saw service in the West against the Indians and also in the Spanish- American War of 1898. He served in the Philippines for four years and was in command of the 

United States troops sent into 

Mexico in pursuit of General

Villa in March, 1916. In 1917 he went to France as head of the

American Expeditionary Forces in the World War.

(13) The establishment of 

Poland as an independent state. 

(14) A League of Nations to promote world peace and to secure the rights and "selfdetermination" of all nations, the small as well as the great. 

American Troops and the

War. At first the American troops remained back of the lines for intensive training in trench fighting. Then they went to the front as small units under their American officers, but were formed into brigades with the more experienced troops of France and Great Britain. The entire American army was under the direct command of

General John J. Pershing, 

Co-operating with the Commander-in-Chief of the Allied forces, Marshal Foch (fösh). 

A good deal of curiosity existed as to how the untried American troops would behave under fire. There was not long to wait. From their first engagement to the

last, the American soldiers and marines carried the fight to the enemy. Their energy knew no bounds. The marines fought with such ferocity that the Germans called them" the Devil Dogs." One of their most notable engagements was in Belleau (bě'lō') Wood, which 

TRENCHES ON THE WESTERN FRONT 

Trench warfare was one of the new features of the World War. Notice the zigzag character of the trenches, the exploding shells, the shell craters, and the barbed wire entanglement in front of the outermost trench. The stretch of ground between the front line of trenches of the opposing armies was called "No Man's Land."

France has since renamed "The Wood of the United 

States Marines." 

Much of the American fighting was done around Chateau-Thierry (shä tō' tyȧ're), St. Mihiel (săn me'yĕl), Metz (měts), and in the forests of Argonne (år gōn'). In the spring of 1918 the Germans began a series of "desperation" drives, bent upon capturing the "channel ports". 

Calais (kǎl'a), Dunkirk (dun'kûrk), and Boulogne (boo lōn') - and upon reaching Paris. At first they met with success, but in July were stopped by the Americans and the French, and their advance was turned into a retreat.

This was the beginning of the end. At the notable defense of Verdun (věr dŭn') in 1916 the French had said, "They shall not pass." The British, French, and Americans now said, "They shall not stop," and the Allied forces, having taken the offensive, never dropped it. 

Under the command of General Foch they struck blow after blow in rapid succession along the entire front, and the enemy was apparently bewildered. 

Finally the Germans, their armies beaten in the field and their spirit weakened, asked for an armistice, a temporary pause in the fighting, to make terms of peace. 

In October, 1918, Germany expressed her willingness to make peace on the basis of President Wilson's "Fourteen Points." The armistice terms were announced by 

General Foch and were promptly accepted by the Germans. On the eleventh day of the eleventh month at eleven o'clock in the forenoon the guns ceased firing and the most terrible war of all history came to a close. 

The Terms of the Armistice. The armistice terms meant a complete surrender by Germany. Her allies- 

Bulgaria, Turkey, and Austria-Hungary—had already been beaten and had given up the fight; revolution was about to break out in Germany, and her armies had been beaten in the field. The Germans, therefore, were compelled to accept whatever terms the Allies saw fit to grant.

Under the terms of the armistice large numbers of guns and immense amounts of ammunition were turned over by the Germans and Austrians to the Allies; airplanes were surrendered; locomotives and cars which had been taken out of Belgium and France were given up; and a large part of the carefully preserved German navy was taken from its hiding place behind the Kiel Canal and handed over to Allied naval officers. The German troops immediately evacuated Allied territory and the

Allied armies took possession of important posts within the German Empire. The purpose of the armistice terms was to make it impossible for the Germans to resume hostilities, a purpose that was fully accomplished. 

The Peace Conference. Arrangements were immediately made for a peace conference to be held in Paris early in 1919. President Wilson, after addressing Congress early in December, 1918, sailed for France. The conference opened January 18, and after the necessary preliminaries, the delegates settled down to the important and difficult task of drawing up the terms of peace. 

So conflicting were the claims of the different nations, however, that almost six months passed before the treaty, embodying a covenant of a League of Nations, was ready. 

The treaty with Germany was signed in the historic palace at Versailles (věr sä'y), near Paris, on June 28, 1919. Americans had been told that the conflict in which they had so loyally engaged was "a war to end war." 

President Wilson, at the Peace Conference, insisted that some agreement among the nations must be made by which such a war might be prevented in the future. He insisted upon and obtained in the treaty a provision for a League of Nations for this purpose. Alsace-Lorraine (ǎl säs' lô ran') was given back to France; Finland was made independent of Russia; Poland was made independent and was given territory that had long since been taken from her by Germany and Russia; Italy's territories were enlarged; the Austro-Hungarian Empire was broken up and some of its provinces were assigned to Czechoslovakia and Yugoslavia, whose independence was recognized; Great 

Britain received additional colonies and "mandates," or control, over certain lands in Asia and Africa. The responsibility for the war was placed upon Germany and her allies. Germany had to surrender her colonies and her navy, reduce her army to 100,000 men, and pay large

sums for the damages she had inflicted. Germany's province of Shantung (shän'toong') in China was surrendered to Japan. The treaty contained hard terms and in time it may need to be revised. 

America received nothing from the war except huge debts, losses, and taxes. She expected nothing.

Although nearly all the nations of the world soon joined the League of Nations, the United States refused to do so, being the only strong nation that has declined membership, except Russia, which has so far not been invited to join. Personal and party opposition to the League of Nations arose in America and the Peace Treaty providing for the League was rejected by the United States 

Senate. Separate treaties with Germany and Austria were made and ratified in 1921.

CHAPTER XXXVI  

Elihu Burritt. Nearly a century ago Elihu Burritt, the "learned blacksmith," entered on a crusade for universal peace and human brotherhood. Burritt had had no chance to go to college; but by reading and study he had mastered many languages. He lectured in America and 

Europe, creating a sentiment for peace. In 1848 he organized the Brussels Congress of Friends of Peace. 

In the history of the peace movement his name deserves to be remembered. He was a pioneer in a great cause. 

Demand for World Peace. Since the World War a demand has arisen in all countries that another calamity of the kind shall never happen again. Differences will arise between nations, some of them serious, but no reason exists why these disputes should be settled by war. 

Disputes between individuals are not settled by duels, although such was the practice years ago; now they are settled in court. . No one has a right to be the sole judge of his own case and to settle a controversy with his neighbor in his own way by force of arms. It should be so with nations.

Old Ways of Avoiding War. Let us notice some of the old ways of keeping the peace among nations and see how the methods have worked in American history. 

1. Diplomacy. The State Department is our Peace 

Department.

Through its ambassadors and agents and 638

treaties, this Department seeks to remove difficulties with other nations as they arise, to bring about understanding, and to cultivate friendly relations with all countries. 

Many quarrels have been settled, or prevented, by forbearance and wise diplomacy.

Sometimes a single treaty makes for lasting peace. 

In 1798-1800 France and America were on the verge of war. Diplomacy and a treaty settled the quarrel, and for over one hundred and thirty years the two republics have lived on friendly terms with each another. In 1846 we were about to go to war with Great Britain over the Oregon boundary. The Oregon treaty of 1846 settled that dispute in fairness to both sides. Nations make treaties not only to end wars but also to avoid wars. 

2. Mediation. If two nations in a dispute cannot reach an agreement, mediation may be used. This involves the friendly aid of a third country that is trusted by both nations in the difficulty. The good offices of a mutual friend may lead to a peaceful settlement satisfactory to both parties. President Roosevelt mediated between Russia and Japan in 1905 and brought about peace between the warring powers. It is always better to try mediation before a war begins. 

3. Conciliation. In the process of conciliation a commission is appointed by the mutual consent of the countries involved in difficulty to investigate the facts leading to the trouble and to suggest a solution. The recommendations of the commission are not binding on either nation, but they are likely to be effective. The nation refusing to accept the solution would appear in an unfavorable light. 

4. Arbitration. This method goes farther than any of the preceding ones. By it a court is formed for the hearing of the dispute. Before this court the evidence and claims of both sides may be brought. The countries

in dispute agree in advance that the award of the court shall be accepted as binding. If the nation which loses the case should break its pledge to abide by the decision, it would be regarded as contemptible by other nations. 

America has always been in favor of arbitration. 

She has urged this way to peace upon other nations. 

We agreed to arbitrate the Alabama claims against Great Britain in 1871 (see page 470) and the differences over the Newfoundland fisheries in 1877. In the first instance, we collected claims amounting to $15,500,000; in the second, we paid $5,500,000. The arbitration awards were probably fair in both cases, although some of the English thought the Alabama award was excessive and unjust. But, as Prime Minister Gladstone said, the cost was but "a drop in the bucket" compared with the expense of a war, and the hatred which war causes. 

In 1892 a treaty of arbitration was made between Britain and the United States to settle a dispute over the capture of seals in Alaskan waters. For a time it seemed that the seal herds would be entirely destroyed, and American authorities had seized a number of British seal-hunting vessels. An American court in Alaska claimed that the Bering sea was a "closed sea," and therefore a part of the territory of Alaska. In this instance the arbitrators decided against America and we had to pay $473,000 damages for the British ships seized. Later an agreement was made between the two countries for the protection of the seals. 99 Again, the Venezuela boundary dispute between America and Britain was settled by arbitration in 1896 and 1897. Once more the decision on the whole went against the United States, but the nation was satisfied and war was honorably avoided.

In 1908 a long standing dispute about the Newfoundland fisheries was also settled by an arbitration treaty

between Great Britain and America. American fishermen were allowed rights on the Newfoundland coast and a commission was established to settle future controversies between Canada and the United States. A number of disputes have since been peaceably settled by this Joint Commission, which is composed of three Canadians and three Americans.

America has made a number of general arbitration treaties with other nations. She made one with France in 1908, which was to run for twenty years, and which was renewed in 1928. In general, such treaties agree that, when differences arise between the United States and another power, the differences shall be submitted to an impartial tribunal. Such an arrangement tends to remove ill feeling and to promote good will and, although all wars may not be prevented in this way, every successful arbitration tends to reduce the probability of conflict. 

The Hague Court. This tribunal is an attempt to provide a regular and permanent means of arbitration. 

The Hague Court is not really a court, but is only a list, or panel, of judges from which arbitrators may be chosen if two nations agree to submit a political dispute to arbitration. Each nation adhering to the Hague 

Court names four persons well informed in international law and of the highest moral reputation for the panel of judges. From the total list so appointed each party to a dispute selects two judges, but may choose only one judge from among the four persons it named for the panel of judges. After the four arbitrators are thus selected, they choose a fifth. If three of the four arbitrators cannot agree upon the fifth, a third power selects him.

The Permanent Court of International Justice. This tribunal is a regular court of law, with a definite body of judges giving their time to the work and receiving

a suitable compensation. The World Court, as the tribunal is commonly called, does not deal with political but with legal questions, such as the meaning or interpretation of a treaty, questions of international law, and the kind of payment that should be made for a violation of an international obligation. The World Court has now been agreed to by more than sixty nations. 

To this court the nations that have joined in recognizing it agree to submit their differences on all subjects that are 66 justiciable"; that is, on all such as can be settled on principles of law, justice, and equity. The purpose is to substitute law for war. 

The United States is not a member of the World Court. 

The Senate agreed to membership in 1926, with certain reservations, but these reservations were not all accepted by all the other nations. Our joining the court was allowed to drop until 1929, when Elihu Root drew up a plan for American membership to which the other nations agreed. Secretary of State Stimson announced that this arrangement was acceptable to the United States, but the Senate has not yet given its approval to the plan, and the United States therefore does not belong to the Court. 

The League of Nations. If a nation should refuse to recognize its obligations and begin a war, could it not be restrained by some combination of nations that wished to keep the peace? This idea led to the League of Nations, proposed by President Wilson at the close of the World War. The chief purpose of the League was to organize some international machinery to preserve world peace-"to promote internal peace and security; to induce nations not to resort to war; to establish open, honorable, and just relations among nations; to induce them to accept the understandings of international law, and to maintain regard for the obligations of treaties.' 99

During the ten years in which the League has been in existence, it has become so important and influential, and America has such important connections with it, that its organization and work need to be briefly explained. 

The League now (1930) has fifty-four member nations.

The non-member nations are Russia, Turkey, Mexico, the United States, and a few minor powers like 

Afghanistan, Egypt, and Hejaz.

The organs of the League are: (1) The Assembly, (2) The Council, (3) The Secretariat. 

The Assembly represents the member nations. It meets annually at Geneva, Switzerland, on the first 

Monday in September. Each nation may have three representatives in the Assembly but only one vote; the nations are therefore equal in power, the little with the big. Any "fully self-governing state, dominion, or colony may be a member, a term which includes such states as Cuba, Canada, and Ireland. The Assembly elects the non-permanent members of the Council; it may discuss any matter touching the peace of the world. 99 The Council is made up of fourteen member nations, five permanent members (Great Britain, France, Germany, Italy, and Japan) and nine members elected annually by the Assembly. In 1930 the nine non-permanent members, who are elected by the Assembly for terms of three years, were Cuba, Finland, Canada, Spain, Venezuela, Persia, Peru, Poland, and Yugoslavia. Three members retire each year.

The Council meets three times a year and special meetings may be called for emergencies. The Council acts as a kind of Executive Committee of the Assembly to handle immediate questions, while the Assembly is like an international forum for free public discussion. 

The Council may take no action without unanimous consent.

The Secretariat. This is the permanent working part of the League. It is steadily active, doing a vast amount of secretarial work, looking after the records and correspondence, collecting information, and registering the business which is to come before the Assembly and the Council.

The League Way for Settling Disputes. The members of the League agree to submit any dispute that may arise either (1) to the World Court, (2) to arbitration, or (3) to the League Council. The dispute may be submitted only by agreement of the parties in dispute, but once a dispute is so referred all members agree to accept the decision and not to go to war with a member accepting the decision. The members agree that in no case will they resort to war until three months after the decision by the arbitrators or the Council. 

The Council may take up a dispute of its own accord. 

Any question likely to endanger the peace of the world may be brought to the attention of the Council by any member state. In 1927 China called the attention of the 

Council to the sending of troops to Shanghai by the British. Parties to a dispute have a right to a seat on the Council while their dispute is being considered, whether they are members of the Council or not. investigation the Council publishes its recommendation. 

If this is agreed to by all the members of the Council (except the parties to the dispute), all members agree not to go to war against any member complying with the decision. After

If any member of the League goes to war in violation of its pledge, it shall be considered as having made war on all the members of the League. It is then subject to the following restraints: 

1. The Public Opinion of the World. This is really stronger than any government.

It will be aroused

against the offending nation, and no nation is likely to defy it or stand against it. Every nation seeks world approval; and no nation can be comfortable under universal condemnation. 

2. The Economic Boycott. Supplies may be cut off and trade restrictions imposed. This is an effective weapon in bringing a war-making nation to peace. No nation can carry on war very long unless it can trade with other nations and get food and supplies. 

3. The Blockade. The war-making nation may have its ports closed to all foreign trade, a form of war that may make fighting with soldiers and guns unnecessary. 

4. Military Pressure-The Use of War Measures. 

This is the last resort, and the use of the other weapons of the League is likely to make war unnecessary. 

The Peace Treaty, providing for the League of Nations, had to be submitted to the Senate for ratification. Here a conflict arose. Wilson had not appointed any member of the Senate to the Peace Commission. 

Henry Cabot Lodge, the chairman of the Senate Committee on Foreign Relations, disagreed with Wilson; so the Senate, after a heated debate, attached certain amendments or reservations to the League covenant which President Wilson refused to accept. Thus a deadlock developed between the President and the Senate, and the Peace Treaty with its provisions for the League of Nations was never ratified. Hence the United 

States did not enter the League, partly from party politics, partly from a desire to preserve complete national independence, and partly from a fear of being entangled in European affairs and alliances.

While the treaty was being discussed in the Senate, President Wilson started on a speech-making tour of the country to arouse public opinion in favor of the treaty. In Kansas he suffered a nervous breakdown.

He was taken back to Washington with an illness from which he never recovered. He filled out his term, WOODROW WILSON 

Woodrow Wilson, 28th President of the United States, was born at Staunton, Virginia, December 

28, 1856; he was graduated from 

Princeton University in 1879; was Professor of History and

Political Economy in Bryn Mawr 

College from 1885 to 1888, and in 

Wesleyan University from 1888 

to 1890. From 1890 to 1902 he was a professor in Princeton University, and from 1902 to 1910 he was President of Princeton. He was elected Governor of New 

Jersey in 1910. On retiring from the presidency he continued to live in Washington, D. C., until his death in February, 1924. although disabled from performing the duties of his office. He died in February, 1924. 

The Geneva Protocol: 

"Aggressive War." The 

League Covenant does not outlaw war, but places reliance mainly on delay, investigation, publicity, and public opinion. If arbitration is refused or if the Council cannot agree unanimously, war may be recognized as legitimate. This provision has been regarded as one of the objections to the League.

In 1924 the Geneva Proto col (a draft of a treaty), agreed to by the chief European nations, took a further step in an attempt to outlaw aggressive war.

An aggres- sor was defined as a nation which refused to submit a dispute to peaceful settlement and took up arms to enforce its claims. Under the Geneva

Protocol each state agreed to submit every dispute to arbitration, to accept the decision, and to unite in war against a disgraceful aggressor. Great Britain withdrew its support of the Protocol and the arrangement failed of adoption. This disappointed public opinion

and four years later the Government in England was turned out of power, partly on this account. 56 

The Locarno (lō cär'nō) Conference. In October, 1925, a famous conference of European nations was held at 

Locarno, Switzerland. It met to consider affairs in

Europe where security is the key to peace, where the nations must be made to feel safe before they can be induced to lay down their arms. A Treaty of Mutual 

Guarantee" was made by which Germany, Belgium,

France, Great Britain, and Italy guarantee the present boundaries between Germany and Belgium and between 

Germany and France. They agreed that German territory for about thirty miles east of the Rhine should have no military forces; and that no forts, arsenals, soldiers, military stores, and means of war are to exist on this frontier. Germany, Belgium, and France mutually agreed not to resort to war with one another, except in defense against a violation of the agreement, or for a cause authorized by the League. If a violation of the agreement occurs, Great Britain and Italy agreed to use arms against the violator. Separate arbitration treaties were signed by Germany and France. 99 In the past every war has been "defensive from the point of view of those beginning it. Locarno defined the aggressor as that nation which, having agreed to settle differences by arbitration and justice, proceeds to attack another nation without fulfilling its agreement. 

The Locarno agreement was hailed as a happy omen for the peace of Europe. The nations had made a beginning toward peace. For the first time in a thousand years, France and Germany pledged themselves not to attack one another. Briand (bre än'), the French 

Minister for Foreign Affairs, welcomed Germany to membership in the League in an eloquent address before the Assembly of the League of Nations (1926). He

expressed the hope that the world would substitute the process of peace for bloody conflicts.

Peace for Germany and for France! That means that we have done with the long series of terrible and bloody conflicts which have stained the pages of history; done with the black veils of mourning for sufferings that can never be appeased; done with war; done with brutal and bloody methods of settling our disputes. True, differences between us still exist, but henceforth it will be for the judge to declare the law. Just as individual citizens take their difficulties to be settled by a magistrate, so shall we bring ours to be settled by pacific procedure. Away with rifles, machine guns, cannon! Clear the way for conciliation, arbitration, peace!

Outlawing War: the Paris Peace Pact. On April 6, 

1927, on the tenth anniversary of America's entrance into the World War, Briand, ten times Prime Minister of France, and then her Minister of Foreign Affairs, made a declaration to the people of the United States that France was ready to enter into a treaty with us that would for all time outlaw war between the two nations. 

In responding to France, Kellogg, the American Secretary of State, proposed that a treaty renouncing war should apply not only as between France and America, but that such a treaty should be extended to all the nations of the world. Secretary Kellogg said that

America was ready to give up war, and he proposed a "multilateral treaty" for the other nations to consider: 

The high contracting parties agree that the settlement of all disputes and conflicts which may arise among them, of whatever nature or of whatever origin they may be, shall never be settled except by peaceful means. 

This is the language of the treaty for renouncing war. 

More than fifty nations of the world have now signed it.

At a gathering in the White House in July, 1929, President Hoover, in the presence of Secretary Kellogg and ex-President Coolidge, solemnly proclaimed the Peace 

Pact outlawing war to be the law of America and of the world. The Peace Pact does not mean that conflicts and 

EWING GALLOWAY THE INTERNATIONAL PEACE BRIDGE 

Commemorating more than one hundred years of peace between the United 

States and Canada. 

disputes will cease to arise among nations, but that when they do arise they shall be settled by the machinery and agencies of peace. 

Disarmament. Are the nations sincere and do they mean what they say when they promise in solemn treaty to abandon war and to settle all disputes by peaceful means? If so, they can begin to lay down their arms.

A splendid example of security by disarmament is found in the Rush-Bagot Treaty of 1818 between Great Britain and America-the treaty by which the Great

Lakes were dedicated to international friendship, disarmament, and lasting peace. 

In 1818 Sir Charles Bagot for Great Britain and Richard Rush for America made a treaty which excluded warships from the Great Lakes and thus secured lasting peace between two great nations. Thus more than one hundred and twelve years ago Great Britain and the United States provided that on a frontier stretching between the two nations for more than 3000 miles not a fort nor a soldier, not a gun nor a war vessel, was to be stationed. There has been no need of any for the treaty has never been violated. The "International Peace 

Bridge," spanning the Niagara River at Buffalo, was officially opened and dedicated in August, 1927, in commemoration of more than a hundred years of peace thus secured between the United States and Canada. 

The Washington Disarmament Conference. It was with reduced armaments in view that President Harding called a conference of several of the powers engaged in the late war-Great Britain, France, Italy, Japan, China, Belgium, the Netherlands, and Portugal-to meet at 

Washington on Armistice Day, November 11, 1921. 

Charles E. Hughes, the American Secretary of State, in opening the conference astonished the delegates and the country by proposing a drastic cut in the chief navies of the world. He proposed that the United States immediately scrap thirty war vessels, half of them new ships; that 

Great Britain destroy nineteen battleships, and Japan seventeen. He also proposed a "naval holiday" for ten years the building of no new battleships in that time. 

He further proposed that the relative strength of the chief navies in "capital ships" (battleships) be as 5 for Great Britain, 5 for the United States, 3 for Japan, and 1.6 for France and Italy. These proposals were agreed to and put into a treaty.

A "Four Power Treaty" was also agreed to at the Conference, by which Great Britain, France, Japan, and the United States agreed to respect one another's rights and possessions in the islands of the Pacific; if danger of war threatened, these nations were to meet in conference and decide on the best course of action. There had been a military alliance between Great Britain and Japan and this was now dissolved, much to the relief of the United States. 

By the "Nine Power" treaty China agreed to give fair treatment to all on her railways, and the other powers promised to respect the sovereignty of China and to recognize the principle of the "open door" equal trade privileges in China for all. The door was "nailed open. "The Washington Conference did much to advance world peace, though its reduction in naval forces applied only to battleships. In cruisers, submarines, airplane carriers, and other craft for naval warfare, the nations were still left open to competition. now

The London Disarmament Conference. The next important step in promoting international peace grew out of a visit to President Hoover (October, 1929) made by Ramsay MacDonald, the leader of the British Labor

Party and the Prime Minister of Great Britain. 

MacDonald was a friend of peace. He had stood for peace in the midst of war and he and other members of his Cabinet had gone to jail for their convictions during the World War. In Hoover he met a Quaker engineer, who had done much to relieve the suffering of war and to feed starving peoples. The two statesmen in quiet retirement on the banks of the Rapidan talked over the problems in which the two nations were interested. They both wanted reduction of naval armament. 

Influenced by the failure of a conference held in 1927 at Geneva, Congress had authorized, early in 1929, the

construction of fifteen new and powerful war cruisers, with the purpose of building up America's sea power until it equalled that of Great Britain. MacDonald's visit and his public addresses made an excellent impression 

WIDE WORLD PHOTOS AMERICAN DELEGATES WITH RAMSAY MACDONALD AT THE LONDON DISARMAMENT CONFERENCE 

Seated, from left to right: Charles Francis Adams (Secretary of the Navy),

Charles G. Dawes (Ambassador to Great Britain), Ramsay MacDonald 

(Prime Minister of Great Britain), Henry L. Stimson (Secretary of State). 

Standing, from left to right: Dwight W. Morrow (Ambassador to Mexico), Senator David A. Reed, Senator Joseph T. Robinson, Hugh Gibson 

(Ambassador to Belgium). 

upon the American people, and he was hailed as a messenger of peace and good will between the two Englishspeaking peoples.

As an outcome of MacDonald's visit and the mutual understanding that developed, an international conference of the naval powers was called to meet in London, January 21, 1930. In his Armistice Day address in

1929 President Hoover said: "We will reduce our naval strength in proportion to any other. It only remains for the others to say how low they will go; it cannot be too low for us.' 99

The London conference adjourned without making much progress toward disarmament. America, Great 

Britain, and Japan agreed to scrap a few battleships and to build no more vessels of the kind before 1936. It was agreed that America may build up her navy to to a "parity" with that of Great Britain, at a cost of nearly a billion dollars in war cruisers in ten years. It is left for 

America to determine whether she will spend this vast amount of money on war cruisers. Another conference promised by 1936 may make this unnecessary. It was also agreed that submarine warfare is to be "humanized." The conference may be said to mark a small step toward world peace, but the nations have not yet made any great progress toward disarmament. Another conference met at Geneva, Switzerland, in 1932, but also accomplished little towards promoting peace.
