PART NINE THE NEW DEMOCRACY

THE NEW DEMOCRACY 

Shortly after the Spanish-American war Theodore Roosevelt became President of the United States. He was young and energetic, and was eager to reform the abuses which had crept into the conduct of business. 

He waged vigorous warfare against the practices of many of the large corporations, declaring that great wealth must obey the law as strictly as is required of the humblest citizen.

The prosperity of the country during Roosevelt's administration resulted in marked improvements in the living conditions of the workers. Roosevelt became the leader in saving the nation's forests and protecting its other natural resources. He considered the digging of the Panama Canal his greatest achievement. 

Under the next strong President, Woodrow Wilson, the reforms begun by Roosevelt were carried forward. 

The income tax transferred a burden of taxation from those least able to bear it to the shoulders of people of means. Banks were made more responsive to Government control, and the Federal Reserve System lessened, if it did not end, the danger of financial panics. 

But unfortunately the end of the World War brought hard times. Many of the returned soldiers could not find work, and the prices of all products fell. 

The President was also confronted by troublesome problems among the small nations of the Caribbean, and had to interfere several times to maintain peace. 

Many of the nation's leaders had long felt that government was not sufficiently responsive to the will of the people. They also believed that citizens should take more interest in political questions. Methods were therefore devised whereby the people themselves could suggest or reject laws. In addition the right to vote was given to women. Thus the people and their government were brought closer together and a new democracy" came into being. 66

THE CAPITOL AT WASHINGTON, D. C. 

1700

PART NINE THE NEW DEMOCRACY CHAPTER XXXVII  Death of McKinley: The Coming of Roosevelt. Presi dent McKinley had served barely six months of his second term when he was shot by an assassin while attending the Pan-American Exposition at Buffalo. He died eight days later, September 14, 1901, and Theodore Roosevelt became President of the United States. 

When Roosevelt took the oath of office as President he promised, as Vice Presidents in such situations usually do, to carry out the policy of his predecessor. But the nation soon saw that a new kind of leader had come into the White House. Roosevelt was a man of great force and energy, bubbling over with eagerness and enthusiasm to use the opportunities before him for the service of the country. 

Roosevelt was only forty-three years old—the youngest man in our history to hold the office of President. 

His critics called him rash and impulsive, needing the wisdom that comes with years and experience. He had not wished to be Vice President, for he looked upon that office as a political graveyard where there was little chance to do anything. He had many of the masterful qualities of Jackson, and as there had been a period of history marked by Jackson's influence, there came a period in which Roosevelt was the leading force. 

Roosevelt's View of the Presidency. Roosevelt had convictions on public policies.

He did not intend to 661

"wait on Congress." He proposed to use all the powers of his office to bring about the things which he thought 

THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

Theodore Roosevelt was born in

New York City, October 27, 1858. 

He was graduated at Harvard University; while a young man became active in politics; was elected to the 

New York legislature; was appointed by President Harrison as Civil Serv- 

66 

ice Commissioner and by President 

McKinley as Assistant Secretary of the Navy. During the Spanish 

War he became Colonel of the Rough Riders." After the war, in 

1898, he was elected Governor of New York. In 1900 he was elected 

Vice President. By the death of McKinley Roosevelt became Presi dent, and was an important force in politics until his death in 1919. 66 ought to be done. In deciding what he had the power to do, he thought it unnecessary to find a grant of power" in the Constitution permitting him to act, but claimed that if his action or policy was not forbidden by the Constitution he had the right to go ahead. This was a shock to many people who thought such a construction of the Constitution would give the President too much power. But since the policies which Roosevelt wished to carry out were favored by most of the people, the country was ready to accept his view of presidential powers. 

Roosevelt was also a good politician. He knew how to play the game of politics, how to manage men or defeat them. He had an unusual understanding of all classes and conditions of men among his countrymen. He knew how to make news and appeal to the public through his messages and the newspapers. Whenever opposed

in Congress, Roosevelt obtained, or forced, the co-operation of that body by sending Congress repeated messages, which were really appeals to the country to back him up. As a result, people all over the country sent word to their Congressmen, urging them to "stand by the President" and follow his lead. He had said, Speak softly but carry a big stick "; that is, never pick a quarrel, but be ready to fight and fight hard if you must. 

He now used the "big stick" on Congress, public sentiment being the "big stick." 66 By such methods Roosevelt controlled the policies of the country for the next eight years. Let us notice his outstanding policies or achievements. 

1. The Coal Strike. Roosevelt settled the coal strike in 1902. Society cannot well exist unless certain basic industries, like mining and transportation, keep going.

If coal mining ceases and the railroads stop running, the factories will have to close their doors because of lack of fuel and shipping facilities. Every one depends on such industries, and there is always uneasiness when the coal miners and the operators cannot agree lest a strike occur and our factories and homes be left without fuel. 

The coal strike of 1902 lasted throughout the spring, summer, and fall. The price of hard coal (anthracite) went as high as $30 a ton. Winter was coming on, industries were closing, people were suffering. The managers were trying to starve the coal miners out. The strike had now become a matter of national concern. The president of the Miners Union was John Mitchell, an honest and able labor leader.

Roosevelt called the operators and representatives of the miners to meet him in a conference at the White House. The spokesman for the operators thought the trouble could all be ended if the Government would only send troops and keep the miners in order. Mitchell, the

leader of the mine workers, proposed arbitration and pledged the workmen to accept its results, but the operators refused this offer.

Then Roosevelt, backed by public opinion, demanded a settlement. He let it be known that the people must have coal and that if the operators would not agree to reason- 

MIRAFLORES LOCKS, PANAMA CANAL 

able terms the Government would take charge of the mines and put the miners to work. When Roosevelt's intentions became known, the coal owners came to terms. 

The people endorsed Roosevelt's course and his action increased his popularity. 

2. The Panama Canal. Roosevelt brought about the building of the Panama Canal. This was one of the most notable achievements in the history of the world. You will remember that the great seamen in the age of discovery, men like Columbus, Magellan, Hudson, and Cabot, were always seeking a passage to the "South Sea," or the Pacific Ocean. The quest was in vain, for no satisfactory passage was ever found. Now, under Roosevelt, a passage was made; that is, a passage or canal was dug

across the narrow Isthmus of Panama, large enough to permit the largest ships in the world to pass from ocean to ocean. Today nearly 6000 vessels go through the canal every year, and the sea voyage from New York to California, or from New York to Asia, is shortened by thousands of miles.

The need of the canal was strikingly shown during the 

Spanish War (1898) when the American battleship Oregon was compelled to make a voyage of 14,000 miles from 

San Francisco around Cape Horn in order to join the

American fleet in the West Indies. The incident convinced the people of the United States that the canal should be dug to promote our national interests by making sea communication more rapid between our Atlantic and Pacific coasts.

In the first place the United States had to secure the consent of Great Britain to build the canal. In 1850 we had made a treaty with that country, known as the Clayton-Bulwer Treaty, by which it was agreed that neither country should have control over an isthmian canal or build fortifications along it, but that it should remain open equally to all nations. At that time British sea power was much greater than America's. As Great Britain had islands and interests in the West Indies, we were afraid the English would seek to build and control the canal. The Clayton-Bulwer Treaty warded off that danger. 

In 1869 the Suez Canal was opened. Then England had a shorter sea route to the East, and she cared less about the western route. After the Civil War and following the Geneva arbitration (see page 470) a friendlier feeling arose between Great Britain and the United States. Great Britain was willing for America to build the canal. A new treaty was therefore made, the Hay- 

Pauncefote (pôns'foot) Treaty, in 1901, by which Great Britain consented that the United States might build and

control the Panama Canal. England thus recognized 

American supremacy in this region. 

A second difficulty arose from early efforts to build an isthmian canal. In 1881 Ferdinand de Lesseps, a Frenchman who had built the Suez Canal. organized a French 

ABANDONED FRENCH DREDGE AT PANAMA CANAL 

Note the stagnant water in the foreground. When the Americans took over the work, the swamps and marshes were drained or sprayed with oil. The houses for the men were doubly screened so as to be mosquito-proof. company to construct a canal at Panama. After spending millions of dollars and losing thousands of lives by disease, the French gave up in despair. But the French company still had property rights in the undertaking. 

After long negotiations the United States bought out the rights of the French company for $40,000,000, as that was thought to be cheaper than to build an entirely new canal. 

Many difficulties had to be overcome before the canal could be built. The greatest difficulty of all, perhaps, arose from the ownership of the Isthmus. Panama

belonged to Colombia, one of the countries of South America. A treaty was negotiated with that country granting the United States permanent control for canal purposes of a strip of land six miles wide extending across the Isthmus. Ten million dollars were to be paid to Colombia, and an annual rental of $250,000 for the lease of the land. This treaty was ratified by the United 

States, but was rejected by Colombia, chiefly because the 

Colombian authorities wanted more money. Roosevelt was determined that the "South American politicians should not hold up Uncle Sam." He said that he did not propose to have any further dealings with "the foolish corruptionists" in Colombia; but added that we could "not permit them to block this great work." 

Then something happened in Panama. The people of the Isthmus wanted the canal built. It would mean prosperity to them as millions would be spent on the enterprise. Aroused by the rejection of the treaty by the 

Colombian Senate, therefore, the people of Panama revolted and set up a little republic of their own. President Roosevelt promptly recognized the independence of Panama, and sent naval forces to the Isthmus to prevent Colombia from interfering. Many Americans thought his action was high-handed and unfair to Colombia. 

Panama next concluded a treaty with the United States granting this country the right to control the Canal Zone and to build and operate a canal. When this treaty was ratified in 1904, President Roosevelt appointed the first 

Canal Commission. 

Yellow Fever in the Canal Zone. Another serious obstacle was still to be overcome. Workmen could not easily live in the vicinity of Panama. Yellow fever, malaria, and other diseases caused their deaths at an alarming rate. This was the principal cause of the failure of the French to dig the canal. A medical commission

under the leadership of Dr. W. C. Gorgas, a colonel in the United States Army, almost worked miracles in the Canal Zone, and " the Isthmus was made one of the most healthful regions in the tropics." Malaria was checked

OUNDERWOOD & UNDERWOOD 

and yellow fever was stamped out by the destruction of the mosquito which carried the deadly germ. The triumph of sanitation under Colonel Gorgas made possible the triumph of engineering that followed.

After slow progress in the work for several years, President Roosevelt appointed 

Colonel G. W. Goethals (gō'

thǎlz), an army engineer, to direct the forces in digging the canal, and soon the dirt began to fly. Engines and dredges, steam shovels and locomotives, with thousands of workmen of many races pushed the work forward. 

Great locks had to be built; hills had to be removed and land slides prevented; but in spite of all such difficulties the work was carried to a successful conclusion at a cost of about $400,000,000. In 

1913 the canal, 50 miles from end to end, was opened to ships, and the dream of centuries was realized by a water route connecting the Atlantic with the Pacific. To celebrate the opening of the canal a great Industrial Exposition was held in San Francisco in the summer of 1915. 

GEN. WILLIAM C. GORGAS 

Chief medical officer at Havana under General Wood, following the Spanish War; son of a Confederate soldier; colonel in the 

Medical Corps of the United 

States Army; chief sanitary offi cer of the Panama Canal Zone he made of the Isthmus a place in which workmen could live. A great representative of preventive medicine, a great servant of humanity.

Colombia continued to complain over the loss of Panama and in 1921 the United States paid her $25,000,000 to settle her claims and to bring about a better feeling toward America. 

3. Conservation of National Resources: Irrigation. 

Another worthy policy with which Roosevelt became identified was that of conservation; that is, saving the natural resources of the nation. His work in this connection is looked upon by many people as Roosevelt's greatest service to his country.

There were vast desert areas in the West. If water could be brought to them, they could be turned into fruitful farms and gardens. Big dams might be built to store water from the rivers and mountain streams. By big and little ditches this water could be spread out over the dry land. This has been done. Great reservoirs, dams, aqueducts, and canals have been built, and millions of acres of sandy wastes that supported only sage brush and cactus, lizards, coyotes, and jack rabbits, have been turned into fertile fields and gardens. As a result rich and valuable crops of fruits, potatoes, sugar beets, and alfalfa have been produced. 

National Forests: Gifford Pinchot (pin'shō). More over, the mountain sides were being robbed of their valuable forests. America had been wasteful of her resources. Fires were destroying great forests of splendid trees, and the spring rains were washing tons of rich soil down to the sea. Gifford Pinchot, head of the

Bureau of Forestry under Roosevelt, sought to educate the public on the need of irrigation for dry lands and on the importance of saving the natural resources of the nation-minerals, forests, water power, and oil. 

President Roosevelt supported Pinchot with all the weight of his influence. Senator Newlands, of Nevada, who knew the West, urged the cause in the Senate.

Many public-spirited citizens gave their aid and the result was the passage of the "Reclamation Act"in 1902. The irrigation of desert lands was the purpose of the act. It provided for a "revolving fund "; the money paid by those who bought the public lands and by the users of water was to go into a fund to build more dams and supply more water. These new projects in turn would produce more land buyers and water users, and their money too would go into new plants for irrigation. Thus the fund would keep revolving and growing. 

The Roosevelt Dam: Forest Rangers. The Roosevelt 

Dam was completed in 1911, and within twenty years nearly 2,000,000 acres of waste lands were reclaimed for cultivation. In 1907 President Roosevelt issued a proclamation reserving over 40,000,000 acres of national forest lands. A body of men called forest rangers were stationed in the forest reserves to protect the forests from fire and from timber thieves. The rangers are stationed on high peaks from which they can detect the smoke of a fire at great distances; since telephones connect the stations, word can be sent quickly to any point of danger. In this way destructive fires are held in check. Wasteful cutting is not allowed, and where timber has been destroyed the land is replanted. 

4. The Portsmouth Peace Conference. Roosevelt brought about the Peace Conference at Portsmouth, N. H., which ended the war between Russia and Japan (1905). Roosevelt knew that both warring powers wanted peace, but were not willing to ask for it. He was ready to use his friendly offices to bring them together. 

He also arranged a conference of European powers which prevented for a time a war between Germany and France. 

For these services to the world and the cause of peace, Roosevelt was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize, consisting of a medal and diploma, and a sum of over $36,000.

He

gave the money to a committee to be used in promoting the cause of industrial peace in America in the struggle between capital and labor. 

THE ROOSEVELT DAM 

In the Arizona plateau stands the famous Roosevelt Dam, built at a cost of over $4,000,000. It is one of the highest dams in the world, being 280 feet high. "It can store so much flood water that the rains of 1930 will water the crops of 1932 on hundreds of thousands of acres of land many miles down stream. Its creation is one of the wonderful stories of western development. The road to the site was built through a waterless desert for twenty miles. Then for forty miles the road was blasted through rock walls of canyons at a cost of millions. Hundreds of thousands of barrels of cement needed for the work were made by a plant built for the purpose. 

A hydro-electric plant was built to furnish power to quarry, lift, and haul the cement and the materials for the dam. A nineteen-mile cement-lined canal brought water to the plant. For months 3000 people worked on the enterprise. They left a real dam, a structure of solid concrete that can stand as long as the mountains stand." J. Russell Smith, North America. 

5. Pure Food Laws. Along with the plans for saving the natural resources and enriching waste lands came laws for safeguarding the food and medicine of the people.

Manufacturers of food products-meats, soups, lard, canned and bottled goods-often used chemical material to preserve these foods. People began to demand pure food laws to prevent the use of harmful preservatives in the food, and to have the labels on the goods tell the truth. Patent-medicine makers sold poisonous drugs as medicine under false labels. In these "medicines" they used opium, morphine, cocaine, laudanum, and alcohol. 

In 1906 Upton Sinclair's novel, The Jungle, described the fearful conditions in the meat-packing establishments in Chicago. When President Roosevelt read this book, he was stirred to action. He recommended two laws that were passed. One provided for Government inspection of meats sold in interstate commerce to make sure that these meats were obtained from healthy animals and that they were slaughtered under clean and healthy conditions. The other act forbade the manufacture or sale of adulterated, poisonous, or harmful foods, drugs, and medicines. The ingredients must be mentioned on the labels. In 1911 the law was made stronger by forbidding false statements to be made about the wonderful cures that could be had from these medicines. "Quack patent medicines and "quack" doctors were nearly driven out of business. One of the valiant public servants in the fight for pure foods was Dr. Harvey W. 

Wiley, a chemist in the Department of Agriculture. 

The Election of 1904. Roosevelt's policies, for the most part, were popular with the country. He naturally desired to be re-elected in 1904 and be President" in his own right," but up to this time no Vice President who had inherited the presidency had ever been elected to succeed himself. Roosevelt became the exception. 66 Although Roosevelt always claimed to be a good party man," the party managers and the machine politicians did not like him. They thought that he was too

radical, and that he stood for too many reforms. They disliked his independence, and they were afraid they 66 could not "" manage "' him.

Accordingly the party bosses and organization leaders looked about for a candidate to oppose 

Roosevelt with a view to defeating him for renomination. Their best hope was the old party captain, Marcus 

A. Hanna, then a Senator from Ohio, who had twice carried the party to victory under 

McKinley. But in 

February, 1904, Hanna died and the plans of the anti-Roosevelt politicians were dropped. 

The result was that 

Roosevelt was nominated by the Republicans in 1904 without opposition and was elected by the largest popular majority any 

President had ever received up to that time.

The Democrats nomi- UNDERWOOD & UNDERWOOD HARVEY W. WILEY 

Wiley was born near Madison, Indiana, in 

1844; graduated from Hanover College, Indiana, at the age of 22; graduated at 

Indiana Medical College (Indianapolis) four years later, and at Harvard in 1873 (B.S.). The next year, when Purdue University, La Fayette, Indiana, was opened,

Dr. Wiley became its first professor of chemistry and later he was State Chemist of Indiana. He did much to educate the public in demanding pure food laws. Dr. 

Wiley died in 1930 at the age of 87. 

He belonged nated Judge Alton B. Parker of New York. to the conservative, anti-silver, and anti-Bryan wing of the party. Thus the country witnessed the strange spectacle of a conservative successor to Bryan running

against a radical successor to McKinley. This time the radical won, because a great many Bryan Democrats voted for Roosevelt since they liked his democratic ways and his "progressive" policies. Roosevelt carried every State in the Union outside of the "Solid South," which since the days of reconstruction had always gone Democratic no matter who was the candidate. On this occasion Missouri broke away from the South and voted for Roosevelt, the first time the State had voted for a 

Republican President since 1868. 

Roosevelt Policies. Roosevelt's second administration was identified with certain policies which came to be called "Roosevelt policies," although they were favored by the Bryan Democrats as well as by the Roosevelt Republicans. The most important of these follow: (1) Government regulation and control of public service corporations, especially the railways, which must be made to serve all equally. Backed by Western farmers and farm journals, the Hepburn Railway Rate Law was pushed through Congress (1906). This act increased the 

Interstate Commerce Commission from five members to seven. It prohibited "rebates" and gave to the Commission (see page 532) the power to fix rates and to regulate railway accounts. It was also given authority to regulate express companies, sleeping car companies, and pipe lines. The conservatives did not like this "meddling with business "; they claimed that "a man should be allowed to manage his own affairs in his own way." Roosevelt was willing that the railroads have a square deal," but he wanted them to treat all patrons alike and to serve the public welfare. 66 (2) The development of waterways. A system of inland waterways, dredging of the rivers, and connecting the rivers by a series of canals for shipping slow freight was advocated.

(3) The conservation movement-saving to the publie, under national authority, the natural resources of the country: water power for irrigation, the forests, the mines, and ungranted homesteads-was continued. 66 66 Roosevelt also showed his sympathy with the common man. He favored the income tax. He preached “ civic righteousness" throughout the country. He announced that he was for a " square deal" to all classes. He was friendly to labor unions. He spoke for workmen's compensation laws to provide payment to laborers injured at their tasks. He sought to make the big corporations obey the law, denouncing "lawless wealth" on the one hand and "dangerous labor leaders" on the other.

Upon his recommendation Congress created a Department of Commerce in 1903.

Election of 1908. By the election of 1908 Roosevelt had placed the Republican Party in a position of great strength with the country. Many of the policies which 

Bryan had previously favored had been adopted by 

Roosevelt. Free silver was the chief exception.

President Roosevelt refused to be a candidate for reelection. Many of his supporters wished him to stand for a second" elective term," but he had announced that he would not be a candidate for a "third term " because he considered that he had already had two terms. He was ready to step aside, and threw his support to 

William Howard Taft, his Secretary of War. His support was decisive in bringing about Taft's nomination and election. As Jefferson had chosen Madison for his successor and Jackson had chosen Van Buren, so Roosevelt brought Taft to the presidency. He assured the country that Taft could be trusted to carry out the "Roosevelt policies." The Democrats for the third time nominated William J. Bryan, their "peerless leader."

The issues between the old parties, the tariff and the

trusts, aroused little popular interest. The Republicans promised to revise the tariff if they were returned to power. Taft was elected by a decisive vote. 

Taft and a Divided Party. Taft became President on 

March 4, 1909. Roosevelt, meanwhile, left Washington

WILLIAM H. TAFT 

Taft was born in Cincinnati in 

1857 and was graduated from

Yale in 1878. He was appointed 

United States District Judge in 1892. He was the first Civil Gov ernor of the Philippine Islands and later Secretary of War.

After retiring from the presidency he became a professor of law in 

Yale University and President of The League to Enforce Peace. 

President Harding appointed him

Chief Justice of the United States 

Supreme Court in 1921. He died in 1930. and started on a big game hunt in Africa. Taft soon had a divided party on his hands. He called a special session of Congress to revise the tariff and on August 5, 1909, the Payne-Aldrich Act was passed. People generally had understood that the promise of the Republicans for a revised tariff had meant a revision downward, but this act was a revision upward. 

Taft did not try to lead Congress, but merely waited for the act to be passed. He believed that the President should not interfere with the work of Congress; that he should leave Congress alone to do its work in its own way; that he might accept or reject what was submitted to him, but that Congress should be independent of the Executive. He thus gave up presidential leadership and 

Congress took the reins. 

The result was a tariff act that was very favorable to the "interests" and the big manufacturers. The act was unpopular in the West, and many Republican Congressmen refused to support the measure. The

President not only signed the bill, but a month later at Winona, Minnesota, called it "the best tariff law that had ever been enacted in the history of the country." 

The Payne-Aldrich Act contributed to the break in the Republican Party. Congress contained a group known as "Insurgents," later called "Progressives," such as

Senator La Follette of Wisconsin, Senator Dolliver of 

Iowa, and Senator Beveridge of Indiana. Some of these men had urged a downward revision of the tariff for years. Speaker Cannon of the House of Representatives said that the party should" stand pat " for protection on the old lines. The conservatives who supported the new tariff were therefore called "stand-patters. 99 President Taft tried to "keep in the middle of the road," that is, to act fairly toward both factions and, if possible, to reconcile them. With this in view he recommended a number of measures-that tariff rates be submitted to an expert Tariff Commission, that there should be equality of privileges in trade with Canada, and that international difficulties should be adjusted by arbitration treaties. His suggestions were not carried out, and other difficulties arose. 

According to Roosevelt, Taft had agreed to retain in his Cabinet James R. Garfield, Roosevelt's Secretary of the Interior. This department has charge of conservation. Instead of appointing Garfield, however, Taft selected R. A. Ballinger, of the State of Washington, who had been an attorney of certain big corporations interested in acquiring Western lands. Gifford Pinchot, head of the Forest Service, the friend of Roosevelt and an Insurgent sympathizer, accused Ballinger of allowing the public domain to be raided by granting to a rich corporation valuable coal lands in Alaska. The Pinchot- 

Ballinger controversy soon became a political issue. Taft stood by his Secretary and dismissed Pinchot from office.

The Progressives and ardent friends of conservation then declared that Taft had lined up with the stand-patters and that he had no intention of carrying out Roosevelt's policies.

Thousands of the rank and file of the Republican Party, influenced by the progressive leaders in Congress, had come to believe that the only changes made by the Payne- Aldrich Act were in the interest of great manufacturers and corporations; that "Sugar Senators," "Lumber 

Senators,"" Steel Senators," and "Railroad Senators," were in control of Congress, and were making laws in favor of the "interests"; and that the President, a good-natured man surrounded by men who knew what they wanted," was yielding to their leadership. As the election of 1912 drew near, the Progressives denounced Taft's administration and began to organize to defeat his renomination. 66 The Election of 1912. And now developed another strange situation in politics. The radical Roosevelt forces, calling themselves "Progressive Republicans,' who had brought about Taft's nomination in 1908, now bitterly opposed him and persuaded Roosevelt to be their candidate. The conservatives, called "stand-patters," who had disliked Roosevelt and had fought Taft's nomination in 1908, now rallied to the President's support. 

Thus the Republican Party was divided in 1912, and as a result, Woodrow Wilson, the Democratic candidate, was elected.

Wilson as Governor of New Jersey had shown himself in sympathy with progressive policies and against the control of the party by the "interests." His nomination, therefore, caused the progressive Democrats to stay with their party.

The combined popular vote for Roosevelt and Taft was over 1,000,000 more than the vote for Wilson. Including

the vote for Debs, the Socialist candidate (897,000), and for Chapin, the Prohibitionist candidate (258,000), the popular majority against Wilson was about 2,400,000. 

Thus the division in the Republican Party made Wilson's election possible, just as the division in the Democratic Party in 1860 had made Lincoln's election possible.

3. Mention the main achievements of Roosevelt's administration. Which do you regard as the greatest? Why? 

4. Explain the reasons the United States wanted to build the 

Panama Canal. Name the obstacles that lay in the way. Tell how we obtained the right to go ahead with the enterprise. 

5. What is meant by conservation? Point out the connection between the events described in Chapter XXIX and the work of conservation related in this chapter.

Roosevelt's administration. Which was the most important?

CHAPTER XXXVIII  

Wilson and the Presidency. On March 4, 1913, Woodrow Wilson became President of the United States. He represented the "scholar in politics." His experience in the political field had not been extensive-only a few years as Governor of New Jersey. But he had been a student of politics all his life. He had written a book on 

Congressional Government and another on The State. 

He had spoken for "the New Freedom," urging that the people exercise more control and activity in government, and that they be set free from restrictions by monopolies and special interests. As Governor of New Jersey he had successfully grappled with the special interests and had opposed the party bosses. 

Wilson had supported most of the progressive measures which had become identified with the names of 

Roosevelt and Bryan. He held Roosevelt's view of the presidency rather than Taft's; that is, he believed that the President should take the lead in determining public policies. He believed that Congress and the President should act together under presidential leadership; that they would not be able to accomplish much if they tried to be entirely independent and separate from one another. 

For the first time in forty years the Democrats had a leader with a united party behind him. They had a large majority in the House, but only a slender one in the 682

66 Senate; consequently, they needed to act together. Wilson, who was a forward-looking man,” wished his party members in Congress to follow his leadership in supporting progressive measures, and for the most part his wish. was realized.

Tariff Reform: The President's Address to Congress. 

The tariff was first taken up. President Wilson called an extra session of Congress, and urged that the tariff be promptly revised downward. He revived an old custom of addressing Congress in person. Washington and 

John Adams had done this, the members of Senate and House marching in procession to the hall of the House of 

Representatives, where the President, mounting the

Speaker's platform, read his message to the members. 

It was a stately occasion. Jefferson thought it was too royal, like a speech from the throne. He was a democrat and wanted" democratic simplicity," and he discontinued this custom partly for the reason mentioned and partly because he was not a good speaker, but was a good writer. Accordingly Jefferson sent a written message to 

Congress, instead of addressing that body in person, and his custom was followed by all succeeding Presidents until Wilson's administration. Wilson thought that he' could impress his ideas on Congress with greater effect by delivering his message in person.

Congress responded to his recommendation on the tariff by passing the Underwood Act six months later. This measure took the place of the Payne-Aldrich Act and provided much lower rates. One of the important features of the Underwood Act was a provision for an income tax. 

The tax was graduated by placing a small rate of tax on small incomes and a higher rate on larger incomes. 

Incomes up to a certain amount were exempt, or no tax at all was imposed; an unmarried person having an income of only $1000 or a married person with only $2500

did not have to pay an income tax. These amounts have since been increased to $1500 for unmarried persons and $3500 for married persons. 

The Federal Reserve Act: Preventing Money Panics. 

For more than a half a century, as we have seen (pages 569-586), the money question had been a subject of bitter

The Twelve' Federal Reserve Districts of the 

United States with their Banking Centers FLA. 

The Federal Reserve banking centers were distributed over the United States in proportion to the banking business of the various localities. In this way the financial needs of the different parts of the country are met more fully than under the old system. controversy. The struggle was chiefly between the creditors and the debtors, or the lenders and the borrowers. 

Men of all parties had come to believe that the banking system and the money system of the country needed reform. This feeling led to the passage of the Federal 

Reserve Act (December 23, 1913). Under this measure twelve cities have been chosen for the location of Federal Reserve Banks. The local banks within convenient distance of these banks become members (stockholders) of

their respective Federal Reserve Banks. Each Federal 

Reserve Bank provides a kind of federation of banks, and does a banking business only with the member banks, not with individuals. The Federal Reserve Board, which controls the whole system of Regional Banks, consists of the Secretary of the Treasury, the Comptroller of the Currency, and five members appointed by the President with the consent of the Senate. When the Federal Board thinks best it may authorize the Regional Banks to issue "Federal Reserve Notes "to be used as money; the notes are then sent to the local member banks to be issued, or lent, to customers across the bank counter. These notes are good money. Receivable by all banks, and for taxes, customs, duties, and other public dues they were formerly redeemable in gold on demand at the Treasury of the United States or at any of the Federal Reserve Banks. 

National bank notes of any local national bank are secured by Government bonds which the bank owns and which are deposited in the United States Treasury. 

Federal Reserve notes are secured by all the assets, or property, of the bank, such as promissory notes given . to the bank by merchants, manufacturers, and farmers. 

The Government's guarantee makes both kinds of notes as good as gold. But the national bank notes were increased or diminished as the interests of the banks demanded. As population grew and trade increased, more currency was needed but no way existed by which the currency in the country could be increased unless more gold was discovered, or unless the banks bought more Government bonds to entitle them to issue more notes. That was often unprofitable to the banks as the bonds drew a low rate of interest and the banks could lend their money at a much higher rate. 

The consequence was that the currency was rigid, neither increasing nor diminishing with the demands of

trade. The Federal Reserve System is designed to cor. rect this evil and to provide an elastic or flexible currency—a currency that will stretch out or draw in as business needs require, giving the country more currency at one time and less at others in answer to demands. 99 The Federal Reserve notes help to provide this "emergency currency. They are furnished by a Government agency through local banks in all parts of the country, and the notes when loaned out to borrowers with good credit will enable the country to avoid panics. 

A money panic comes from lack of credit and loss of confidence; or when the demand for money is greater than the supply. People are then seized with fear lest the money they have loaned, or deposited in a bank, cannot be had when it is wanted. A "run on the bank" is started, people draw out their deposits, and hide their money away. The banks are then unable to make loans and have to call in those they have made. As a result, people who have to meet their obligations and pay their debts in money suffer foreclosures of mortgages and property sales under the hammer of the auctioneer. This compels them to sacrifice their property often at less than its real value. The result is loss or ruin to thousands of people who could have paid their debts without the loss of their property or business, if time (a loan or credit) had been allowed to them. When emergency currency is available, as in the Federal Reserve System, the wheels of industry are kept in motion and men who can give security are able to get an extension of time or credit and are not forced to close up their businesses or sell their property.

One of the main purposes of the Federal Reserve System is to prevent panics. People used to think that a money, panic with all its loss and ruin was to be expected at certain intervals, say every twenty years. There was

a disastrous panic in 1819, another in 1837, another in 1857, another in 1873, the worst of all in 1893, and one in 1907. These panics caused the loss of homes and untold misery. All of them could perhaps have been prevented if the Government had exercised proper control over the supply of money. The Federal Reserve System, which is under Government control, can supply emergency currency; by distributing this currency (Federal Reserve notes) through the Regional Banks, good money in sufficient quantities can be provided wherever it is most needed.

The Deflation of 1920-21. Hard times or good times may depend on the policy of the Federal Reserve Board in controlling the money supply. Contraction of the currency and a sudden fall in prices will cause hard times. 

During the World War billions of Government bonds were issued. These were used as security for borrowing money. Federal Reserve notes were in circulation, more gold was produced, money was plentiful and "easy" to borrow. The currency of the country was greatly increased, up to $58 per capita, more than the Populists had ever demanded. Prices rose very high. Wheat sold for $2.20 a bushel and Iowa land brought from $200 to $400 an acre. The times were prosperous. 

The Federal Reserve Board thought there was too much speculation and began to reduce the currency. In two years (1920-21) a thousand millions of dollars were withdrawn from circulation; the member banks could not get more Reserve notes to lend and were required to pay back those they had received. Therefore the local banks could not lend money so easily and in many cases they had to call in money they had loaned. Farmers and business men could not get a renewal of their promissory notes held by the banks. The result was a great slump in prices. Western lands went down to half their former

value, and the prices of farm products fell. Many farmers could not pay their obligations at the banks, their lands had to be sold at ruinous prices to pay their mortgages, and many rural banks in the West had to close their doors. The farmers were especially hard hit; they claimed that if a policy of "deflation" were needed it should have been more gradual and should have been extended over more time. 

The Federal Farm Land Bank. The farmer needs long-time credit when he has to borrow money. At the local bank he has to pay (or renew his note) within ninety days or six months. If the bank will not renew the note, the farmer may have to sell his property at a sacrifice. He needs to be able to borrow for three or five years at a time on a farm mortgage. As a rule banks do not lend on farm mortgages, because such mortgages may be hard to sell and the bank's funds should be in a form that can easily be turned into cash. If its funds were tied up in mortgages, the bank might not be able to pay its depositors.

To remedy this situation, Congress passed the Federal Farm Land Bank Act in 1916. This system of Farm 

Banks is like the Federal Reserve System. The country is divided into twelve districts, each having a Federal 

Farm Land Bank. A Federal Farm Loan Board is in control. Farm Loan Associations, organized in each district, are made up of farmers who desire to borrow money on the security of farm mortgages. The Farm Land 

Bank, on these mortgages as security, issues Land Bank bonds which it sells to the public. With the money received for the bonds it buys more farm mortgages. 

Thus loanable money is made available to the farmers and at a low rate of interest. The Federal Land Bank 

System offers credit to the farmers as the Federal Reserve System does to manufacturers and merchants.

The Federal Trade Commission Act and the Clayton 

Anti-Trust Act, 1914. Urged by President Wilson, Congress passed the Clayton Anti-Trust Act and the Federal Trade Commission Act, to prevent abuses by the trusts and unfair trade practices by corporations. The Federal 

Trade Commission has been helpful in enabling business men and the Government to get on well together, the Government helping to guide business by giving information and letting business enterprises know what they may do and what they may not do under the law. 

Wilson and Mexico. Before President Wilson came into office, a revolution broke out in Mexico (1911) which drove the President, General Diaz (dē'äs), out of the country. Diaz had ruled Mexico for forty years like an absolute monarch. He had not shown much consideration for the welfare of the common people. His successor, Madero (mä thā'rō), had hardly entered the presidency before he was assassinated. General Huerta (wĕr'tä), an ambitious military leader, then became dictator. In these successive disorders and revolutions many 

American citizens in Mexico lost their lives, and many more lost their property. Mines, oil wells, plantations, and other investments and business enterprises, many of which were owned by Americans, were seized or destroyed.

Events in Mexico finally caused Wilson to send an expedition to Vera Cruz to safeguard American interests in the city. He tried to induce Huerta to arrange for a peaceful election in Mexico to choose a President, and not to be a candidate himself. When Huerta refused, Wilson warned Americans to leave Mexico and adopted a policy of "watchful waiting.' He refused to recognize Huerta as a lawful ruler.

This policy finally drove Huerta from power. Carranza (cả rän´så) succeeded him as President of Mexico,

in 1914, but was not able to preserve order or prevent wrongs against Americans. One of the Mexican outlaws named Villa (vēl'yȧ), with a band of followers, crossed the American border and killed a number of Americans at Columbus, New Mexico. President Wilson immediately sent troops to the Mexican border and ordered General Pershing to pursue Villa and punish him. General 

Pershing led his soldiers for a hundred miles into Mexico, but was unable to capture Villa.

Wilson did not wish to go to war with Mexico, but he had committed acts, such as the invasion of Mexico and the bombardment of Vera Cruz, which were much like acts of war and which aroused suspicion and distrust against the United States in Mexico and among the Latin- American powers. Conditions remained much the same in Mexico for several years. Finally, Carranza was murdered (1920) and Obregon (ō'brẻ gōn) became the President of Mexico. Three years later the United States recognized the Obregon government. 

The Caribbean: Haiti and Santo Domingo. Disorder had broken out also in Santo Domingo (sän'tō do min'gō) and Haiti (ha'ti). These two countries occupy one island, Haiti the western part, Santo Domingo the eastern part. The island lies between Cuba, an American protectorate, and Porto Rico, an American possession.

It also lies on the direct route from Europe to the Panama Canal. Naturally the United States would not like to have the island fall into the hands of any European power. Both Haiti and Santo Domingo were nominally independent, but there were almost constant disturbances within the two countries.

In 1905 the finances of Santo Domingo were in bad shape. Either she could not, or would not, pay her debts to European claimants, and for a while it seemed that European Governments might intervene and perhaps

seize the island. In this emergency the United States made a treaty with the Dominican Republic, by which the United States agreed to take charge of the revenues of the island republic and help it to get its debts paid. This action was satisfactory to the European nations and prevented them from trying to collect their debts by force.

Several years later came a new revolution (the island had twenty revolutions in thirty years). Such was the situation when President Wilson came into office. He ordered American marines into the island to restore order and to supervise the elections. The outcome was that Santo Domingo became practically a protectorate of the United States.

Similar action occurred in Haiti. In 1915 a revolution occurred in the island. After American marines restored order, Haiti made a treaty with America by which the United States was to control the police forces of that country and manage its finances. Haiti, too, thus became a protectorate of America and our marines are still in the island (1930). Such developments make it clear that the 

United States has become the dominant power in the 

Caribbean Sea. 

President Hoover and Haiti. President Hoover did not wish to impose the authority of the United States upon these island republics. He desired them to be orderly and self-governing. As dissatisfaction with American control and disorder continued in Haiti, he appointed a commission to investigate with the result that a fair election of a Haitian legislature was provided to elect a new President. The Americans promised to withdraw their marines and leave the Haitians to govern themselves as soon as possible, certainly by 1936.

1917, the Danish West Indies (now called the Virgin

Islands) came into the possession of the United States by purchase from Denmark for $25,000,000. The islands contained 33,000 people, nine-tenths of whom were Negroes engaged in the production of sugar. The inhabi- tants were allowed to retain their Danish citizenship, 20 16Havana 80° 72° 68 64°

THE VIRGIN ISLANDS 

60° Notice the strategic location of the three islands (St. Thomas, St. John, and 

St. Croix), recently purchased by the United States from Denmark. In case of war on the American continents the possession of the Panama Canal would be exceedingly important. These islands are important outposts for its defense. or to become citizens of the United States as they chose. 

For military and naval reasons, the islands are important. They serve as a coaling station for naval vessels and help to protect the entrance to the Panama Canal. 

The Election of 1916. The administration of Presi dent Wilson was generally approved by the country, and he was very popular with his party, which nominated.

him for re-election in 1916. As his opponent, the Republicans nominated Charles Evans Hughes of New York.

Hughes had gained favor with the country by his investigations of the scandals of the big insurance companies. 

These companies had used the money of their policyholders for party contributions and to corrupt the New 

York State legislature—a situation that Hughes helped to bring to an end. Shortly afterwards, he was elected 

Governor of New York and administered that office in the interest of the people. When he was nominated for 

President, he was serving as a Justice of the Supreme Court of the United States, having been appointed to the position by President Taft. Hughes resigned from the Supreme Court to accept the nomination. 

In this campaign the Progressives called on ex-President Roosevelt again to be their leader, as in 1912. He declined the nomination, however, and announced that he would support Hughes. He called upon all Republican Progressives to help reunite the party in an effort to defeat President Wilson. The Progressive Party then practically disappeared, most of its members following Roosevelt in support of Hughes, but many of them, especially in the West, voted for President Wilson. 

Wilson was re-elected in one of the closest elections in forty years (since 1876). The result turned on the vote of California, whose Progressive leader was Governor 

Hiram Johnson, at that time a candidate for United

States Senate on both the Republican and Progressive tickets. The Progressives came to the conclusion that 

Hughes was in sympathy with the "stand-pat " "' wing of the party and was not at all interested in the Progressives of the State; as a result Hughes lost the State to Wilson by a small majority while Johnson was elected to the Senate by 300,000 majority. Wilson also carried 

Kansas and other Western Progressive States.

99 President Wilson's Efforts for Peace. Wilson was inaugurated for a second term on March 5, 1917. "He kept us out of war was a slogan used by the Democratic Party in supporting Wilson for re-election. He had served only about a month of his second term when he led his country into war. He was a man of peace and had done what he could to bring about peace in Europe and to keep his country out of the World War. 

But his peace efforts were in vain. Germany revived her submarine policy of sinking merchant ships. This, as we have seen (pages 611-616), led to the loss of American lives and finally brought the United States into the great conflict.

CHAPTER XXXIX  Stages of Democratic Advance. The United States has steadily grown more democratic since the beginning of our national history. The era of Jefferson, of Jackson, and of Lincoln each made its advance toward a more popular control of the Government. Jefferson probably did more than any one else to make the United States a democracy. Fully 90% of the people were farmers in those days, and Jefferson led the farming masses against the banking and commercial classes. After Jefferson's triumph the States gradually extended the franchise to men without property. Jackson represented the "plain people"; he was backed by the western democracy of farmers. Lincoln emancipated the slaves, and they were soon made citizens and voters. The doctrine of the 

Declaration of Independence that "all men are created equal "in regard to their legal and political rights was now to apply to all men alike," regardless of race, color, or previous condition of servitude."

Again, a generation later, in 1896 and following, another advance took place. The growth of the People's 

Party and its demands, the democratic principles and policies advocated by Bryan and Roosevelt promoted this movement. The people seemed determined that 

America should not be ruled by a plutocracy, that is, by a combination of great wealth. 697

To achieve, or retain, government of, by, and for the people requires a constant struggle. A review of some of the achievements for popular government during the last fifty years will show how true this statement is. 

The Australian Ballot. For years party bosses and rich men who wanted special favors from the Government had corrupted the ballot and bought elections. The "floater "a man who waited around on election day for his vote to be bought-had become an important factor in politics. In the States where the parties were evenly matched, " floaters "sometimes held the "balance of power," and the party managers who could raise the largest slush funds " for buying votes generally carried such States. 66 This situation made necessary a change in the method of voting, if the people were actually to rule. Up to this time (1888-1890) political parties, or candidates, printed their own ballots, or tickets. Since the Democratic ballot might be white and the Republican ballot red, it was easy to distinguish them from one another. 

The party workers distributed these ballots on the streets or at the voting places, and it was easy to buy and sell votes under the plan, for there was no secrecy about voting. After paying the "floater," the buyer gave him the right ballot and went with him to the voting place to see that he handed the ballot to the election officer. The buyer could thus make sure that the voter "stayed bought."

But in 1888 Kentucky and Massachusetts began a ballot reform; several other States followed the next year. 

These States introduced what is called the "Australian ballot," because it was first used in that country. Under this system of voting the names of all the candidates of all the parties are printed on an "official" ballot furnished by the State. No one is allowed to have one of

these ballots outside of the election room. When the voter goes in to vote, he is given a ballot by an election officer. He then enters a booth by himself, and secretly marks the ballot for the candidates for whom he wishes to vote. He next folds the ballot, comes out of the booth, hands his ballot to the election officer, and sees it deposited in the ballot box. If any one has bribed him to vote a certain way or frightened him by threats, it is not easy to determine that he has voted as he said he would. Since 1888 nearly all the States have adopted the 

Australian ballot. The use of this ballot has done much to purify elections.

The Direct Primary. For more than sixty years following Jackson's time, nominations for public office were made by party conventions. There were city conventions, county conventions, State and national conventions. A convention was a gathering of delegates chosen at a local meeting by party workers or by such of the voters as had time and interest enough to attend. 

Party bosses and workers generally controlled the choice of delegates. Counties sent delegates to a State convention; States and congressional districts sent delegates to a national convention. Persons who gave their time to politics-the "professional politicians" and the office holders-usually controlled the conventions. Only a few citizens gave much thought or attention to politics until an election campaign was on, and by that time the candidates had already been selected. The party bosses handled the conventions and rich candidates sometimes bought enough delegates to secure nominations to office. 

The foregoing situation created a demand that the primary system be substituted for the convention plan. 

Under the direct primary system every voter of the party has an opportunity to vote for the candidate he prefers. 

This gives more power to the rank and file of the party.

Candidates announce themselves for various offices, their names are placed upon official tickets, and members of the various parties cast their ballots upon the day appointed for the primary election, just as in a regular election. As a rule the candidates receiving the highest number of votes for each office become the nominees of the party. The primaries of all parties are held on the same day and all have the same legal safeguard against fraud as the regular election. In some States, if no candidates for Governor or United States Senator receives a majority (more than half of all the votes cast), but only a plurality (or the greater number) a second primary is held to let the voters choose between the two candidates receiving the largest number of votes.

The direct primary is merely another election and adds to public expenses. Many people claim that the bosses and regular party workers manage the primaries as completely as they managed the conventions and that only rich men can afford to run for a State Governorship or a United States Senatorship, since men without wealth cannot afford two contests, the one in the primary and the one for the regular election. But large sums of money need not be spent in the primaries or in the elections. While rich candidates occasionally spend large sums, many persons have been nominated in the primaries and elected without great expenditures. Campaign expenses must be filed by the candidates and some men chosen for the Senate have been deprived of their seats because of having spent too much. A wide difference of opinion exists concerning the merits and defects of the primary system. 

In 1912 thirteen States had some form of presidential primaries. The voters in these States had opportunity to express their preference among the presidential candidates. In 1913 William J. Bryan expressed the opinion

that the Democratic Party would never hold another national convention but that the people in the primaries would decide upon candidates and policies; President 

Wilson urged legislation upon Congress to bring that situation about by law. But Bryan's prophecy has not come to pass, and presidential primaries have not been used much since 1912, partly because in the presidential years there have been no real contests within the parties. 

Initiative and Referendum. Along with the direct primary came the initiative and the referendum. These are political devices for adding to the power of the people. They were first used in South Dakota in 1898, and soon after were provided for in the constitution of Oregon. The two measures generally go together. 

They are intended as a check, or remedy, for the shortcomings of legislatures. They came from the feeling that legislators were not true to the pledges they made in order to secure election; that legislators, instead of being representative," were "misrepresentative "; that they were too often controlled by powerful lobbies, corporations, and corrupt methods; that they frequently passed laws which the people did not want and refused to pass laws which the people did want. 66 The initiative is a process for obtaining legislation which the people desire, in spite of the legislature. The referendum is a process of preventing legislation which the people do not desire. By the initiative, if a certain per cent of the voters petition to have a measure or policy submitted to the voters for approval, the legislature must provide for a popular vote upon it. If the measure carries in the election then the legislature must pass the act. By the referendum (meaning it must be referred) on a similar petition, a measure which the legislature has passed may not become a law until it has been submitted to the people and approved by them.

In this way the people can prevent legislation they do not want.

These measures bring direct government to the people, or enable them to make laws directly. In Oregon sixtyfour laws were passed in this way in four general elections. This system of legislation has not been used much in recent years, perhaps because legislatures have behaved better. The initiative and referendum have been compared to the policeman's club or the gun behind the door- to be used only in an emergency.

Amendments to the Constitution. The growing power of the people brought about several amendments to the national Constitution since 1910. This document had not been changed for over a hundred years, except during the Civil War period when eleven of the States were out of their usual relation to the Union. It was then that the three war amendments" were adopted fifty years later, with increased power in the hands of the people, several amendments which had been urged for years passed Congress and were ratified by the 

States in quick succession. 66 But

The first of these (the 16th Amendment) allows Congress to levy an income tax, a tax upon the income or profit of an individual or corporation, without apportioning the tax among the States according to population. 

This amendment became part of the Constitution in 1913. 

An income tax act was passed during the Civil War and the Supreme Court allowed it to stand. Another was passed in 1894, but the next year (1895) the Supreme Court (by a vote of five to four) declared the law unconstitutional. The decision was very unpopular and a movement was at once begun to obtain an amendment to the Constitution authorizing such a tax, but nearly twenty years were required to bring it about, so great was the opposition of the wealthy class.

Popular Election of Senators. Another amendment of this period (the 17th) provides for the election of United States Senators by direct vote of the people, not by the legislatures of the States as heretofore. For years the Senate was criticized as a "rich man's club," many of whose members represented special interests and corporations rather than the people. The Senators held back the Income Tax Amendment again and again. 

Many people thought that the election of the Senators by popular vote would make the Senate more responsive to the people and more ready to carry out the popular will. The Populists and the Socialists long declared for this change and in later years Republican and Democratic platforms also endorsed the amendment. 66 99 

Suffrage for Women. Another amendment (the 19th) provides for woman suffrage. It declares that "the right of citizens of the United States to vote shall not be denied nor abridged on account of sex. Thus the women, too, came to be included in “ equal rights for all." 

Heretofore they had been citizens, but not voters. The amendment allowing them to vote throughout the United States became a part of the Constitution in 1920. 

Women in all the States voted for President that year. 

Women sought the suffrage just after the Civil War when the vote was conferred upon the freedmen. Many people thought that the women of the land were as competent to vote as the Negroes just out of slavery. But the idea of women voting and taking part in politics met only with the contempt and ridicule of most men, and of many women, too. This famous amendment was drawn up in 1875 by Susan B. Anthony, a pioneer of the woman suffrage movement in America. It was introduced into the United States Senate three years later, and was presented to one Congress after another, until, in 1919, it was passed by Congress and ratified by the States.

A hundred years ago, during the early years of the abolition and labor movements, Robert Owen, Frances 

Wright, Abby Kelly, William Lloyd Garrison, Lucretia

Mott, and other advanced reformers, advocated woman suffrage for America. Organized work began with the 

UNDERWOOD & UNSERVO SUSAN B. ANTHONY Woman's Rights Convention at Seneca Falls, New York, in 1848. In those early days the right to vote was not the only right women needed to contend for. In some States a woman had no right to make a contract, to own property, or to have the guardianship of her own children.

When a man and a woman married, they became one and "the man was the one. If the wife inherited property from her father, the inheritance belonged to the husband who could do as he pleased with it, without any consent from her. Robert Dale 

Owen, son of Robert Owen, brought about a change in the laws of Indiana (1847-1850) for which women have built monuments to his memory. Other States gradually changed their laws, giving women more privileges. 

In 1869 the National Woman Suffrage Association was organized under the leadership of Susan B. Anthony, Elizabeth Stanton, Lucy Stone, and Julia Ward Howe, and later the work was led by Anna Howard Shaw, Carrie 

Chapman Catt, and Jane Addams.

Deciding who should vote had always belonged to the States each State reached its own decision. But it has been decided that the nation may limit the "rights of the States" in such fundamental matters. Originally

66 99 it was the right of a State to permit slavery, or to deny citizenship and civil rights to some of its people; that is, the right to hold, inherit, or bequeath property, to make contracts, or to appeal to the courts for justice. These seem to be fundamental rights of the citizen. The right "of the State to deny them was taken away by the 66

Amendments have further abridged "States' rights" by forbidding any State to impose undemocratic discriminations in the suffrage. 

Formerly a woman's citizenship in America depended upon her husband. She was a citizen of her

husband's country. His naturalization naturalized her. 

If he lost his citizenship, she lost hers. If an American woman married an alien, she lost her citizenship. Now under the Cable Act (September 22, 1922) an American woman, if she marries an alien, retains her citizenship

States instead of five. 

But she must go through certain forms, obtain a certificate of arrival in America, and apply for her "second papers. 991 

During this period of growing democracy, a number of measures which the people had long desired were established.

ALEX Wengell ARCHIPELAGO OCEAN ALASKA 

The United States purchased Alaska from Russia in 1867 for $7,200,000. 

The region contains 570,000 square miles. Most people of that time thought that it consisted of rocks and icebergs and was of no value; but its fish and seals, and later its gold from the Klondike, have yielded many times the sum paid for the territory. carries parcels for the people, the charges varying according to the distance as fixed by a zone system. The parcel post has proved very useful and popular. Free rural delivery of the mail has also been extended to the country districts from time to time.

Two New States. During this period local self-government, the system America has always lived by, was extended to two Western States, New Mexico and Arizona. 

For more than fifty years after their acquisition from Mexico, statehood had been denied to them. Now both

territories were made into States, New Mexico in 1911 and Arizona in 1912. Arizona was the last of the territories in what is called "Continental United States."

Alaska is on the continent, but it is separated from the other States. Any new States in the future will have to be created out of Alaska or the island possessions.

PART TEN THE NATION OF TODAY

THE NATION OF TODAY 

If it were possible for a Revolutionary soldier to revisit the United States today, the scene would be almost as strange to him as life on a different planet. 

He would find his countrymen using automobiles, telephones, telegraphs, and airplanes; their homes offering the conveniences of running water, electric lights, and furnace heat; their daily newspaper keeping them in touch with the happenings of the whole world. He would conclude that a common laborer of today lives in greater luxury than did the kings of old. 

Another advance that he would notice is the establishment of common schools, high schools, and universities, supported by the State for all the children of all the people, girls as well as boys, colored as well as white, foreign as well as native. All of these great changes have been reflected in our literature, for it offers a faithful picture of American life yesterday and today.

The United States, however, has not solved all of its problems. Many vexing questions remain for the present generation and for those that are to come afterward. Among these problems are the following: 

How can unemployment be avoided and poverty prevented? Should the nation continue to admit immigrants from foreign countries, or should the resources of America be kept for Americans and their descendants? How can friendly relations be established with the Latin-American countries and with the other nations of the world?

The older generations have not been able to solve all of these problems. You are receiving an education in order to be prepared to help in their solution.

PERHATIVANA BALLRO HARNESSING THE AIRPLANE TO THE IRON HORSE

PART TEN THE NATION OF TODAY CHAPTER XL  The Hard Ways of Living Seventy Years Ago. At the close of the Civil War (1865) there were few household conveniences and comforts such as we know today. The great majority of the people still lived in the country or in small villages. The hand loom was used for weaving rag carpets and coarse clothing. Men wore blue jeans, and the women wore calico or linsey-woolsey, a mixture of linen and wool of their own carding and spinning. Wood from the forests was the chief fuel in the open fireplace or the wood-burning stoves, which came later. Baking in the Dutch oven or in pans over the hot coals and cooking in pots on the open fireplace were common practices. 

Bathrooms were unknown to common people. Water for household use was carried from the spring under the hill at some distance from the house. Soft water was caught in a barrel under a pipe. The more expensive houses had cisterns or deep wells from which the water was forced by a chain pump or was drawn up in "the old oaken bucket." There were no refrigerators and no ice in summer; the cellar was the only cooling place.

For household lighting there was still the tallow candle, whose wick required" snuffers," or the little hand lamp with its wick soaking in whale oil, a poor light which 715

made reading difficult. The kerosene lamps which later were introduced were ill-smelling and required frequent filling. They were followed by gas jets and gas lamps. 66 Transportation and communication were difficult. It was long before the days of good roads. There were a few turnpikes" with toll gates at which the traveler had to stop and pay according to the length of his journey and the size of his vehicle. There was no rural delivery by the postoffice. Most farmers lived isolated lives. 

Let us now review the changes of the last seventy years.

Our attention comes to an age of machinery and an age of wonders.

TRANSPORTATION 

Let us notice first the main changes in our means of transportation. We have already noticed the progress of our railroads. (See pages 420-424.)

When the dynamo and electric motor were developed, the street car lines in our cities were electrified and the old horse car disappeared. Trolley lines connecting distant cities were run by electric power and became a common mode of travel. Milk, poultry, and livestock were sent from the farms to the cities and much heavy freight was transported over the trolley lines. Within short distances these lines became competitors of the regular railroads.

The Automobile. Soon the trolley lines felt the competition of the automobile and the motor truck. 

No one can say for certain who invented the first automobile. It is the work of many men and many minds. 

It is an assembling of parts more than an outright invention. Almost all its parts had been in use before. The first automobiles were made in France in 1891, though America has done more in the production and use of automobiles than any other country.

The buggy auto, made by Charies E. Duryea, of Massachusetts, was the first gasoline automobile to run successfully in America, in 1892. In 1894 

Elwood Haynes, of Kokomo, Indiana, built a "horseless carriage," which he ran on the highway near his home town at about eight or ten miles an hour.

In 1895 an auto race among these early machines took place in Chicago, in which Duryea's car was the winner. 

Henry Ford was eager to see the race, but he did not have money enough for his railroad fare. 

At that time Ford was a young mechanic working in a power-house in 

HENRY FORD 

Detroit. He had always been interested in machinery, in threshers, binders, and mowing machines. Later he devised an automobile which he sold at a low price in great numbers.

Almost five years passed by after the first cars appeared before the manufacture of automobiles became an important industry. Fewer than 4000 cars were made in 1899. Then in ten years (1909) 130,000 cars were

made in a single year.

In 1919 the number made in a year had risen to 2,000,000. Ten years later automobilemaking had become the third among the industries of America in the value of its products. Over 5,500,000 cars were produced in a year, and over 22,000,000 cars were in use in America. Since there were only about 25,000,- 

000 families in America the figures mean that there was 

THE FIRST FORD CAR 

nearly one automobile to the family. Not every family has a car, but many families have two or three or more. There is now one car for every seven people in America, while in France there are 93 people to every car. If all the cars in America were lined up, bumper to bumper, the line would reach more than twice around the world.

Influence of the Automobile. What effect has the automobile had on American life? (1) People travel more widely, and more easily. They drive across the continent, to California, to Florida. 

People in the North go South and those in the South go North. If they could have done that as easily in the days before the Civil War, that conflict might not have happened. People go longer distances to visit, to trade, and to attend church or entertainments. (2) Better roads have come into use. 

The whole highway system of hard-surface roads has been the direct result of the automobile. If people are to travel, they must have good roads to travel on. The States and the 

National Government have voted millions of dollars to """" Mohawk build" Lincoln Highways, """" Oregon Trails,

Trails," and "Dixie Highways" to and fro across the continent. (3) On these good roads the motor bus and the motor truck carry passengers and freight. Travelers may go by bus, cheaper than by rail, from New York to Los Angeles; the number of people using bus transportation 

EWING GALLOWAY A CORNER IN A MODERN AUTOMOBILE PLANT 

for short distances is enormous. Trucks carry household goods, livestock, and other freight long distances; in many places auto traffic has put electric trolley lines out of business, and has reduced the income of the railroads. (4) The use of the automobile has made living conditions easier and more pleasant for millions of farmers. 

They can get about the farm and go to town much more rapidly. The tractor has taken the place of the old-time plow, especially in large-scale farming. (5) More employment is given to labor. More than a million laborers are engaged in the automobile industry.

One out of every ten persons gainfully employed in America is now deriving a livelihood from the automobile or its allied lines of work. (6) The automobile has changed the appearance of our cities. Many former residence streets have been given

Traffic policemen and a signal system of "stop" and " go signs have become necessary. One must drive according to the rule of the road." He cannot drive just where and when and as fast as he pleases. 66 (7) In spite of legal precautions and " safety first " campaigns, automobiles cause the death of thousands of persons every year. It is estimated that more than 30,000 people were killed in America by automobiles in 1929. 

This is more than were killed in any battle of the Civil

War, and more than were killed in both the Mexican War and the War of 1812 combined. 

Advance in Aviation. Man has always wished to fly and conquer the air. He has not attached wings to his body, but he has made a flying machine by which he can go through the air 4 very rapidly and he can "loop the loop," or do tricks in the air, no bird would that no attempt. 

The problem of flight was solved by the development of a lightweight " power plant" which the airplane could carry. 

The pressure of the air against the wings of the planes causes 

A SPERRY SEARCHLIGHT BEING OPERATED IN CONNECTION WITH AN ANTI-AIRCRAFT BATTERY 

the machine to rise, and the steering apparatus enables the pilot to turn and guide the machine as he pleases. 

An American scientist, Samuel P. Langley of the

Smithsonian Institution, was the first to indicate by his experiments that man might navigate the air. As early as 1896, one of Langley's machines sustained itself in the air for one minute and twenty seconds, flying more than a mile. Langley died in 1906, before he had succeeded in making flight practical, but his pioneer work pointed the way. Two other Americans, the Wright brothers, brought the flying machine to practical success. The first successful flight of an airplane was made by Orville and Wilbur Wright at Kitty Hawk, N. C., December 17, 1903. The first flight lasted for twelve seconds; two years later a flight was made that lasted half an hour.

Improvements in aviation have steadily continued. 

Men have flown to both the poles and over the Atlantic and
