Two Heroes of the Air. In May, 1927, Charles A. Lind bergh in the monoplane The Spirit of St. Louis made a successful transatlantic flight from New York direct to

Paris in thirty-three hours. His feat was the more remarkable because he flew alone. What would our ancestors have thought of Lindbergh's flight to Paris?

They would have been equally surprised to learn that this flyer could telephone greetings from Paris to his mother in Detroit, and that millions throughout the land

Soon after Lindbergh's transatlantic flight, Commander 

Richard E. Byrd, who had made the first flight to the

Late in 1929 Commander Byrd flew over the South 

Pole, accompanied by three companions. From their camp in the Far South the fur-clad flyers took wing amid white clouds and snow. For half the journey they flew only 500 feet high; then they had to climb 10,000 feet between the walls of a glacier.

The expedition was a thrilling adventure, but it was more than that, for the trip was undertaken for scientific reasons. The geologist of the expedition, Dr. Lawrence 

Gould, of the University of Michigan, who remained at the earth's level, traveled 1200 miles by dog sled. He found that what was supposed to be a continent of land was a wide sea. Dr. 

Gould climbed Mt. Nansen and at 6000 feet of altitude found a sandstone formation with a layer of coal, showing that in ages past there must have been vegetation in the region. 

JAME ST WHITE &CO. 

ALEXANDER GRAHAM BELL COMMUNICATION: 

THE TELEPHONE 

We turn now to see the ways by which men have learned to communicate with one another. The telegraph has already been described. (See page 424.) The telephone is an even greater marvel. This instrument first came into use in 1876, when 

Alexander Graham Bell received a patent for the invention from the United States.

At first people did not grasp the importance of the telephone. In 1876 a Centennial Exposition to celebrate the one hundredth anniversary of American independence was held in Philadelphia. People came from all over the world to see the marvelous exhibits. Bell had his telephone on exhibition, but it was in an out-of-the-way place and did not attract much attention. Among the visitors was the Emperor of Brazil, however, and one of the first

things that he wanted to see was Bell's telephone, of which he had heard. On several occasions the Emperor talked over the experimental wire that had been set up for the entertainment and enlightenment of visitors. The interest of this foreign ruler in the telephone attracted much attention to the invention.

From then on its progress in the country was rapid. 

Following Bell, many workers and inventors improved the telephone.

A DUP OF THE TELEPHONE 15 INSTRUMENT THROUGH SPEECH SOUNDS WERE TRANS MITTED ELECTRICALLY. 1875. 

THE FIRST TELEPHONE 

At first the instrument reached only from one house to another. It became necessary to increase the number of 

A CENTRAL TELEPHONE OFFICE IN NEW YORK CITY

people who could use the " phone." Provision was made for leading all wires to some central place called "the exchange, "" or "central." Then any user of the telephone could call

AND 6% OF

THE WORLD'S POPULATION) HAS 60% OF THE WORLD'S TELEPHONES 

"central" and be connected with any other user. 

Next came long-dis tance telephoning. By 

1892 Chicago could talk to New York. In 

1915, less than forty years after Bell's invention was first used, a network of telephone wires had spread out over the continent and a man in New York could talk to a man in San Francisco. An outstanding event in the realm of the telephone came in 1927 with the opening of the transatlantic telephone service. Then New York could talk to London. 

The wireless (1894–1896), brought into use by Marconi (mär kō'ni), an Italian inventor, made possible the sending of telegrams or telephone messages long distances through the air without connecting wires. By 1925 the human voice could be heard half way round the world. 

Then came television, by which a man may not only talk to a person at a distance but actually see the one to whom he is talking. This was demonstrated in 1927 in 

New York, when a resident of the city sat at a telephone and not only talked with Herbert Hoover, then Secretary of Commerce at Washington, but saw him, too. While waiting for his call to be put through, he also saw the telephone operator at the Washington switchboard making connections.

The Radio. Of all inventions and changes promoting communication among men, the radio is the most wonderful. It enables a person anywhere in America to talk with friends aboard an ocean steamship many miles at sea. The voice of President Hoover accepting, at Palo Alto, California, the presidential nomination of his party (1928) was easily heard by millions of his countrymen seated comfortably in their homes. The words of King George of England at the opening of the 

London Naval Conference (January 21, 1930) were heard by 100,000,000 listeners throughout the world.

The most outstanding advance of 1929 was the coupling of the radio with the airplane for communicating purposes and for guiding planes in flight. Byrd, while in his plane in the farthest South, was able to talk by radio with The New York Times, the newspaper that was financing his expedition. A message was sent to Byrd which called for an answer. The New York Times flashed the message 12,000 miles to Byrd's Antarctic base. At the moment Byrd was flying his plane 100 miles away, but while in flight he wrote out his answer and sent it to the base, from which it was sent on to The New York Times. 

The whole incident occupied less than forty-five minutes! 

All steamships carrying as many as fifty persons, counting passengers and crew, are required to have a radio equipment. Calls for help may be sent and received at sea, and thus many lives may be saved. 

When radio stations began interfering with one another in their wave lengths, Congress passed the Radio Act of 1927. This created a Federal Radio Commission consisting of one member from each of five zones into which the country was divided. The Commission regulates all radio stations, and may revoke any license for cause. 

Age of Electricity. It was in 1881 that Washington 

City was first illuminated by electricity. Now the business

sections of our cities have the bright lights that make the streets as light as day. In 1882 the first electric lighting plant was installed in New York City. Electricity quickly drove out oil and gas for lighting houses and streets. 

Edison. The most famous name connected with the world of electricity is that of 

Thomas A. Edison, known as the "Wizard of Menlo Park." 

Edison was born in 1847, in 

Milan, Ohio. He attended the public schools only three months. Like Lincoln, Edison came up out of poor and hard conditions. What teaching he had came chiefly from his mother. He became a great reader, especially of science books, among them the works of Michael Faraday, the English scientist.

While a newsboy he became interested in electricity.

This interest later led him to become a telegraph operator in various cities. In 1873 he contrived the duplex telegraph, a device for sending two messages at the same time on a single wire. 

OUNDERWOOD & UNDERWOOD THOMAS A. EDISON 

He was born in Ohio in 1847. At the age of 12 he was a newsboy on the Grand Trunk Railway, and later became a telegraph operator. He invented the incandescent lamp, the phonograph, and hundreds of other instruments and appliances- mostly of an electrical character. He died at his home in West Orange, New Jersey, in 1931.

In 1877, after Bell's success with the telephone, Edison invented the carbon telephone transmitter, which enabled the conversation to be sent over greater distances. In the same year came the phonograph, which Edison's inventive genius first made practicable.

In 1879 Edison gave the world the first practical incandescent light, in which a fine wire enclosed in a

glass bulb, without oxygen, is heated by an electric current. This gives us the light that we now have everywhere in our homes and in our offices and shops. 

In 1893 Edison was the first to show pictures of objects in motion. Three years before this he had invented the kinetoscope and the kinetograph, machines for projecting pictures and writing upon a screen. A projection lantern and a photo film were used, the film being moved in front of the projecting lens. The film carried a series of photographs of a moving object made at frequent intervals; when moved rapidly the images projected on the screen give the impression of a moving object. 

Out of these machines came the "movies " of today which have given amusement and instruction to millions of people throughout the world. Later came the "talkies," so called, in which the actors talk as well as act on the screen. The motion-picture industry has now become one of the largest in the country. Millions of dollars are invested, highly paid actors employed, and expensive films produced; it has been estimated that the attendance at the picture shows in America reaches at least 100,000,000 every week. The industry has an invested capital of nearly $2,000,000,000 and owns over 20,000 theaters. It may become the most powerful instrument in the world for public education as well as for public entertainment.

Electricity: Man's Servant. The use of electricity in housework began about 1900. Since that time the work in the home has been revolutionized. All kinds of electrical devices have been invented for the saving of labor, among them the washing machine, the ironing machine, the dishwasher, the sewing machine, the vacuum sweeper, the toaster, the waffle iron, the chafing dish, the table stove, the fireless cooker, the warming pad, the floor polisher, the refrigerator, and the curling iron. With these

servants at her command, the modern housewife escapes many of the burdens of the hard-working housewife of the long ago.

It is well said that "electricity burrows and climbs through the house with its tentacles," rendering many services. Mark Sullivan has said of it: "In any house at the same time it may be producing heat in one room, cold in another, and light in another, while in still another it may bring voices of friends from thousands of miles away over the radio." The days of dipping the tallow candle or trimming the wicks and filling the kerosene lamps with oil are now gone. George Washington, one of the richest men of his day in America, had no such comforts as many humble homes may have today. 

BETTERMENT IN FARM LIFE 

What have modern inventions and improvements brought to the rural districts? The farm may have not only the telephone and the automobile, but also through the electric power stations may have the advantage of electric lights and labor-saving devices. Electricity may be used to milk the cows, and the cream separator will prepare the dairy product for the market.

The refrigerator car has been of vast importance in the fruit and meat trade. It is indispensable for the shipment of" perishable freight." By means of it beef, fish, pork, and fresh fruits may be shipped almost any distance and be kept fresh and wholesome. Thus distant markets are opened up for the products of the farm. 

Other inventions that have aided the farmer are drills, mowers, hay-tedders, hay-loaders, corn-cutters and cornplanters, ditchers, straw-stackers, stump-pullers, and the tractor plow. The plowman need no longer “plod his weary way "between the plow-handles, following the horses furrow after furrow around the field, but

he may now sit on his tractor driven by a gasoline engine and turn up five or six furrows at a time. The farmer today also has the combination harvester, which cuts the grain, threshes it, and pours it into bags all by the continuous operation of the machine, thereby making possible largescale wheat-raising as on the vast farms of the Northwest.

OFFICE EQUIPMENT Modern inventions have also brought aid to the offices of professional and business men.

RENTSOPORTAADHU TEPEWILL THE FIRST SHIFT-KEY TYPEWRITER, 1878 

The writing machine or typewriter, invented by Remington in 1873, now does the writing of business letters and documents, of the lectures and addresses of professional and public men. Handwritten letters of public men now seldom appear, for the typewritten letter has displaced them.

The dictating machine enables one to speak through a tube and to have his words recorded on a roll. When placed on a somewhat similar machine, this roll reproduces the message distinctly. With such devices a business man may dictate all his letters to the machine, while his typist is busy at something else. Upon receiving the cylinders, she is able through ear-phones to hear the letters, a phrase or a clause at a time, and to type them as accurately as she would type letters dictated directly to her. 

In 1884 William Seward Burroughs first introduced a model of an adding machine. With its assistance, the

adding of a long column of figures is no longer a hard mental process, but is done mechanically by punching the figures on a keyboard. If you punch right, you may 80 06.00 5 10 15 20 $2 25 

THE FIRST CASH REGISTER, 1879 

depend upon the machine to add right and to make no mistake.

All banks, offices, and business houses where bookkeeping and accounting are needed on any large scale consider adding machines a necessity. 

In retail stores, the cash register rings up the amount of the purchase, helps to make change, and shows the amount of money paid in and out during the sales of the day. It is also a necessity in all retail shops. 

THE MODERN PRINTING PRESS AND THE LINOTYPE 

The first Gutenberg press, invented about 1446, was a small wooden hand-press with movable types. It was improved but little in the next three hundred years. In 

1847, however, Richard Hoe invented a revolving cylinder or rotary press which turned out 20,000 copies of newspapers in an hour. Improvements since 1865 have enabled the modern printing press to turn out over 300,000 copies an hour. The machinery in one operation prints, cuts, folds, and counts the copies. 

To have such quantity production in printing, type must be set rapidly. Setting type by hand was a slow and tedious process. Each piece of type had to be picked up and put in place. Sometimes a "stick" (or

column) of set type would fall from the printer's hands and be "pied," that is, scattered or mixed, and the type would then have to be distributed to the various cases, OEWING GALLOWAY GUTENBERG EXAMINING THE FIRST PROOF FROM HIS PRESS 

This photograph was taken at the Cologne Industrial Exposition in 1928, where the story of printing was dramatized. or boxes, and the whole setting-up process would need to be done over again. To "pie " a column of type was an expensive blunder. In 1884 the linotype was invented to set the type. This machine is operated by working a keyboard like that of an adding machine or typewriter, only much more extensive. As each key is pressed, the right mold falls into place, then hot metal flows into the forms and holds the "line-o'type " in place. Types are set as fast as a man can work his fingers. The monotype, a machine to set letters separately and hold them in a line, came into use in 1888. These improvements have made possible cheaper and more extensive printing.

ADVANCES IN SCIENCE, MEDICINE, AND SURGERY 

Science and invention have worked together in making life better for mankind. The telescope brought distant worlds within man's vision. The microscope extended man's knowledge of the small forms of life. In recent years these instruments have been greatly improved. The telescope has been enlarged, more powerful lenses have been invented, and far distant stars and suns heretofore unknown have been brought into view and the vastness of the universe revealed. At the Lowell 

Astronomical Observatory, at Flagstaff, Arizona, a new planet, Pluto, was discovered, in 1930, beyond Neptune, on the outer rim of our planetary system, a discovery that would not have been possible with the old telescopes. 

Science has also added to the length of man's life. 

More thorough knowledge of the laws of hygiene has brought better health and longer life. Modern surgery has made wonderful progress. Today a surgeon by his skillful operations saves lives that would have been lost a generation ago. Doctors and scientists are carefully studying the causes of diseases and their prevention. 

In 1890 the average length of life was under forty years. Especially among infants, deaths were due to lack of hospitals, nurses, and medical care, and men did not know how to combat disease. By 1930 the average age at death had risen to fifty-five. 

In 1859 Louis Pasteur (pȧs tûr'), a famous French physician, began the study of diseases resulting in the discovery that many human ailments were caused by germs. The breeding-places for germs are refuse piles, garbage, unclean streets, and polluted streams. Flies carry disease germs to the food of the people in their homes. In summer the doors and windows must be open for cool breezes. Formerly there were no screens to keep

out the flies and other insects. Now a good housewife is ashamed to find a fly in the house, and if she sees one she has no rest until she can "swat the fly."

As the result of increased knowledge about disease, public health service is provided. This health service, which provides competent doctors, visiting nurses, and public hospitals, is extended to almost every city and county in the country. There has been a marked improvement in public health. Better medical schools, nurses' training schools, increased number of hospitals, and health boards have brought about socialized medicine for removing bad and unhealthy conditions in society. Men are enriching life by living healthier lives. "Prevention is better than cure."

EXPOSITIONS 

America has held a number of expositions to show the progress that man has made. that man has made. The first of these was the Centennial Exposition held at Philadelphia in 1876, to celebrate the centennial of American independence. 

This furnished a remarkable object lesson in the indus trial growth of the United States. 

The World's Fair held in Chicago in 1893 celebrated the 

400th anniversary of the discovery of America. Repro ductions of Columbus's ships were on display. The Fair was a fine exhibit of the products and the costumes of the nations of the world. The Pan-American Exposition was held in Buffalo in 1901 to cultivate good will among our American neighbors. There was a brilliant display of electricity and electrical lighting. In 1904 the Louisiana 

Purchase Exposition was held in St. Louis, and in 1907 the 

300th anniversary of the settlement of Jamestown, Va., was celebrated by an exposition there. In 1915 the 

Panama-Pacific Exposition was held in San Francisco and in 1926 the 150th anniversary of American independence

was celebrated by another exposition in Philadelphia. 

In 1933 a Century of Progress exposition is planned in Chicago to commemorate the 100th anniversary of the founding of the city.

CHAPTER XLI  

Common Schools. By the close of the Civil War education had come to be recognized as the business of the State. Previously the church, the minister, the home, or private individuals in "subscription schools" had often been relied upon to teach the children. But within a few years after the Civil War common schools supported by public taxation had been established in all the States.

High Schools. The high schools came later and more slowly than the common schools. High schools were intended to form the link between the common schools and the university. Indiana was the first State to provide by law for a general system of public high schools. That 

State in its very beginning looked forward to a complete graduated system of education. Its constitution of 1816 placed upon the legislature the duty of providing by law a general system of education, ascending in regular gradation from township schools to a State University, wherein tuition shall be free and equally open to all." 

Fully fifty years passed before Indiana carried out this provision, but now that State and practically all the other States have established such systems of education. 66 In 1860 there were only about 300 free high schools in the country; half of these were in Massachusetts, New 

York, and Ohio. The South had none at all. In those 740

days nearly all educational opportunities above the common schools were to be found in academies and seminaries which had their chief support from the tuition charge paid by the pupils. In the East there were some 

Latin schools and grammar schools designed to prepare boys for college, but such schools could be attended only by the children of the well-to-do. They had no connection with the grades of the common school; there was no graduation into the "Junior" or "Senior" High School; there were no such free high schools. 

About ten years later the free public high schools began their work. They then grew rapidly, and within a few years every city and town had established its own high school. In the country districts a " Township High 

School" frequently was provided at some central point. 

The high schools were "commissioned, or authorized, to prepare boys and girls for college. Their graduates were admitted to many colleges and universities on the basis of their diplomas or certificates without further examination. 

High schools have greatly enlarged their courses of study. They have established libraries, laboratories, and gymnasiums for physical training. The best of them offer not only English, Latin, mathematics, history, and civics, but also economics, modern languages, domestic science, mechanical drawing, manual training, stenography, typewriting, and most of the natural sciences. As time passed, a few cities have extended their high schools into "Junior Colleges" in which the first two years of college work are given. 

Colleges and Universities. Colleges and universities existed long before the common school or the high school. 

It was the educated men from these institutions who went out to advocate and promote a common education for all.

At first the Church as well as the State was the

founder of higher institutions of learning. The Church institutions for higher learning, except those in the Far West, were established between 1830 and 1860 before the expansion of the common school. 

Growth of Universities. In education the era since the 

Civil War has been marked by the great growth of American universities, especially of State universities in the West. While the population of the country in the decade from 1910 to 1920 increased about 20 per cent, the student population in colleges and universities increased about 65 per cent. Columbia has a student attendance, winter and summer, of about 30,000. The University of Chicago, established in 1892 and richly endowed by John D. Rockefeller, now has an attendance of 12,000. At the University of California nearly 20,000 students are enrolled and at the University of Michigan more than 10,000. 

Other State universities of the Middle West have equally large numbers.

These universities not only offer general courses but also provide professional schools (Law, Medicine, Engineering, Music, Education, Journalism). In addition they offer extension courses, by which thousands of people who have been denied the privilege of a college education and who cannot go to the campus to study are given the benefits of university study. In such cases the professors go to "extension centers" to give lectures; readings are assigned to the students, who hear the lectures, pursue their studies, and take the examinations. Many universities offer courses by correspondence and in a few instances courses are broadcast by means of the radio. 

Summer sessions, evening schools, and "chautauquas also help to provide educational opportunities for thousands of people.

Higher Education of Women. A century ago it was generally thought that higher education in college and

university was not intended for women. A woman's brain was thought to be too weak to pursue advanced studies. Her education, beyond the common school, was provided only in the "finishing school," where she might study English literature, French, polite manners, art, drawing, music, and similar subjects.

As years passed, however, a demand arose for a more vigorous training for women, and as a result women's colleges came into existence. Mrs. Emma Willard opened a "female" seminary in Troy, New York, in 1823; she became one of the best known women teachers in America. Elmira College, the oldest of women's colleges in America, was founded in 1855. In 1865 Matthew 

Vassar gave a large sum for the founding of a woman's college at Poughkeepsie (po kip'si), New York; this is 

Vassar College, and the standard it set was to equal that of any college for men. Among the other institutions in the East that provided for the higher education of women were Wellesley, Smith, and Mount Holyoke Colleges in 

Massachusetts, Hunter College in New York City, the largest women's college in the world, and Bryn Mawr College, near Philadelphia, established especially for graduate and research study. 

Co-education. The State universities in the West have admitted women to their classes on equal terms with men for more than fifty years. This higher co-education has become the custom in the West. With few exceptions colleges on private foundations also have co-education. 

Oberlin, in Ohio, was the pioneer co-educational college, admitting women to some of its courses from its foundation in 1833. Antioch College, in Ohio, admitted women on an equal basis with men in 1853. In 1867 Indiana 

University opened its doors to women on equal terms with men. The University of Michigan did likewise in 

1870. The other State universities in the Western States

and a number of similar institutions in the East also adopted the same policy. 

Today the women graduates from women's colleges and co-educational institutions are numbered by the thousands. They have organized an "Association of American University Women," and exercise an important influence on education, politics, and public morals. Women have gone into the professional schools and have become successful lawyers and physicians. Judge Florence 

Allen of Ohio is a member of the Supreme Court of that State. Women have also served as State governors and as members of State legislatures and of Congress. 

Education of the Negro. The progress of the American Negro since his emancipation from slavery is a remarkable chapter in our history. The Negroes came out of slavery in poverty and ignorance. With the aid of friends, but chiefly by their own efforts, they now own over 30,000,000 acres of land and other property worth more than $1,000,000,000. While the masses of the race have relied upon manual labor for their living, thousands of Negroes have become well educated and have entered successfully into business and into the various professions-medicine, law, dentistry, engineering, and the ministry.

Since the Civil War the Negro race in America has increased from 4,000,000 to nearly 12,000,000. Illiteracy among Negroes has been reduced from 90 per cent to less than 30 per cent. Among the men of distinction the race has produced are W. E. B. Dubois, Booker T. Washington, Kelley Miller, and Robert Moton in literature and education; Paul Lawrence Dunbar in poetry; and Roland 

Hayes, one of the great tenor singers of the world, in music.

The Negroes were greatly helped and encouraged by the Church mission schools that were established in the

South just after the Civil War. In 1867 George Peabody, a Northern philanthropist, gave a large sum for education in the South. Fisk University in Nashville, Tennessee, was established for Negroes. Wilberforce University near Xenia, Ohio, had been established by the 

African Methodist Episcopal Church as early as 1856. 

Howard University for Negroes was established at Washington, D. C., in 1870.

One of the outstanding educators of America, as well as one of the benefactors of the human race, was General Samuel C. Armstrong, founder of Hampton Normal and 

Industrial Institute at Norfolk, Virginia. Armstrong served in the Union Army in the Civil War, showing special interest in Negro troops. Soon after, through the influence of General O. O. Howard, Armstrong worked with the Freedmen's Bureau, having charge of the Negroes in ten counties in Virginia. He became interested in Negro education and in 1871 founded Hampton Institute. For ten years only Negroes attended the school, but in 1878 Indians were also admitted. By 1893 the school had 100 teachers and 1800 students. Part of the funds donated by the National Government to Virginia for a State Agricultural College (by the Morrill Act of 1862) was given to Hampton for the benefit of the colored people.

Among the early students at Hampton was Booker T. 

Washington. In 1872 "by walking, begging rides in wagons and on trains," Washington, who was then only 13 years old, traveled 500 miles to Norfolk, where he came under the influence of General Armstrong. He studied three years in Hampton, working as janitor for his room and board. Later he taught at Hampton, assisting General Armstrong in the training of 75 Indians. 

Booker T. Washington decided to devote his life to the education of the Negro. In 1881 he founded at

Tuskegee, Alabama, in the heart of the Black Belt, the

Tuskegee Normal and Industrial Institute. The school was opened in a little shanty and Negro church.

Within 

BROWN BROS BOOKER T. WASHINGTON 

twenty years the school had 40 buildings, erected mostly by student labor. In 1889 

Tuskegee was endowed by 

Congress with over 25,000 acres of land. Andrew Carnegie gave the school $600,- 

000. Tuskegee now has an endowment of $2,000,000 with 

100 buildings, a library of 20,000 volumes, and in 1928 enrolled nearly 2000 students from 31 States. Graduates from our best universities are teachers in the institution. 

It has had a great influence in promoting the progress of the 

Negro in the South.

The main work offered at Tuskegee is industrial. The young men are taught shoemaking, plastering, carpentry, blacksmithing, printing, farming; the young women learn laundering, house work, cooking, and sewing. Washington's plan was to make the Negro self-supporting and independent.

A number of other Negro schools patterned after Tuskegee have been established in various parts of the South. 

Tuskegee's" movable school" (a motor truck with tools and teachers) travels through the Black Belt teaching the Negroes how to improve their working methods, their health, and their homes. The annual conference held at 

Tuskegee for many years was like an educational revival. 

It was attended by Negro farmers and teachers from all

parts of the South; it did much to spread better ways of work and living among Negroes. Many of the one-room cabins in which Negro families had lived for years were done away with, and the Negroes began to buy farms. and build good homes. 66 

American Literature: National Period. The early period of American literature, in which Washington Irving was the leading figure, has sometimes been called the Knickerbocker Period." Much of the literature of this period was English rather than American, most of the writers looking to England rather than to the United 

States for their inspiration.

The second period of American literature was dominated by a group of New England writers, among whom Ralph Waldo Emerson was prominent. This period is sometimes called the "New England Period." It included such authors as Longfellow, Whittier, Holmes, Lowell, and Hawthorne.

The third period began a few years after the close of the Civil War and has been well named the "National Period." During this period the West and the South as well as New England and the East were represented by writers of the first rank. Some of the authors wrote on subjects taken from their own neighborhood, but most of them reflected the expansion of the country and the development of ideas which followed the Civil War. In this sense the period was national, not local. 

Mark Twain, Bret Harte, and Edward Eggleston were pioneers in the National Period. Mark Twain (Samuel 

L. Clemens) was born in Missouri in 1835. Like Benjamin Franklin he secured a large part of his education as a printer and newspaper reporter. Later he gave his entire time to literature and wrote, in all, more than 25 volumes, including such famous stories as Tom Sawyer and Huckleberry Finn. He is best known as a humorist,

but was also a philosopher and a reformer. He reflected the wild, care-free spirit of the West, and the atmosphere of the Mississippi River appears in many of his books. 

When a young man he served as pilot for a time on a 

Mississippi River steamboat, JAMES WHITCOMB RILEY 

James Whitcomb Riley, the "Hoosier poet," was born in 

Greenfield, Indiana, in 1853 and died in Indianapolis, in 1916.

Much of his verse is in the Mid dle-Western or 66 

Hoosier" dialect and is characterized by humor and pathos. His poetry is homely, likeable, and democratic. He was also widely known as a reader of his own productions. and he loved the river almost as if it were a human being. 

He looked upon it with the eyes of a poet, and it appears over and over again in his 

Bret Harte was born in 

Albany, New York, a year after Mark Twain. Mark 

Twain was a product of the country, the river bank, the mining camp, and the West, while Harte came from the city, a cultured home, and the 

East. Never was there a more striking contrast in the boyhood days of two noted men. 

When Harte was a boy of seventeen, his family moved to California. This was a turning point in his life, and threw a new atmosphere about him. Presently he began to write on subjects connected with the Far West. His story, The Luck of Roaring Camp, made him famous, but he is probably known to more people by his jingling poem, "The Heathen Chinee." 

Edward Eggleston was born in Indiana in 1837, one year after the birth of Bret Harte. His father, a college

man, had moved from Virginia to Indiana to practice law. Edward was nine years of age when his father died. 

The boy's education was meager and irregular, for his health was delicate. While still a lad in his 'teens he determined to become a circuit-riding Methodist preacher. 

He entered upon his arduous duties with great enthusiasm, but after a few months his health gave way, and he sought rest and strength in the pine forests of the North. 

Later he made literature his profession and became famous by the publication of The 

Hoosier Schoolmaster and The Hoosier Schoolboy, fas cinating tales of pioneer life in Indiana. 

JOHN BURROUGHS 

John Burroughs, the famous naturalist, was born in the State of 

New York in 1837. He taught school for a time and was later a treasury clerk and bank examiner. For many years he lived on a farm and devoted his time to writing books and raising fruit.

He was the author of a long list of very interesting books on nature. He died in 1921. 

James Whitcomb Riley. A little after Eggleston's time, James Whitcomb Riley, another Hoosier, wrote poems "from the heart out," in language so simple and touching that he endeared himself to the masses of the people and particularly to those who had passed their childhood days in the country. His father was a lawyer and wanted young 

James to study for the same profession, but the boy preferred the sign painter's trade. Later he became a newspaper man and a lecturer, and finally devoted his entire time to literature. Among his best known poems are "The Old Swimmin'-Hole," "Out to Old Aunt 

Mary's," "Thoughts fer the Discuraged Farmer," and "An Old Sweetheart of Mine."

John Burroughs and John Muir. Burroughs and Muir were students of the great out-of-doors. They interpreted nature in all her moods and described her in all her beauty. Burroughs was not an educated man in the ordinary sense of that term. 

JOHN MUIR 

John Muir, the great naturalist, geologist, and explorer, was born in Scotland in 1838, but spent most of his life in the United 

States. He discovered the Muir glacier in Alaska and also explored the Arctic regions. He studied and traveled in almost every quarter of the globe, including Manchuria, Africa, and 

South America. His Stickeen, the story of a dog, is a fascinat- 66 66 "I was born of and among people," he said, ""who neither read books nor cared for them." His book was the great book of nature, and he read it as few men could. 

All his life he lived close to the out-of-doors. His writings are interesting for their literary form as well as for their scientific value. 

John Muir was a Scotchman who delighted in mountains, canyons, and glaciers. 

When a boy he came from 

Scotland to Wisconsin.

Later he studied four years at the University of Wisconsin.

After that the world was his laboratory. Burroughs loved to saunter through the woods and fields, listening for the notes of the first birds of 

Spring, while Muir liked to climb mountains, explore glaciers, and sleep by his campfire often without pillow or blanket of any kind. Muir was also interested in animals, but he would not take the life of a fish or a rattlesnake, and he studied the repulsive, slimy lizard with real affection. He wrote little, but what he did produce contains the real spark of life. ing book. He spent the later years of his life in California, where he died in 1914.

The literary output of the National Period was enormous. Scores of names could be given-names that are, some of them, just as worthy and as famous as those already mentioned. During this period the South gave us Sidney Lanier, the poet, Thomas Nelson Page, the novelist, and Joel Chandler Harris, author of the "Uncle

Remus" stories. New England is pictured for us in the stories of William Dean Howells. In historical writing 

Francis Parkman, Henry Adams, James Schouler, James 

Ford Rhodes, and Edward Channing won distinction.

CHAPTER XLII  

The Growth of Cities. One of the noteworthy features of recent American history is the enormous growth of manufactures and cities. The two have grown together. 

Factories seek railroad centers and the workers follow the factories. This brings an increase in population to the city.

By 1900 America had about twelve billions of dollars invested in factories, with an annual output of fifteen billions in value, over fifteen times as much as when the Civil War began. At first New England contained nearly all the factories, but gradually manufactures spread to the West, and Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, and Wisconsin became industrial States. By 1900 Illinois, once almost wholly agricultural, had gone beyond Massachusetts in manufacturing. 

America is no longer a purely agricultural nation.

Farming is still a great industry in America, but if we take all manufacturing together we find its output to be greater in value than the products of the farms. In 

1850 the manufactured products of the United States brought a little over a billion dollars, but by 1930 these products had mounted to more than sixty-two billions. 

In 1890 the value of manufactured goods in America was for the first time above the value of farm products; in 1930 the value of manufactured goods was more than 754

PENNSYLVANIA TERMINAL, NEW YORK CITY 

UNION STATION PENNSYLVANIA R.R BURLINGTON ROUTE THE ASTON ROAD KEE RR UNION STATION, CHICAGO 

five times that of the farm products. In 1865 the United 

States held fourth place as a producer of manufactured goods. Now the country ranks first in the world's manufacturing. This may be due in part to the enterprise and

industry of the people, but more especially to high wages, skilled labor, and vast natural resources-coal, iron, copper, zinc, lumber, gold, and silver.

In 1789 the United States contained only six cities with a population above 8000, and only one with a population as high as 33,000. Less than 4 per cent of the people lived in cities; the rest dwelt in the country or in small towns and villages. Now, according to the census of 

1930, the United States has more than 600 cities with a population of at least 8000 and sixty-eight with a population of over 100,000. Each census shows a large increase in such cities. Today more than half of the 

American people live in cities. New York City contains a greater population than all the rest of New York State. 

The growth of city population has been so rapid that the cities have had difficulty in meeting their increasing needs. Within a hundred years New York City grew from a population of about 50,000 to over 6,000,000. In a little more than ten years Detroit received nearly 600,000 of new population, attracted largely by the automobile industry. In the same time Flint, Michigan, and Schenectady, New York, more than doubled their populations. 

The growth of Los Angeles has been equally rapid. 

Within the memory of men born and living within her limits, Chicago has changed from a small prairie town to a large city of over 3,000,000 people. 

Causes of City Growth. There are many reasons for the rapid growth of cities. Let us note a few: (1) During the last fifty years most immigrants settled in the cities; free land had largely disappeared and work could be found in factories and mills. (2) Sanitary conditions in the cities have improved, and the death rate is lower than in the country. (3) Labor-saving machinery for the farms has made it possible for fewer people to cultivate the land.

(4) Hard times and reverses have come to the farms. 

Consequently farm boys abandon the farms for the cities, where they find jobs that pay better and amusements that are more attractive than in the country. (5) Cities are the centers of trade, of business opportunities, of shipping and manufacturing. Better transportation facilities, the growth of steam and electric railways, the increased use of machinery, and the rise of great factories and businesses have promoted city growth by leading to a greater demand for city workers. (6) Fine parks, libraries, museums, churches, theaters, and amusement houses have lured people to the city. 

The Problem of the City. Two-thirds to three-fourths of the dwellers in cities are of foreign birth or parentage, and many of them are unable to speak the English language. Large numbers of all races and nationalities are gathered in groups and districts. City population constantly shifts, and people who live in the same block are often unknown to one another. The criminal classes usually have their dives in the great cities. Here the dependent poor and the unemployed are also numerous. 

In the cities a wide division between the rich and the poor exists and a conflict appears between classes whose methods, conditions, and ideals of life differ widely. 

The city therefore offers special problems in government. 

With so assorted a population the city finds itself forced to attend to a multitude of duties. It must arrest criminals and punish crime; protect property; prevent the use of firearms and fireworks that might be dangerous to life or property; regulate the erection of chimneys and smokestacks; and require the inspection of gas pipes, drainage, and electric wires. The city government is expected to afford police protection; care for the public health and prevent disease; provide a good water supply; protect the people against poor milk and adulterated

food; afford fire protection; provide for public lighting; attend to street paving and keep the sidewalks in repair; license and regulate all kinds of street traffic; regulate the operation of automobiles; provide and control transportation facilities; dispose of sewage and garbage. In addition, the city undertakes to maintain schools, libraries, hospitals, museums, and parks.

Old Form of City Government. American democracy almost broke down in the city, and new forms of city government were found necessary. The old type of city government rested on the idea of the "separation of powers. There must be a city legislature (council), a city executive (mayor), and a city judiciary (courts and police judges). All these departments must be kept separate and distinct. Most American cities held to this plan until the opening of the twentieth century. 

Commission Government. Then the commission form of city government arose. This disregards the old theory of dividing powers and is based on the idea that in administering a city the work to be done is more like business than government. The commission plan, therefore, abolishes the city council as a legislature, on the ground that not laws but contracts and business arrangements must be made and carried out. All authority (except judicial) is given to a small group of men. 

Commission government originated in Galveston, Texas, after a great tidal wave had flooded the city in 1901. Before that disaster Galveston had been badly governed. The city debt had increased; the city council had had to borrow to meet expenses; city affairs were managed wastefully; spoilsmen were put into places of profit; taxes were high; accounts were poorly kept; and there was graft in office.

After the destruction caused by the flood, Galveston could not obtain funds to rebuild its waste places under

the old, corrupt, city government. The people sent men to the legislature of the State and asked that body to overthrow the old form of city government and give to a commission of five men the powers formerly vested in mayor, council, and other officers. The request was granted; and the new Galveston charter provided for the popular election, every two years, of five commissioners, all to be chosen from the city at large. This commission enacts all city ordinances, passes all appropriations, regulates expenditures, handles all franchises, and awards all contracts on public works. One member of the commission is chosen as mayor-president. The other four members are heads of administrative departments: (1) finance and revenue; (2) water and sewerage; (3) Police and fire protection; (4) streets and public property. 

Commission government fixed responsibility for the failure of any department of the city service; it made business methods possible, prevented delays, and improved the quality of the city officers. Seeing the advantages of the new plan, many other cities of the country have adopted it. 

The City Manager. As one great flood brought reform to Galveston, so another brought a change to Dayton, Ohio. In March, 1913, heavy rains destroyed thousands of dollars' worth of property. New business management was necessary to put the city on its feet. 

The plan adopted by Dayton is called the "citymanager” plan, or the "commission-manager plan.”

The people of Dayton, like those of Galveston, elect a commission of five members, but the Dayton commission places the business of the city in the hands of one person who is an expert business manager. The city-manager plan looks upon the city not as politics, but as business. 

The citizens, as stockholders in the city, choose a board

of directors (the commission) and the directors appoint a foreman, or business manager, to manage the city's business, just as a school board, elected by the people, choose a superintendent to direct the schools of the community. 

The city manager is expected to be a business expert with experience in managing a city's business. He chooses his own subordinates and may remove them. 

On the whole, the plan seems to have succeeded and more than 600 cities in America have adopted the plan. 

Immigration. During the last one hundred years the 

United States has admitted from foreign lands fully 30,000,000 people. Indeed, all who ever came to America were immigrants, and nearly all came to improve living conditions and to give the children a better opportunity than the parents had enjoyed. 

Twenty years after the Civil War a new kind of immigration to America began. The earlier immigrants were nearly all from northern and western Europe - Germans, Irish, French, Scotch, English, Welsh, Scandinavians.

They were much like the people who were already here. 

The Scandinavians (Norwegians, Swedes, and Danes) began to come mostly after the Civil War; they settled in great numbers in the States of the Northwest. 

Most of the immigrants that poured in after 1885 were from southern and eastern Europe - Italians, Sicilians, Greeks, Russians, Jugoslavs, Slovaks, Austrians, Poles, Hungarians, Armenians, Croatians, Czechs, Bohemians, Lithuanians, and Rumanians. About forty different nationalities mixed into our population, and a babel of tongues, a mixture of all languages and religions, occurred. Taking all the newcomers, together with the 

Negroes and including Mexicans, Portuguese, Japanese,

Chinese, and Filipinos, America became a mass of all the races on earth.

Could any common life or national unity come out of such a population? Could the immigrants be merged into one people, speaking one language and having one allegiance to one Government and one flag? 

Honest and industrious laborers undoubtedly bring to a country great benefits. Millions of immigrants have 1 

A TRANSATLANTIC LINER IN NEW YORK HARBOR This is the S.S. Majestic of the White Star Line. 

proved to be good laborers. The foreign-born laborers who worked in our country fifteen or twenty years ago added over a half billion dollars of wealth to the country every year.

The great bulk of our immigrants are between fourteen and forty-five years of age-within the years of their greatest productivity. Many talented musicians and artists, as well as business managers, scientists, journalists, and statesmen, have been immigrants—men like 

Alexander Hamilton, Albert Gallatin, Robert Owen, Carl 

Schurz, Jacob A. Riis, Edward Bok, Louis Agassiz, James J. Hill, Andrew Carnegie, and Joseph Pulitzer.

But the coming of great numbers into a country in which free land no longer existed caused serious difficulties. The twenty years before the World War saw over 15,000,000 immigrants come to America. During the year ending June 30, 1914, the year the World War began, 1,218,418 immigrants entered the United States.

The World War cut down the flood to almost nothing, only 110,000 entering in 1917. But as soon as the war was over, the stream of immigration began to rise again. 

The entrance of many laborers combined with the industrial depression that followed the war threw many Americans out of work and by 1921 more than 2,000,000 men had no jobs. These conditions made it seem wise to restrict or check immigration. 

Restricting Immigration. In 1882 Congress passed the 

Chinese Exclusion Act, excluding Chinese laborers from the country and providing for the inspection of immigrants and for the exclusion of undesirables-idiots, the insane, convicts, and paupers likely to become a public charge. Gangs of " pauper laborers were being hired in Europe by big companies and brought to America; to stop this practice Congress in 1885 passed the Alien Contract Labor Law, prohibiting the bringing of workers to this country under contract to perform labor. 99 The Immigration Act of 1910 levied a head tax of $4.00 upon every immigrant, and also forbade the admission (in addition to the classes already mentioned) of epileptics, persons suffering from tuberculosis or other dangerous diseases, polygamists, anarchists, children under sixteen not accompanied by their parents, and all Chinese, except those who were students, merchants, professional men, and employees of exhibits or expositions. 

But, as has been pointed out, the need for greater restriction became more apparent as the years passed.

Three restrictive acts were passed by Congress, but were vetoed by three successive Presidents, Cleveland in 1897, Taft in 1913, and Wilson in 1915. In February, 1917, however, Congress passed the Burnett Immigration Bill

What promise does the land of liberty hold for this immigrant family? 

The Statue of Liberty stands in the background. over President Wilson's veto. This act strengthens and extends the exclusion of aliens. It increases the head tax from $4.00 to $8.00, and imposes heavy penalties on steamship companies that bring to America persons whom the law seeks to exclude; its provisions are more rigid upon contract labor; and it imposes the literacy test, requiring all immigrants over 16 years of age (not blind nor dumb) to be able to read in some language.

The Burnett Act definitely checked immigration, but after the World War, when the flood of immigration rose

again, Congress passed an act (1921) limiting the number of immigrants which any country might have to a certain percentage of its people living in America in 1910. 

About 350,000 aliens could come in under this act. 

In 1924 another act reduced the quota of each country to only 2 per cent of the number of its people living in the United States in 1890. Under this law, as amended in 

1928, about 153,000 immigrants a year may be admitted, the quota of each country to be determined on the basis of the "national origins" of the American people from the beginning of our national life rather than on the number in the United States in 1890. The present law also provides that immigrants who are not acceptable shall be weeded out before they start from home, instead of after they get here. This is done through American consuls in 

European cities who give visas, or passports, to those who are thought to be admissible. The act seeks to prevent the separation of families, and to prevent delays, detention, and hardships at Ellis Island, the gateway to New York City. It also excludes those aliens who are not allowed to become citizens. This provision refers primarily to the Japanese, for Japan is allowed no share in the number of immigrants admitted to the country; this special discrimination was naturally very offensive to Japan. Up to this time the Japanese had been excluded by mutual agreement between Japan and the 

United States.

Prohibition. The movement that brought about the national prohibition of the manufacture and sale of intoxicating liquors started more than a hundred years ago. Societies were formed, the members of which gave their pledges not to drink alcoholic beverages, for they believed that such drinks led to poverty and crime, and that mankind would be benefited if the manufacture and sale of such beverages were prohibited by law.

The Prohibition Party was organized in 1869 to carry the temperance movement into politics. The party has nominated a candidate in every subsequent presidential election.

The agitation for prohibition in time caused law-making bodies to give attention to the movement. In 1846 

Maine passed a law against the liquor traffic, the first in history. Kansas adopted prohibition in 1881, and 

Iowa soon after. Other States passed laws providing for "local option," under which localities, such as counties and precincts, voted independently on the liquor question. Because women were not then allowed to vote, the Prohibitionists felt that their cause was not fully represented in "local option "contests. However, the movement continued to spread. By 1900 five States had gone dry, and by 1915 three-fourths of the country had some legal provisions for prohibition. Largely because of the necessity for the conservation of food materials during the World War, the cause of prohibition was won through the adoption of the Eighteenth Amendment by 

Congress in December, 1917, and the subsequent ratification by forty-six of the States. 

The Eighteenth Amendment states, in part, that "the manufacture, sale, transportation, importation, or exportation of intoxicating liquors for beverage purposes is hereby prohibited.' 99 The Volstead Act, passed over President Wilson's veto, October 28, 1919, defined intoxicating liquors as those containing one-half of one per cent or more of alcohol, and provided for the enforcement of the amendment. 

The Jones Law, approved by President Coolidge, March 

2, 1928, provided severe penalties for offenders repeatedly guilty of violations of the prohibition acts. 

The Pan-American Union. In 1881 James G. Blaine, then Secretary of State under President Garfield, invited

the Latin-American countries of Central and South America to a conference to discuss arbitration and other matters of interest to all American countries. Garfield's early death ended this plan for a time, but in 1889 

Congress authorized the President to invite the governments of the Latin-American countries to send delegates to Washington. Mr. Blaine, again Secretary of State under President Harrison, presided over this conference.

The conference discussed the preservation of peace and the establishment of means of better communication among the American countries. An International Bureau of American Republics was established for the collection of information and the cultivation of good will. 

Mr. Blaine and President McKinley were eager to bring about equality of privileges of trade among these countries. The Bureau has since become the Pan-American Union, which is now an important factor in the promotion of friendly relations among the American republics.

Politics and Elections. In the presidential election of 

1920 the Republican delegates nominated Warren G. 

Harding, a United States Senator from Ohio. They then nominated Calvin Coolidge, Governor of Massachusetts, for Vice President. The Democratic candidates were 

James M. Cox, Governor of Ohio, and Franklin D. 

Roosevelt, later Governor of New York.

The League of Nations was much discussed in the campaign. It was a new issue and both parties were divided upon it. Many Republicans favored the League and thirty-one prominent Republicans issued an address to the American people assuring them that the best way to bring about America's entrance into the League was to elect Senator Harding. 

Harding became President on March 4, 1921. As usual, corruption followed in the wake of war. One of

the members of Harding's Cabinet, A. B. Fall, Secretary of the Interior, was generally believed to have accepted a bribe of $100,000 for leasing to several oil men the Teapot 

Dome oil lands in Wyoming.

The Secretary of the Navy resigned under a cloud.

Attorney General Daugherty 

(do'er ti), who had played an important part in bringing about Harding's nomination, was involved in these scandals. Corruption also occurred in the Veterans' Bureau. 

His knowledge of these conditions may have hastened 

President Harding's death, which occurred in the summer of 1923; Vice President Coolidge then became President.

Presidential Election of 1924. 

In 1924 President Coolidge was nominated by the Republicans without opposition for re-election. Charles G. Dawes of Illinois was named for Vice 

President. The Democrats nominated John W. Davis, of 

West Virginia, for President and Charles P. Bryan, Governor of Nebraska (brother of 

William J. Bryan), for Vice

President. 

Coolidge continued his peaceful administration, avoiding sharp issues whenever he could do So. He sought to hold down the expenses of the Government and to promote business prosperity. During his administration two tax reductions were made and the national debt was reduced below eighteen billions. At the close of the World War the debt had been as high as twenty-eight billions.

Presidential Election of 1928. In its national convention in June, 1928, the Republican Party nominated for 

President Herbert C. Hoover, of California, and for Vice

President Senator Charles Curtis, of Kansas. The 

Democrats in their national convention nominated for

President Governor Alfred E. Smith, of New York, and for Vice President, Senator Joseph T. Robinson, of Arkansas.

The issues of the campaign were farm relief, immigration, protective tariff, prosperity, and prohibition. 

There was little difference between the candidates or their parties on any of these questions except prohibition. 

Hoover was recognized as a "dry" and Smith as a "wet." 

The election resulted in a decisive victory for Hoover.

The Republicans also carried both the Senate and the 

House of Representatives. 

The Great Depression. For several years America had enjoyed prosperity, but in 1929 a great industrial depression set in. Some banks could not collect their loans, nor get money for their notes, bonds, securities, and claims which they held. Many banks failed and their depositors lost their money. Men who were unable to pay taxes and rents lost their homes and farms. Factories and shops were closed, and workers were thrown out of employment. When their savings were gone, many had to be helped by neighbors, welfare societies, community funds, and appropriations from taxes. To make the Government income equal its outgo, Congress increased taxes and reduced Government expenses. 

Hard times continued, and such times usually cause the defeat of the party in power. In the election of 1930 the Democrats secured the control of the House of Representatives and almost wiped out the Republican majority in the Senate. President Hoover sought to stem the tide of the depression. He opposed the payment of the soldiers' bonus and held the country to the gold standard in money. He opposed all "inflation," or increase of the currency, by the issue of paper money. Congress created the Reconstruction Finance Corporation, provid-

ing large sums of money for loans to banks, railroads, and other corporations, to keep them going. A Home
